In the center, a blue oval medallion depicts a radiant sun shining down upon a **pelican in her piety** <note>A "pelican in her piety" is a traditional symbol of self-sacrifice, where a mother bird was believed to feed her young with her own blood. ->[Bidpaí]<-
<note>Bidpai (or Pilpay) was the legendary narrator of the *Panchatantra*, a collection of ancient Indian animal fables. In European esoteric manuscripts, his name was frequently invoked to suggest the presence of ancient, Eastern moral allegories and wisdom.</note>

# The Sayings of the Ancient Sages
## Exemplary Proverbs / illuminated with many beau-
### tiful examples and figures. Where-
### in almost all human nature / dealings / infidelity / cunning / speed / envy and hatred / are figured and displayed.

### In which also no less the secret envy and
hatred / as it occasionally occurs at Royal / and Princely courts / be-
tween Councilors and others / related to the Government / with false flat-
tery / and treachery of the malicious / against the faithful and pious
is carried out / may be seen and recognized as if in a mirror. For all
people not only fruitful and entertaining / but also
humorous / and serious to read / and to hear.

<summary>This is the title page of a German edition of the "Fables of Bidpai," a collection of ancient moral stories intended to illustrate human nature and the political intrigues of royal courts. It presents the work as both a moral mirror for behavior and an entertaining educational tool for all readers.</summary>

---

Stringed instruments make sweet tones, but above all, speaking kindly scatters enmity and is the beginning of friendship.
A person who is too ready to speak has folly closer to them than wisdom.
He who is master of his words is a treasury of wisdom.
A person should not quarrel over a thing that does not concern him.
Nothing is more pleasant than when one has a friend with whom he dares to speak and trust.
Rebuke your friend in secret, and praise him before the people.
No man has greater love than he who dies for his friend.
He who is a rogue to himself, how can he be good to another?
Three things please God: harmony, brotherly love, and a husband and wife who are of one mind. Three things God hates: a proud poor man, a rich liar, and an old fool.

--- Page 16 ---
**Solomon.** Do not seek honor, piety, or constancy in the house of the greedy.

**Sirach.** Do not forsake your friend, even if he occasionally speaks harshly.

**Solomon.** Where there is drunkenness, there is no secrecy; and where there are many words, there is folly.

**Seneca.** There is no difference between an angry man and a madman.

**Sirach.** If you find a wise and discreet man, hold him as your brother.

**Solomon.** The counsel of a faithful friend gladdens a man; even if he rebukes you, it is a sign of loyalty.

**Jerome.** He who praises you too much shows you a sword anointed with honey. <note>original: "Hieronymus"</note>

**Cicero.** One should not do evil for the sake of friends.

**Gregory.** Love is a demonstration of works. <note>original: "Gregorius"; referring to the idea that true love is proven through actions.</note>

**Cicero.** Nothing is as common among friends as the revelation of the heart.

**Solomon.** He who wishes to maintain friendship should uplift his friend; if he is honored more than you, do not let it vex you, for a friend is a comfort.

**Solomon.** He who gains a good wife has the best of gifts; but if she turns out badly, he has grasped a scorpion.

**Solomon.** Where an honorable wife is lacking, the husband sighs; and the house is like a courtyard with no wall around it. And as nothing is more poisonous than the head of a serpent, so nothing is angrier in wrath than an evil wife; and when she weeps from anger, depart from her.

**Seneca.** Good works and gifts create the very best friendship.

**Solomon.** When your friend asks you for something, do not say, "Come back tomorrow."

**Pamphilus.** He who gives quickly, gives twice; it is better to refuse a request than to delay it for a long time. <note>A reference to the Latin proverb *Bis dat qui cito dat*.</note>

**Cato.** Rebuke your friend for his sinful deeds, yet you should deliver the words as a plea. For harsh words wound both the rebuker and the listener. <note>original: "Catho"</note>

->The wise masters say that a man's favor and infidelity are recognized by his countenance.<-

**Sirach.** The look of a man's face and his limbs proclaim him.

**Solomon.** As a man sees his face in a well, so the wise see the hearts of fools.

**Aristotle and Cicero.** Friends should greet one another amiably, go to counsel with one another, and where they meet unexpectedly, neither should be startled by the other, nor feel the need to hide anything from him. And he is a wise man who knows how to maintain friends and friendship well.

<summary>A collection of moral aphorisms and proverbs attributed to biblical figures and classical philosophers regarding the nature of friendship, anger, marriage, and social conduct. It emphasizes loyalty, the transparency of the heart, and the importance of choosing virtuous companions.</summary>

## To the Reader.

So that pious and good people
Might keep themselves in good care,
And protect themselves from the wicked,
This book was composed many years ago
By the wise men of old,
Just as the title reports.
It had its beginning in India,
Through many most excellent men.
Afterward, it came into other lands,
Until it became known to the Germans.
In summary, this book contains
The lives and minds of all people.
How one should generally behave,
So that one may live well in peace.
What great cunning and swiftness <note>The original "gschwindigkeyt" implies both speed and clever dexterity or trickery.</note>
Occurs at the courts of Princes,
With complaining, lying, and deceiving.
Also what arises in wars,
With cunning and much treachery,
And what fortune dwells therein.
He who is too hasty in his affairs,
And does not consider the end.
And what this book reveals,
The Table of Contents <term>Register: a list or index of chapters</term> shall show you.
Therefore I say at this time,
This book is to be held in honor.
And also to be read with diligence,
For great benefit comes from it.
And also a pious Christian life,
If one would give it obedience.
May eternal God grant us that,
So we may emerge from all distress.

->A M E N.<-

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface to the "Book of Wisdom of the Ancient Sages," establishing the lineage of the text and its pedagogical purpose. It follows the title page and printer's marks.</meta>

# The Book of the Wisdom of the Ancient Sages, from the beginning of the world, from generation to generation. Concerning morals, character, loyalty and betrayal, the ways of the world, and cleverness; and how, in contrast, man should provide for himself with the fear of God and righteousness, set down in the form of parables of speaking animals and birds.

## Preface of this Book.

<margin>Three reasons for this book.</margin>
<margin>Parable of a blind man walking unknown paths.</margin>
This book was first composed by the ancient sages of the world's lineages in the Indian language <note>original: "Indischer sprach"; likely referring to the Sanskrit Panchatantra</note>, and thereafter transformed into the letters of the Persians, and after that the Arabs brought it into their language, and so it further came to the Hebrew tongue, subsequently set into Latin, and now finally translated into the German language. It is full of lovely words and precious speeches, through which the ancients wished to pour out their wisdom and to demonstrate it through the words of reason; and they composed this book to speak in parables of animals and birds. And they did this for three reasons. First, so that they might find a cause for their expression. Second, for the amusement of the reader; for therein the rational person reads and finds wisdom, while the simple, plain person enjoys the amusement of the illustrations. Third, because those who are learning are inclined to read examples <note>term: "beyspil" (examples/parables) - used here as moral teaching stories</note> and find them pleasant to hear, and moreover, they are memorable through the display of the figures; and even if they might not grasp them with final understanding in their youth, yet when they have grown in reason and wise things are considered by them, they will then reflect on what they have read in this book through the examples of the images. And that may fruitfully serve them toward high understanding and foresight, as well as toward good conduct, honor, and property. For a rational person may find many kinds of opinions herein regarding the world's practice of both evil and good—whoever reads this book thoughtfully and with an observation of the meaning of the figures and recognizes the cause, through which he may rightly protect himself from evil and practice good. For whoever reads this book must take heed of why it was made; for whoever does not know this and does not take heed of what the poets have considered herein, it happens to him just like a blind man who walks unknown paths. Therefore, one must take heed of what one reads or has read. For instance, when you have read an example, the answer to the speech you read follows only after the figure. But that requires attentiveness; even if an example is introduced in between, you must not forget what you read before; then you will have the understanding.

<summary>This preface outlines the cross-cultural transmission of the text from India to Germany and explains that its animal fables are intended to teach moral wisdom to both the learned and the simple through entertaining parables.</summary>
<keywords>wisdom, animal fables, Panchatantra, translation history, moral education, parables, Indian origins, Persian, Arabic, Hebrew, Latin</keywords>

<header>The Book of Wisdom</header>

# ¶ How treachery often strikes its own master / as happened to him / who wished to steal grain from his companion.

<margin>Of the sighted and the blind who fall into the pit.</margin>
Repentance and lamentation / should rightly flow over this man's own head / since he chose evil / and despised good works. For whoever recognizes good over evil in his reason / and yet allows his lust to overcome him / to him it happens / as to the sighted man / who wandered across a field with a blind man / and both fell into a deep pit / in which they both died. Now the sighted man has no advantage over the blind / for it was said / Justice has been done to him / since he saw the pit / he should rightly have warned both himself and the blind man of it.

<margin>Parable / a wise man should practice good.</margin>
For the wise man should be diligent / to do good works and to teach others the same / and what he teaches others / he should not forget himself / so that he does not become like / a well / which gives drink to all animals / and yet receives no help from any of them. But a wise man should instruct other people to practice good works / only after he has brought himself into practice.

<margin>No one should scold his neighbor.</margin>
<margin>Parable of two blind men.</margin>
For the wise say / that it is fitting for every man to seek three things. Wisdom / Wealth / and also Mercy / and that no one should scold his neighbor / for the same reason / that he has within himself / for he would be esteemed the same as the blind man / whose eyes were gouged out / and who scolded the one / who was born blind / because he had never seen from his youth up / and yet at that time they were both blind.

<margin>One's own profit is not to be sought to the harm of another.</margin>
It is also not fitting that a man seek his own profit to the harm of another / so that it does not happen to him / as to one of whom it is told.

<margin>Parable of two grain buyers.</margin>
There were two companions who had bought grain or wheat in common / and divided it into two heaps in a granary / where many other heaps of wheat also lay separated. One thought how he might deceive his companion regarding his heap / and asked another man to join him / and promised him half the grain / and went one day and covered his companion's heap / from which he intended to steal / with his cloak / so that when he came there at night / he would recognize the heaps by it. In the meantime, his companion came to the grain / and saw his companion's cloak lying on the grain / and said to himself. How faithful my companion is to me / that he covers my grain before his own with his own garment / so that no uncleanness comes into it. But surely that shall not be / and he took the cloak and laid it upon his companion's grain.

<margin>Treachery strikes its own master.</margin>
At night the schemer came with his other companion / whom he had commissioned / to the granary / and felt in the darkness / for where he would find his cloak lying on his companion's grain / and when he grasped it / he took half from it / and gave the other half to his fellow thief to carry away. Early the next morning the thief went with his companion / with whom he had bought the grain / to the granary / there he saw / that he had stolen his own grain from himself / and had given half of it away / and became mournful of his loss / etc.

# The Preface of the Ancient Sages.

¶ No one should follow their own evil will too closely, lest it happen to them as it did to the one who robbed himself.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts an indoor scene. On the left, a man in 16th-century attire (doublet, hose, feathered cap) is carrying a large sack through a wooden doorway. In the center and right, another figure sits on the floor amidst several large bulging sacks, handling a piece of fabric or a net. A window in the background shows a distant tower or building.</image-desc>

<margin>
Take heed that your hearts be not weighed down with the cares of this world. Luke 21. <note>original: "Luce. xxi."</note>
</margin>
You should note this: that no one should pursue their desires when it serves to harm their fellow human being, and especially regarding temporal goods <note>material wealth or worldly possessions</note>. For the wise people say: whoever’s mind is strictly inclined toward temporal goods, nothing remains for them in the end but a heartfelt sorrow when they must depart from them through death. And yet there are two things that are useful to a person: Wealth and the Fear of God. For whoever has the fear of God shall not despair in poverty; because through the fear of God—which is called the beginning of all wisdom <note>A reference to Psalm 111:10 or Proverbs 9:10</note>—a person is sometimes advanced to wealth, as happened to one who was poor. 

This man went to his friends in his poverty to complain of his lack and his needs and to ask them for help. And when help was denied to him by all of them, he came back to his house sorrowful, and lay awake at night in his bed out of dejection. He heard a thief in his house and thought: "What can this thief steal, since there is nothing in my house but a little flour, with which you were supposed to bake bread tomorrow morning for your hunger?" The thief thought: "You shall not leave this house without stealing something," and yet he found nothing else but the flour. And he took off his cap and poured the flour into it. On the cap was a tip <note>a pouch or corner of the headgear</note> in which he carried gold and silver that he had also acquired by stealing in times past. 

At that moment, the master of the house thought: "If the thief takes the flour, you must suffer hunger tomorrow," and he stood up and cried out at the thief with a loud voice, and hurried after the thief with his weapon. The thief fled and could not carry the cap with the flour, and had to let it fall. 
<margin>
He who wants too much gets too little.
</margin>
Thereupon the master of the house grabbed it and rescued his flour, and found silver and gold inside it for his necessity. As the following figure shows.

<meta>This page continues the introductory themes of the preface, transitioning from the general value of wisdom to practical advice on labor, social conduct, and the necessity of seeking reliable counsel.</meta>

# The Book of Wisdom

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two men in an interior setting with architectural details. On the right, a partially clothed man stands holding a wooden staff or rod aloft as if to strike. On the left, another man in a tunic and hose is bent forward in a submissive or defensive posture. The background features vaulted arches, a lattice window, and on the far right, a classical pedestal topped with a small figure.</image-desc>

But a wise man should not rely solely on the fact that his sustenance is provided for him without effort; rather, he should travel from kingdom to kingdom, so that he may acquire what is necessary for the upbringing of his children and the maintenance of his life. Although some people have come into wealth without labor, especially before they were even fit for work, the prudent wise man should not take this as an example, for it rarely turns out well. Instead, every wise man should obtain his sustenance with justice and in the fear of God according to his station <note>The "station" refers to one's social class or rank in the medieval hierarchy</note>, and take heed with prudent attention how he may protect himself from that 

<margin>Parable of the dove.</margin>
which may cause him harm, so that he is not esteemed as the dove; for no matter how often her young are taken or robbed from the nest, she nevertheless raises young again in that same nest.

<margin>Three things necessary for man.</margin>
The wise say that three things are necessary for man. The first is law and order. The second, sustenance and necessities. The third, righteous protection among oneself and one's fellow man. Conversely, for whoever does not adhere to these four things written hereafter, his being cannot be sufficient. The first: transgression of the commandments of the laws. The second: neglect of his own benefit. The third: believing everyone. The fourth: despising all wisdom. And it is fitting for every wise man to act and walk deliberately in his being, and you should not take counsel from the unknown, but only from the righteous and the faithful should you take counsel.

<margin>To beware of doubtful things.</margin>
But first, ask after the faithful; do not rely on doubtful things until you find the truth of the matter, so that it does not happen to you as it did to the one who walked a doubtful path: the longer he walked, the further he was from the right road; or like the one who finds something in his eye and does not stop rubbing it with his hands until it becomes worse. End of the Preface.

# The Ancient Sages. IIII.

<margin>
Anastres Tasri.
Berosias.
Of the high mountains in India and the awakening of the dead.
High mountains, wise masters.
The understanding of the revival of the dead.
</margin>

THE reigning lord of the kingdom of Edom <note>likely referring here to a legendary Persian or Eastern realm rather than the biblical Edom</note> was a powerful king, named in his time Anastres Tasri. He had with him a wise man learned in scripture named Berosias, who was a prince among physicians through his high art of medicine, and he received from the King high wages and an honorable position. At one time, a book was sent to the King, in which it was written, among other things, in the German tongue <note>original: "teütscher zungen"; the translator here likely refers to the language of the current edition rather than the historical setting</note>: that there are high mountains in India, upon which grow certain trees and herbs; whoever recognizes them and prepares them according to their form, from them is made a medicine with which the dead, by God's permission, shall be made living. The King desired to find the truth of this saying and commanded Berosias his physician to reach the meaning of this poem through his research; he would provide him with gold and silver for this purpose and give him recommendations to the Kings of India, to each of whom Berosias brought gifts from his lord, as is the custom for powerful lords to send to one another. Such gifts and letters were delivered by Berosias to each King, who offered themselves willingly in the mission and offered the wisest men from their courts and lands. In this undertaking, Berosias labored for twelve months and gathered together all kinds of trees and herbs with a mixture of apothecary things, and made from them an <term>electuarium</term> <note>a medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup</note> all according to the instruction of the mentioned book, and attempted to revive the dead. And when that could not be, Berosias considered the writings to be lies and became sorrowful, for it was difficult for him to return to his King unsuccessful. Because he feared being despised for this, he went to the highly learned men in India and opened these things to them. They said that they also found such indications in their books and had searched further for it, until they found the interpretation in a book by the Ancient Sages from the beginning of the world in this meaning: Namely, that the high mountains signify the wise masters. The trees and herbs are the art and high understanding that grow out of those same masters. The electuary that is prepared therefrom are the books of wisdom and art. The dead who are revived through medicine are the foolish, unwise people who wear out their lives without any wisdom or enlightened reason; these are revived from the death of reason and thereafter with the medicine of wisdom, when they read and learn from those same books, and retain and fulfill what stands therein, and are made living. And when Berosias heard this, he desired to have these books, and found them in the Indian tongue, and brought them into the language of the Persians. And he came again to his lord, King Anastres Tasri. And when the King heard this, he became eager to have the books and the understanding, and practiced with all his might to learn the art of wisdom, and exalted it within himself, and set it before all other pastimes and riches (in which Kings are accustomed to live).

TRuly, under high penalty, King Anastres commanded that high schools of learning be established in his kingdom, and that they be paid for from his chamber, and that the art be spread and the books increased. Among these, this little book was also in Indian writing...

<sig>B iiij</sig>
<meta>catchword: writing</meta>

<summary>This page recounts the legend of King Anastres Tasri and his physician Berosias, who discovers that the "medicine to revive the dead" is actually a metaphor for the transformative power of wisdom and philosophy found in Indian books. The King subsequently establishes schools to spread this knowledge, marking the legendary origin of the text.</summary>
<keywords>Berosias, India, Persia, allegory of wisdom, medicine of the soul, King Anastres Tasri, transmission of knowledge</keywords>

The First Chapter speaks of Berosia <note>likely referring to Berosus or a legendary sage figure</note> / and is about the fear and justice of God.
¶ The Second Chapter speaks of the Lion and the Ox / and speaks of deceitful seduction.
¶ The Third speaks of the investigation of envious matters / and is about the end of him / who rejoices in another’s misfortune.
¶ The Fourth speaks of the dove and of faithful companionship.
¶ The Fifth speaks of the raven / and also of the Eagle / and is about him / who trusts his reconciled enemy / and what finally befalls him because of it.
¶ The Sixth speaks of the Monkey / and the tortoise / and is about him who has a faithful friend / and does not know how to keep him.
¶ The Seventh speaks of a Hermit / and is about him who is hasty in his affairs / and does not consider the end.
¶ The Eighth speaks of the mongoose <note>original: "maußhund", literally 'mouse-dog', a term often used for the ichneumon or mongoose in fables</note> / and the mouse / and is about the enemy who seeks peace in times of need with his other enemies.
¶ The Ninth speaks of a King / and of a bird / and is about companions who carry secret envy / and how one should guard oneself against the other.
¶ The Tenth speaks of King Sedra / and is about him / who can restrain his anger / and overcome vice.
¶ The Eleventh is about a Hunter / and a Lioness / and speaks of him who desists from evil through the evil that happens to him.
¶ The Twelfth speaks of a hermit and the pilgrim / and speaks of him who abandons his own work / and uses that which is not proper for him / nor inherited by him.
¶ The Thirteenth speaks of the Lion and the fox / and is about the love of Kings / which they should have according to justice.
¶ The Fourteenth speaks of the goldsmith / the Monkey / the Adder and the snakes / and speaks of mercy / and that ingratitude should not be shown.
¶ The Fifteenth is about the King’s Son and his companions / and speaks of Divine Providence / against which no one may stand.
¶ The Sixteenth is about the birds / and is about those / who deceive themselves among each other.
¶ The Seventeenth is about the dove and the fox / and speaks of him / who can give counsel to another / and not to himself.

# V.
# Of the Ancient Sages

<margin>
Praise for the elders who send the young to school in good time.
</margin>
<margin>
Parable of the merchant.
</margin>
<margin>
Parable of the spoon.
</margin>
<margin>
Parable of the censer.
</margin>
<margin>
Parable of the transplanted hair.
</margin>

Desire and art, says Berosias <note>likely a variation of Borzuya, the legendary Persian physician</note>, a chief of the sages of the kingdom of Edom, who translated this book into the tongue of the Persians: "Not the least thing was granted to me by my father and mother in the Kingdom of India above all my other siblings, in that they placed me in school in the seventh year of my age to learn the books of medicine. There I praised my father and her who bore me; and when I had prepared my mind with the art of medicine, so that I might come to the aid of the sick with my art from the books by the grace of the Almighty, I then considered that there are four things toward which the human spirit should incline: noble customs, sustenance, a good reputation, and the merit of the future life. And I chose for myself the fourth, and thought that it would not be enough to give my medicine to the poor for their bodies for free, but that I must also have and use the medicine from the books of wisdom, so that I might not only make the sick healthy, but also quicken the dead <note>metaphorical for spiritual awakening</note>, and use this for the praise of the Eternal Name. Thus I would become like the merchant who sells a precious stone for a penny which might have been worth much. And I therefore advised my spirit to withdraw from the pleasures of this world, for the end of its joy is adversity. I did this so that I would not become like the spoon; for while it is new, the cook uses it, but as soon as it grows old, he throws it into the fire. And I spoke to my spirit: do not let yourself be led by company where you might gather things that cause you to become like the censer, which is ignited by many coals with incense from which the bystanders receive a good scent, while nothing remains for it but that which is burned away. I also warned my spirit that it should not let itself be greatly deceived by worldly pride, so that it would not become like transplanted hair <note>likely referring to a wig or hairpiece</note>, which is so valued upon the head of its wearer, but when it falls to the earth, he kicks it here and there with his feet."

---

->¶ No one should let their labor grieve them, lest it happen to them<-
->as it did to him who sold all he had bought together,<-
->and lost a good half of it thereby.<-

---

<margin>
Parable of the merchant of silk garments.
</margin>
<margin>
Concern for the eternal life is a priority for a wise man.
</margin>

"Do not be weary with your labor," I said to my spirit, "and do not let adversity grieve you for the sake of the future life, lest you lose the earned reward thereby. And let it not happen to you as it did to a merchant who had a shop full of golden and silken cloths and thought: 'If you sell all this by the yard, it will be tedious for you,' and so he sold it all in a lump sum. And when he calculated it, he had given it away for half its value to his own loss. Therefore, I undertook the medicine that quickens the dead, and chose for myself these books, wherein I find the knowledge of the distinction to avoid injustice and to accomplish good; for I found in the books of medicine <note>original: "artzney"</note> nothing of the laws or statutes to purify the soul. Thus I undertook the books wherein I might recognize how to earn the future life, so that the human spirit may be quickened from the death of ignorance."

# The Book of Wisdom

But when I recognized the books of wisdom from the laws of all the races of the world, and questioned the masters of all the tongues of the nations to instruct me—by which laws I might best distinguish truth from untruth, and justice from injustice, in order to walk therein and remain with simplicity of my heart and with the practice of common works—I found among them all nothing but that <margin>Every nation praises its own law.</margin> each nation praised its own law and scolded the others. I noted thereby that each one walks in his law according to the desire and pleasure of his mind, rather than according to the foundation of justice; for I found among them all nothing that could show me true justice. And therefore I would believe none of them, for everything happened in such a way that it would be a concern <margin>Concerning the night-thieves.</margin> to one who was easy to believe. 

For once upon a time, a thief went with several of his companions in the night to a rich man's house to steal. And when they had now come upon the roof of the man's house, they were heard by the master of the house, who lay in bed by his wife. He immediately noticed that they had come there for the sake of stealing, for they had previously committed many thefts and murders in the city. And so he spoke to his wife: "I hear the murderers on our roof; they want to steal what is ours, and if we resist them, they will perhaps murder us. Therefore, follow my advice and ask me in a loud voice how I gained my wealth; and if I refuse to tell you, do not stop <margin>Conversation of the man and wife.</margin> your questioning." 

The woman did according to her husband's command. The man gave her an answer in an audible voice and spoke: "Let it suffice you that I have placed you in great goods and wealth. Eat and drink, and live in joy, and do not ask me about things that are not to be told to you. Someone might hear it, and it would result in great harm to you and me." 

The woman spoke: "I pray and exhort you by the good faith which you have always found in me, do not hide the cause of our wealth from me; for there is no one who can <margin>Micah 7.</margin> <note>A reference to Micah 7:5: "keep the doors of thy mouth from her that lieth in thy bosom."</note> hear us now, so you are certain of my silence." 

The man answered the woman and spoke: "Although the words of the wise give warning to guard secret things from her who sleeps in your bosom, yet the faithfulness of your love compels me not to keep this from you." And he spoke: "Know that I have gained all my wealth through stealing." 

The woman answered: "How can that be? You are esteemed as pious by all people." 

The man answered: "Know that I have done such things with reflection and wisdom, secretly and cautiously, so that no one could become aware of it." 

Then the woman spoke: "How was that?" 

The man answered: "I went by night in the full moonlight and climbed upon the roofs of the houses, and observed the roof-windows through the roofs through which the moon shone. And I spoke this incantation <margin>Sulem Sulem.</margin> <note>A magical formula or "power word" used in the story to deceive the thieves.</note> seven times: *Sulem, sulem*, and then I grasped the beam of the moon and let myself down by it through the house to the earth without any injury. And when I thus came to the floor of the house, I spoke the words of the incantation again; then the place of the treasure was shown to me through the shine of the moon, and all locks also sprang open. And when I had taken according to my desire, I went back to the shine of the moon and performed my incantation as before; thus the shine gave me a good way to go back out through the roof-window."

<summary>The narrator searches for universal justice among world laws but finds only bias, leading into a parable where a clever homeowner tricks eavesdropping thieves by pretending his wealth was stolen via a magical moonbeam incantation.</summary>

# The Ancient Sages. VI.

¶ No one should be quick to believe every speech, so that it does not happen to him as it did to the thief who, by the light of the moon, fell through the attic window, as this figure shows.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a narrative scene. On the tiled roof of a building, three thieves are positioned; one is looking through an opening, while another is shown falling head-first through a skylight. Inside the building, the homeowner stands over the fallen intruder, poised to strike him with a large wooden club. To the left, a woman (the homeowner's wife) lies in a bed, observing the scene. The right side of the image depicts a narrow street with multi-story buildings and a figure walking in the distance.</image-desc>

<margin>
Believing too quickly causes harm.
</margin>

So the three thieves were on the roof when they heard this "art" <note>The "art" refers to the fake magical incantation the homeowner invented to trick them.</note> and spoke together: "Now we have found that which is better for us than all treasures of silver or gold; for now we have found the art by which we may acquire wealth without any worry." And they waited so long until it seemed to them that the master of the house and his spouse <note>original: "gemahel"</note> were asleep again. Then the eldest among the thieves stood up and went to an attic window, and lowered himself onto the moonbeam—believing he would enter the house that way—and spoke the words of the incantation, and with them embraced the light of the moon. And he dropped down and fell on his face upon the floor of the house. Immediately the man in the house stood up and ran over him with a large club <note>original: "bengel"</note> and beat his skin <note>The phrase "erbört im sein haut" suggests a thorough thrashing or "tanning his hide."</note> as was only right, and said: "Who is here?" The thief answered and said: "It is one who believed too quickly and was thereby deceived; for that which I heard, I believed before I had rightly experienced it, and for that reason, I am quite worthy of your blows."

<meta>This page continues the philosophical and moral reflections found in the "Book of Wisdom," transitioning from a personal crisis of faith to a cautionary parable about stubbornness and distraction.</meta>

# The Book of Wisdom

¶ Let no one engage in long, useless arguments.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular border. On the left, stone pillars support a vaulted interior. In the center, an elegantly dressed man wearing a feathered hat and tunic speaks with a woman in a gown and a tall, patterned headdress. In the background to the right, another man is depicted falling head-first into a circular stone well.</image-desc>

To believe that I did not know the truth, and that I might easily go astray because of it, I became fearful. I resolved to remain within the law of my ancestors <note>original: "gesatz meiner fürfaren"</note>. Yet I thought: if I were to go astray in this way—since my father, mother, and all my ancestors were sorcerers—would I not still be blamed? A man is not permitted to be considered better simply because he follows the long-standing customs of his forefathers. I thought of a man who was immoderate in eating and drinking, and when he

<margin>It is no excuse to say: "This is how my parents lived."</margin>
was rebuked for it, he answered: "This is how my father, mother, and all my ancestors lived." But I understood that this was no excuse for him to live that way himself. Thus, I considered that I had no reason to live by the law of my ancestors [merely for tradition's sake]; instead, I took up the laws of the true truth, through which men earn eternal life. I realized then that the end of my life was approaching and that many of my days were already spent. I said to myself: "You do not know how swiftly the end of your life comes," and I realized that I had only just begun the works which I believed should count toward the number of good deeds. But by the wandering of my searching, I was hindered; and perhaps it would happen to me as it did to one

<margin>Concerning the adulterous woman.</margin>
of whom it is told: There was a woman who had a lawful husband and also a lover <note>original: "bůlen," a term for a paramour or illicit lover</note>. She had a secret exit from her courtyard by a draw-well <note>original: "galgbrunnen," a well with a counterpoised sweep or "gallows" structure</note>, for use in case the husband should come home unannounced, so that the lover might escape through it. One time, she stood with her lover and said, "Run quickly, there is an exit by the well!" He said, "I find none; it is filled in." She said, "Fool! I did not tell you about the well itself, only that you should find the exit *by* the well." He replied, "You should not have named the well to me if it was no longer there." She said, "Be gone and do not make so many words, or else time will run out for you!" But the fool continued to argue with her.

### ¶ How a merchant wasted a day's work of his business with a master who was supposed to polish precious stones for him.

### ¶ No one should be too greedy, otherwise it will happen to him as it happened to the dog, etc.

<margin>Worldly life.</margin>
I resolved for myself to leave the world, since I recognized its vain practices, and to strengthen myself in a proven, secluded life. For I noticed that a secluded spiritual life protects a person as a father protects a son. For I see that spiritual, secluded people are magnified—those who humble themselves therein and let themselves be satisfied with the food that is set before them. They separate themselves from the pleasure of this world and are content with that which God deems for them and is assigned to them by their superiors. They are free from the misery 
<margin>Spiritual life.</margin>
of this world, and of a fearless, peaceful mind. And I intended to become one of the aforementioned people, and yet I began to wonder within myself whether I might not be able to sustain this in the long run, and whether my desire might force me back toward the food to which I had been accustomed since my youth. And I considered: should I leave my current state, in which I was honorably raised, and then

# The Wisdom of the Ancients VIII

<margin>All joy with sorrow.</margin> Truly, I first became fearful when considering that I should begin the departed life <note>The "departed life" refers to a life of religious or philosophical seclusion, "departing" from worldly vanities.</note> and persevere in it; I thought to remain in my current station, for it was revealed to me that there is no joy, desire, or being in this world that will not, in its time, be mixed with pain, sadness, or adversity.

<margin>Parable of the salt water.</margin> Like salt water: the more one drinks of it, the more one desires to drink, and thirst grows more and more.

<margin>Parable of the hard bone.</margin> Or like a hard bone in which there is still some taste and sweetness of the meat; if a dog finds it, he gnaws at it for the taste without any fruit of satisfying his hunger, until the gums of his teeth become bloody and sore from it, and yet he does not stop; and the more he gnaws, the worse he treats his teeth.

<margin>Parable of the old hawk.</margin> Or it is like an old hawk that finds a small piece of meat, and when he wishes to feed himself with it, the other greedy birds fly to him, and he must defend himself from them with great distress while he carries the meat in his claws, and yet he cannot enjoy it because of them. At last, wishing to have peace, he leaves the meat and remains hungry as before.

<margin>Parable of the vessel with honey on top.</margin> Or it is like a vessel that is covered with honey in its upper part, but underneath is hidden poison; and whoever tastes the sweetness of the honey finds much bitterness therein.

<margin>Parable of the sleeper.</margin> Or it is like a sleeper who dreams something and has a joy in his mind; when he awakes, his joy ends with the sleep.

<margin>Parable of the night lightning.</margin> Or like a flash of lightning at night, which gives a person a brief moment of brightness, and afterward leaves the person in darkness as before.

<margin>Parable of the silkworm.</margin> Or like a silkworm, which spins its thread very long out of itself, and thereby brings itself to nothing.

### ¶ An Example of a Right Life.

Such I judged my mind to be, that it was changeably inclined toward living, and I said: "It is not fitting that I change from one state to another, and then from that one into this, and not remain in the same being and mind." I feared I might now be judged like the judge who lived in ancient times, of whom it is said: a man came to him and told him his case, and he gave judgment for him. Soon the other party came and also told him his case, and he also gave judgment according to their pleasure. And then I thought of what adversity might befall me in the departed life.

<margin>Parable of the judge.</margin> Then I feared to enter into it, and found in my mind that such a thing was difficult for it, and that it was better for it to live in the pleasure of this world. And I spoke rebukingly to my heart: "Do you not know that the pleasure of this world is so full of troublesome adversity, and that its pleasure leads to eternal torment?" And I said to it: "Remember that the bitterness of wormwood <note>Wormwood: a proverbially bitter herb used here to represent temporary hardship leading to spiritual health.</note> which has a healthy sweetness behind it is better than the sweetness which has a long bitterness behind it."

<margin>Parable of the wormwood.</margin> If a person were granted to live a hundred years, yet in nothing but great labor, trouble, sickness, and sadness, poverty, and all adversity—and afterward, he should have all the pleasure of his body and riches in this time and live without any care—would those hundred years not be to him as a single day? Thus I exhorted my mind and said: "Why should I scorn the departed, or a right...

# The Book of Wisdom

<margin>Man goes from one misery into another.</margin>
life; therefore, I might for a short time endure adversity, and thereafter come to eternal joy and peace. And I spoke to my mind: Do you not see that this world is full of misery, poverty, labor, sickness, and adversity? Do you not know that man, from the time of his conception in the mother’s womb—whether he be king or shepherd—goes from one misery into another?

### ¶ Description of Human Misery.

Actually, you find in the books of the natural masters of <term>medicine</term><note>original: "artzney"</note> how the first form of the creation of man in the mother’s womb, called <term>Embryo</term><note>original: "Embrio"</note>, comes from the seed of a man with the mixing of the woman’s contribution. And from this becomes a sharp little bundle that mixes itself a little into a thick matter. Thereafter, the fluidity gains the upper hand with mixed blood and then becomes like water. After that, it is divided into limbs in a specific number of days; for if it is a boy, his face is positioned toward the loins of his mother, and in sixty days the division of his limbs is completed and admitted into the secret of his birth. And his eyes and face are bent down toward his knees, and he cannot move any of his limbs. Instead, he is bent like a card <note>likely meaning "curled" or "folded"</note> to suck from the navel of his mother, from which he receives his nourishment. And he remains in such labor until the time of his birth. With what distress he is moved to leave his place at the time of birth, and with what anxiety he is born, can hardly be described. And what he feels when he is touched [for the first time] coming from the mother’s womb is no less than if a grown man’s skin were being peeled off. Thereafter, he remains in much labor and pain: first with hunger when he must drink, then with thirst when he must eat, with aches that he cannot speak of; then he is carried when he would gladly be free. Afterward, when he overcomes the time of the cradle, he must then live under the daily punishment of the rod and endure many kinds of sicknesses and accidents. When he then reaches the days of manly youth, he first falls into conscious temptation: here with the desire for riches, there with the fire of love, elsewhere burdened with a wedded wife and the worry of arriving children, and how he may nourish them. Then follow the accidents of the four <term>Elements</term>, so that his <term>Complexion</term><note>The "humors" or physical temperament believed to be governed by the four elements (earth, air, fire, water).</note> is inclined toward every accident, from which sicknesses will arise for him. Then he is tormented with waking, with dreamy sleep, cold, heat, snow, rain, frost, and with many other misfortunes. After that comes his old age—yes, if he lives to see it—then two servants are hired for him. One is called Pain, the other Sickness, who wait upon him most closely. And even if all that were not so, and he could protect himself from all adversity, and only thought of <term>Death</term><note>original: "tod"</note>, which he must suffer with such hard grimness—which separates him from goods, from honors, from his beautiful wife, children, father and mother, and companions, and from all his great pleasure of this world. And he must travel to where he does not know how he will be received, or where. Thus, he should rightly consider and provide for himself according to his ability, to leave all the joy of this world's time which may bring worry for the future life, and especially the practices of the present world, which has placed itself so entirely in perversion, in order to fulfill misdeeds and to avoid unrighteousness.

<summary>This page provides a grim, late-medieval reflection on the inherent suffering of human life, tracing misery from embryonic development and the trauma of birth through the burdens of adulthood and the inevitability of death.</summary>

# IX.

### ¶ Narrative on the vanity of this world.

Now we see that the time of this present age has turned itself so completely from good to evil. For the words of justice are entirely extinguished, and injustice presses forward; justice is weakened, and injustice is strengthened. The art of wisdom remains hidden, while folly and its customs are revealed. Love for one's neighbor is concealed; envy and hatred are made known. Governance is taken from the righteous and given to the wicked. Falsehood is awake, and truth sleeps. The tree of lies bears fruit, and the tree of truth is withered. The paths of malice shine, but the paths of justice are dark. The greed of avarice is opened wide to swallow whatever it finds, but good will is entirely abandoned. The wicked are exalted even unto heaven, and the good are pressed down into the depths. The noble spirit is trampled under the feet of the ignoble. The Prince turns himself from the throne of mercy to the seat of wrath, and this whole world is unjustly perverted, saying: I have hidden the good things, and the evil things I have revealed.

### ¶ On the contemplation of the five senses.

<margin>To preserve the soul.</margin>
Reflectively, I then undertook an examination of my reason and found that man, in his creation, is better than all other creatures. And yet he does not wish to preserve himself for grace, but goes from one evil into another; and yet every man who has even a little wisdom within him knows that he might well consider how to avoid this. But I find therein something that causes me wonder: that a small cause of this hindrance is a brief pleasure and joy which man finds in this world, solely through seeing, hearing, smelling, tasting, touching, and feeling. And it is possible that man has little remaining of this world but a short while, and yet through this he forgets to preserve his soul.

### ¶ Description of this present life, in the likeness of a well, four animals, and the honeycomb.

Such a man is rightly likened to a man who fled from a lion that hunted him, and he came to a deep well and lowered himself into it, and held himself with his hands by two small twigs that grew at the edge of the well-sweep <note>original: "galgbrunnen" - a shadoof or sweep-well, often used as a metaphor for the precariousness of life</note>, and he set his feet upon a rolling stone. And he saw before him four animals with lowered heads, desiring to swallow him; and when he turned his sight from them toward the valley, he saw a gruesome dragon with an open mouth at the bottom of the well, ready to receive him in its gullet <note>original: "giel" - throat or maw</note>. And he perceived that by the two twigs to which he clung, there were a black and a white mouse, gnawing away at them according to their ability. This man, while he stood in such great anxieties and knew not when his end was near, saw beside him

# The Book of Wisdom

<margin>Understanding of this parable.</margin>

...beside him between two stones a little honeycomb, from which he licks with his tongue; and through the sensation of that small sweetness, he forgot to look out for himself or how he might be delivered from his anguish, until he fell and perished. ¶ I compare the well to this world. The four beasts are the <term>four elements</term> <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water, believed to compose the human body</note>, by which all men are summoned to death. The two mice are the life of man: the white mouse is the day, the black mouse is the night, which are constantly gnawing away at the life of man. By the dragon, I mean the grave of man, which waits for him at every hour. The little honeycomb is the transitory pleasure of this world, through which many a man sinks himself into eternal unrest.

### ¶ On the Unification of the Right Mind.

To rightly complete my work and to improve it according to my ability, I thought to turn myself toward a hermitage and to dedicate myself to Divine service, so that I might earn for myself a lasting peace in the future world, in which the inhabitants do not die, nor does any unpleasant accident befall them. And I disciplined my mind to guard itself against all unjust things with penitent remorse for that which I had accomplished in past times; and so I remained in such solitude of my life. And when I returned home from India, I brought this book in the <term>writing of the Persians</term> <note>original: "gschrifft der Persien"</note>, into which I had translated it from the Indian tongue, beginning thus:

<header>The Wisdom of the Ancients, Part X.</header>

# The Second Chapter / Tells of the Lion and the Ox / of deception / and unfaithfulness.

Give me, spoke Disles <note>The King of India, often identified as Dabschelim in other versions of these fables</note>, the King of India, to Sendebar <note>The philosopher/sage, also known as Bidpai</note> his wise master, an example of two good friends, between whom a hater intervenes with lies, until he brings them to the shedding of their blood. Then Sendebar answered the King: Lord, I know that when faithful companions are wounded by one another through lies, their hatred becomes bold, and their lives against each other are utterly destroyed; and it happens to them as it did to the lion and the ox. The king spoke: How did that happen? Sendebar answered and spoke: It is said there was in a province of India a very rich merchant who had three sons. They took no care to preserve the wealth of their father, but rather to consume it extravagantly. Their father called them and spoke:

<margin>Three things to seek in this world.</margin>
"Dear sons, there are three things that a person should seek, and yet should only find them through four things. The three he should seek are: nourishment for his body, an honorable standing among people, and knowing how to protect himself. But the four by which he shall honorably obtain these three things are: first, that he gains his goods honorably and justly. The second, that he knows how to increase them. The third, that he knows how to use what is necessary with honor. The fourth, that he thereby knows how to obtain the eternal reward of the future world. And whichever of these one neglects, the best end does not appear for his goods.

<margin>Of ointment for the eyes.</margin>
For whoever neither knows how nor wishes to increase his goods, and uses from them at all times, it happens to him as to one who had an ointment for his eyes: however little one is accustomed to take from it, in the end, nothing is left there. But he who increases what he possesses with his foresight, yet does not use it for his necessity, he is rightly esteemed poorly, for his wealth is his heart <note>Meaning his heart is buried in his gold rather than in living</note>. And at last, it happens to his treasure as to a jug that is full of must <note>unfermented grape juice</note> and tightly plugged; if it has no air, it bursts all at once, and the must is lost to no use." And when the sons heard this, they set their minds to follow it. And when the last son went about his trade in a city, into which he drove two oxen to sell there—one ox was named Genesba and the other was named Teneba—he came upon a narrow path on the road that was very deep and foul, so that Genesba sank into it. He had to pull him out again with great labor, and left him behind him, powerless, in the house of one of his friends. And the ox became so sick that the host of the house chased him from him into the field. This Genesba went through the forest and found a great wide space with very fruitful pasture and moisture, and thought to himself: "Here you are departed from all hardship and labor in good pasture, where you may restore yourself. Here you will establish your being and let yourself be satisfied with this, so that it does not happen to you as to one who had gone into a forest for wood for his needs. And whatever wood he found seemed useless to him, and he went on and on until he encountered a pack of wolves. He took fright at them, and to escape them, he fled to a bridge. And as they followed him, he saw that the bridge was broken and thought: 'You cannot cross the bridge, nor can you swim; if you wait for the wolves, you will be eaten; nothing is better, you...'"

So after a few days, Senesba <note>The ox, known as Schanzabeh in other versions</note> had regained his strength from the good pasture / and began according to his nature to bellow with a strong voice. Now near this pasture was a dwelling / where a Lion lived / who was a Lord and ruler of all the animals of the land / and with him were many animals / such as wolves / bears / foxes / and the like at his court. This Lion was of a great spirit, headstrong, and secretive in the undertaking of his counsel. At one time he heard the voice of Senesba the Ox / and was terrified by it / for in all his days / he had not heard the like of that voice / nor seen such an animal / and he kept the fear in his heart / and was ashamed to tell it to anyone / and thus avoided walking where he usually did. Now there were with him among his courtiers two animals / brothers and companions / one named Kellila / the other Dimna <note>The two jackals who serve as the primary narrators/manipulators in these fables</note> / and Dimna said to his brother Kellila / have you not noticed / that our lord the Lion does not go out or travel according to his custom / or take the recreation / which he has used until now? Kellila answered him. Brother, what moves you to investigate / that which does not concern us / we have our station / which is assigned to us / and in it there is no lack.

# The Wisdom of the Ancients XI.

<margin>One should not inquire into the secrets of lords.</margin>
defects. And it does not befit us, according to our station, to inquire after our lord's secrets, unless we were moved to do so by his own indication. For know: whoever wishes to learn that which is not permitted to him, and is not part of his business, it may happen to him as it did to the monkey. Dimna said, "How did it happen to him?" Kalila answered, "It is said of a monkey that he saw a carpenter standing over a thick tree trunk, wanting to split it open; and as often as he struck the tree open with the axe, he pushed a wedge <note>original: 'wöcken' - a wooden wedge used to keep a split open</note> into it, and then drew the axe out to strike again. It happened that the carpenter went from his work to eat; the monkey was nimble there and wanted to perform the carpenter's work as well. He placed himself over the tree, and because of the shortness of his legs, his genitals <note>original: 'geschirr' - a euphemism for genitals or tackle</note> hung into the split of the tree. He pulled the axe out of the tree and forgot to strike the wedge in first, and was clamped within the tree so that he was trapped there. From his crying out the carpenter came, and gave him many blows as his punishment."

<note>The woodcut depicts the monkey trapped in the log, illustrating the physical consequence of his curiosity, with a detailed 15th-century German town in the background.</note>

### ¶ How one should direct oneself to a higher state, if he is worthy of it.

<margin>A parable of the lion who catches a hare.</margin>
**I**dleness <note>original: 'Tragkeyt' - laziness or lack of ambition</note> now possesses many a man, said Dimna, for I have well understood your words and your example. Therefore I know that it is not an evil thing to inquire after that by which one may make his lord or friend joyful and his enemy sorrowful, and raise himself in the eyes of his lord. For those are justly esteemed as fools who allow themselves to be satisfied with small things when they are capable of achieving greater ones. They are like a dog that takes joy in a dry bone that he finds. For it does not befit a well-born man to let himself be satisfied with small things; rather, he should set his mind high until he comes to the place of which he is worthy. Like the lion: when he has caught a hare and sees something greater and better, he leaves the hare and desires the better thing. You see that the dog wags his tail only until his master gives him bread.

<meta>catchword: The elephant</meta>

<summary>Kalila warns Dimna against meddling in the King's business using the fable of the monkey and the carpenter, but Dimna counters that a noble spirit must always strive for higher status rather than being satisfied with small rewards.</summary>

# The Book of Wisdom

the wise say that a highly sensible man is only to be found in two places: in the service of his natural lord, or in the service of God; in solitude like the elephant, which through its pride and dignity is only found in the courts of kings or in the loneliness of the forests. <note>The elephant was often used in medieval fables as a symbol of solitary dignity and royal stature.</note> Kalila answered: "Go forth, and may the Lord protect you in your undertaking." So Dimna went to the King, the Lion, and greeted him. The Lion asked those who stood around him who this might be; they said: "Lord, he is one of your court servants, the son of such-and-such a lineage, etc." The Lion, the King, answered: "Yes, I knew his father well," and called to him and asked: "Where is your dwelling?" Dimna answered: "Lord, for many years now I have never failed to serve at the door of your hall, thinking that perhaps the hour will come when I may be of some use to the King. Although I do not claim high birth or great intellect, I am also not among the least, so that perhaps something useful might be accomplished through me. For just as base and worthless as the chaff is—which is swung out of the hemp and thrown down upon the earth—it is sometimes used for a purpose, such as when a man uses it to pick his teeth. <note>original: "aglen" (awn/chaff). Dimna uses a humble metaphor to argue that even the lowliest subject can be useful to a monarch.</note> For those animals who are most knowledgeable and aware of the world's course and dealings are rightly honored by the King above others."

# XIII.
## Of the Sages of Old.

It was also commanded to the King’s most secret counselors and servants not to withhold the King’s secrets or the affairs of the realm from him, but to act according to his advice. Thereupon, he exerted himself to be near the King, and not to depart from him, and to speak to him so that he might earn the King’s favor and demonstrate his wisdom to him.
<margin>Hidden wisdom is not praised, like seed in the earth.</margin>
And he spoke: "As long as wisdom lies hidden and unpracticed within a man, it is like seed in the earth, which is not praised until it brings forth its fruit. For it befits a king to take heed of this, and to appoint each person according to his worth. For it is said:
<margin>Two things that befit no one.</margin>
that there are two things that befit no one, not even the King. For he is accounted a fool who puts his shoes on his hands and his gloves on his feet. Likewise, it is when the wise man is placed in the stead of the fool, and the fool in the stead of the wise man." And he spoke: "Lord King, if you wish to prosper in the affairs of your realm and govern peacefully, then take heed of your servants—how each may be most useful to you, and how each performs his service. For the salvation of your realm does not consist in a great number of servants,
<margin>A prince should not have many, but useful servants.</margin>
but in useful servants, even if there are not many of them. For if one were to carry a large, useless stone on his shoulder all day, he could not receive as much strength or benefit from it as another receives from a very small precious stone <note>original: "Edlen steyn"</note> which he wears on his finger. However, servants of deceit and cunning, who must be feared, are of no use to the King. It also does not befit the King to despise noble deeds and a rational mind in anyone, even if he finds them in an undistinguished man. For very often one seeks the small in many ways, and that which is despised becomes useful. Just as the guts of a dead animal are cast away, yet from them strings are made with which the King is entertained with music, or a bowstring is made, which the King may use for sport or in earnest." And here Dimna did not wish it to be thought that the King should love them [only] because he had said he knew his father well; rather, he prided himself on his wisdom, as he heard that it pleased the King well, so that he might win his love for that reason and be honored above others of higher birth. And he spoke: "The King should love no one for the sake of their father and mother. He should also hate no one because of their father or mother. Rather, he should take heed of the reason <note>original: "vnunfft" - likely a typo for "vernunfft" (reason/intellect)</note> and skill of his servants, and what he needs from each, and then place each in the station of his worth, whereby he may protect his person.
<margin>Two kinds of human races.</margin>
For the mouse is closest of all to the King, as she dwells by night with him in his chamber, sometimes under his bed, and yet she is of no use or delight to him, but rather a notable disturbance. But the hawk is raised far from the King, and through his noble birth and manners, he becomes so dear to the King that he takes him upon his own hand and strokes him. Therefore, the King should take heed of who is useful or useless to serve him, and recognize them by their conduct and practice. For there are two kinds of human races: those who live only for their pleasure and wantonness, with all evil habits. From such, the King should distance himself in time. For one who has ignorantly walked over poisonous serpents without them poisoning him would act unwisely if he [went] back...

<margin>Sorrow withers the bone.</margin>
"You are troubled in your spirit, my Lord King," said Dimna, "and fearful; your face shows it. Now, the wise men say that a sorrowful spirit withers the bone <note>A reference to Proverbs 17:22: 'A cheerful heart is good medicine, but a crushed spirit dries up the bones.'</note>. Therefore, it is not useful for you to keep this silent. There is no malice in my questioning, other than to help relieve your burden according to my ability." And as they were speaking thus with one another, Senesba <note>The ox, whose loud lowing is terrifying the Lion.</note> began again to cry out with a loud voice. The Lion was terrified by such a voice and said, "This voice has hindered me and made me afraid to leave my chamber; for I imagine that his person must be as large and strong as his voice, and consequently powerful. If that were so, we could no longer remain in this place." To this Dimna answered, "If there is no other reason why

XIIII.
# The Wisdom of the Ancients.

<note>The text continues from the previous page describing a fox encountering a drum hanging from a tree.</note>
blew, the bell <note>original: "schell"; here referring to the drum or hollow object making noise</note> gave its sound. And when the fox heard the loud sound, it brought him fear, and he thought that such a sound must come from a powerful animal. He worried about being driven from his home, and he crept virtuously <note>original: "tugendlich"; likely meaning cautiously or skillfully in this context</note> toward it. But when he saw the bell—that it was large but entirely hollow, empty, and powerless, and that there was nothing behind it but a sound—he spoke: "No longer will I believe that all things which have a greater appearance and a more gruesome voice therefore possess more strength."

# The Book of Wisdom
<page-num>XIV</page-num>

<margin>Parable of the wind.</margin>
The wind does not blow away the grass in the meadows, yet it blows around the strong oaks and great trees; so do the strong usually act, who do not show their strength against the sick and the powerless, but against the stronger or those of their own kind. <note>This parable suggests that the Lion King, being truly powerful, only contends with worthy rivals, not the humble.</note> Dimna spoke: "Lord King, do not be fearful; if it pleases you, I shall bring him before your face." The King said: "Go forth after him." Dimna went after Seneßba and spoke with him virtuously, so that he should not be afraid. And he said: "The King, the Lion, has sent me here to you, so that you may come to him and hasten to fulfill his commands; and he hereby forgives you the wrong of having lived here in his land for so long without presenting yourself to him. And if you wish to be disobedient in this, give me an answer to tell the King." Seneßba answered: "Who is he who has sent you to me?" Dimna said: "It is the King of all animals." Seneßba answered: "If you will give me a safe-conduct <term>gleydt: a formal promise of safe passage or legal protection</term> and keep faith that no harm shall be done to me by the King, then I will be willing to obey the King's command." Dimna swore an oath of faith to him, and thereupon went with him before the King.

# The Wisdom of the Ancients. XV.

The King greeted him and asked him virtuously: "How did you come into this land, or what cause brought you into this wilderness?" Seneßba <note>The ox, whose name in the original Sanskrit is Sanjivaka</note> recounted to the King all the matters and accidents that had befallen him from the beginning until now. The Lion spoke: "Seneßba, make your dwelling with us and fear not; I will keep you at my court and do you good." Seneßba thanked the King humbly. And when Seneßba had lived with the King for a short time, the King took him into his council and appointed him as an official <term>Amptman: a district governor or high-ranking administrator</term> of his land; for he found in him sensible advice and useful wisdom for all his affairs. Therefore, he entrusted him with his secrets and honored him more every day, drawing him closer to himself until he exalted him and honored him above all those who were in his court, and gave him the highest place above all his advisors.

And when Dimna <note>The jackal, known as Damanaka in the original Indian text</note> saw that the King had placed and exalted Seneßba in his stead over him and all his advisors, and that he was now his most secret counselor, it became very difficult for him. He took on a mournful spirit and began to envy him. He went to his brother Kellila <note>The jackal Karataka</note> and told him of his grievance, saying: "Brother, do not wonder at my foolish counsel and my vain oversight which I have done to myself, in that I brought Seneßba to the King for his benefit, so that he now drives me out from my place? I have put the sword into his hand with which he harms me." Then Kellila answered him: "It has happened to you as it once happened to a hermit." Dimna spoke: "How did it happen to him?" Kellila answered:

# The Book of Wisdom

### ¶ No one should trust entirely, lest they be deceived.

<margin>Of the
stag and the
fox.</margin>
"O holy man, I beg of you. I am naked and poor, and I hear much said of your holiness; therefore, I have come from far-off lands to take up residence with you, to serve you, and to learn from you." The hermit <note>original: "Einfidel" - a recluse or religious hermit</note> spoke: "You shall have lodging with me tonight." And in the morning, the thief's manner so pleased him that he asked him to stay with him. The thief lived with the hermit and served him well and devoutly, so much so that the hermit gained faith in him, trusted him entirely, and gave him full authority over his house. One day, the hermit went into a city to beg for his necessities. Then the thief arose, took all the hermit's clothes, and fled away. When the hermit came home again, he found that the thief had stolen his clothes and thought to search for him. He turned toward a city, and on the way in a forest, he found two stags fighting with one another until their blood was shed. He saw how a fox came there and licked the blood from the earth that ran down from them; the fox was so intent on this that he was stepped upon by the stags and also wounded, so that he fell dead.

---

### ¶ Treachery often strikes its own master.

<margin>Of the woman
who wanted to
blow poison
into the
nostrils.</margin>
Thus the hermit persisted in searching for the thief and came by night into a city, where he was sheltered in the house of a woman who was a procuress <note>original: "doppel" - a woman who runs a brothel or manages prostitutes</note>. She had a housemaid with her who was meant to earn money with her body and hand it over to her mistress. But the housemaid had found a lover <note>original: "bůlen"</note> who pleased her, and she would give herself to no one else. When the woman realized this was against her profit, she thought of how she might kill the lover for her. That evening, she sent the maid for her lover and gave him food and good drink so that he fell asleep therefrom. The woman took note of this and came secretly to the sleeper with a pipe <note>a hollow tube or reed</note> in which she had placed powder and poison, intending to blow it into his nostrils. But as she set the pipe to his nostrils—the one in which she had put the poison—and as she opened her mouth to blow, the sleeper breathed into the pipe so that the powder came into the woman's mouth, and she died from it immediately.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the hermit's travels, following his departure from the previous scene where he was seeking a thief.</meta>

# The Wisdom of the Ancients

### ¶ How a woman faithfully allowed herself to be bound to a pillar in place of her friend, and for this, her nose was cut off in her friend's stead.

Early in the morning, the hermit <note>original: "einsidel"; a religious recluse</note> went further to seek the thief and was sheltered for the night in the house of one of his friends. The friend commanded his wife that she should provide for the guest sufficiently, for he himself was invited out as a guest; he thus left the house, indicating that he would not return home that night. Now, the wife had a lover <note>original: "bülen"; a paramour or illicit lover</note>, and her neighbor, a barber's wife <note>original: "schererin"; the wife of a barber-surgeon</note>, was her procuress <note>original: "kupplerin"; a go-between for lovers</note>. She commanded the neighbor to bring the lover secretly through her house at night, as they had a secret passage between them; and so it happened.

In the meantime, her lawful husband returned and became aware of the lover in the house. When the lover escaped him, the husband beat his wife and afterwards bound her to a pillar in the house with a rope, and he laid himself down in his bed. The lover sent the barber's wife and asked her to inquire how it fared with his beloved, the mistress of the house. She found her bound to the pillar, and the wife told her that her lover was still in her house. The wife begged her with great urgency and said: "O dear friend <note>original: "gespil"; companion or playmate</note>, let yourself be bound here in my place, so that I may go to my lover for a time."

The barber's wife did as her friend requested and allowed herself to be bound to the pillar until the wife should return from her lover. During this time, the master of the house awoke and called to his wife with scolding words. The barber's wife gave no answer, for she feared that he would recognize her by her voice. And when he had called many times and she did not answer him, he ran to the pillar in a rage and cut off the woman's nose, thinking he had done this to his wife, and said: "Go now, and take your nose to your lover!"

<summary>A wife tricks her neighbor into taking her place while bound to a pillar so she can visit her lover, resulting in the innocent neighbor's nose being cut off by the jealous husband.</summary>
<keywords>infidelity, deception, punishment, barber's wife, pillar, betrayal, hermit's travels</keywords>

# The Book of Wisdom
<note>original: "Das bůch der Weißheyt"</note>

<margin>
A wise man
should consider
three things.
</margin>

Upon this Dimna spoke: "I understand your words, and our situation is indeed similar. Yet no one has done me harm but myself; therefore give me counsel: what should or can I do about it?" Kellila answered, "Brother, let me understand your intention." Then Dimna spoke: "I fear that I need no longer hope for a higher position, nor even to return to where I was before I was pushed out by Senesba <note>The ox, whose name is often rendered as Shanzabeh in other versions of these fables</note>. For every wise man should take heed and consider three things. First: that he distinguish between good and evil, that he guard himself against evil, and that he may draw the good toward himself. Second: that he maintain himself in his position of honor, and if he is pushed from it, that he may bring himself back into it. Third: that in all his affairs of this time, he can choose the good over the bad. Now that I see my accidental fall, it is necessary for me to consider how I may bring myself back into my position and overcome the one who cast me out. I know of nothing better for me than to seek plots against Senesba until I can deprive him of his life. And when I accomplish this, I know that I may return to my position with the King. I believe this would also benefit the lion, for the exceeding great love the lion has for Senesba makes him despised among the people." To this Kellila spoke: "It does not seem to me that the King should suffer for being so intimate with Senesba in secret, or that any evil should arise for him because of it." Then Dimna answered: "There are six causes

<margin>
Six causes
by which
the King
is harmed.
</margin>

by which the King is harmed: through the turning of fortune, through the rebelliousness of his servants, through lust, through the times, through flightiness of the head <note>meaning impulsiveness or lack of mental stability</note>, and through unvirtuous words. First, it is rightly called a turning of fortune when a lord loses his best and wisest counsel and servants, and when he changes his good customs. Second: when he gives cause for his countrymen to war against one another. Third: when the lord has so much pleasure with women, drinking, eating, and hunting that he neglects the necessities of his realm. Fourth: through flightiness of the head, when the lord is frivolous in his manners. Fifth: through the times, when through the course of time death and famine <note>original: "theůre" (dearth/high prices)</note> come into the lord's land. Sixth: through contrary works, when the King does what is not fitting for him to do and leaves undone what he ought to do. Thus the Lion has now placed his faith in Senesba and has revealed all his secrets to him; therefore he is despised by him." Kellila spoke: "How do you intend to harm Senesba (the Ox), since he is stronger than you, greater in dignity, in a higher position, and is more beloved by the King and the people than you? He also has more friends, companions, and followers." Dimna answered: "Brother, do not regard it this way. For a deed is not accomplished by strength and power alone. For many who are weaker of body and poorer of goods have, through their foresight <term>foresight: "fůrsichtigkeyt" - a mix of prudence, caution, and strategic planning</term>, reached such positions where the very powerful and rich could not go. Or perhaps you have not been told how the raven, with his foresight and cunning, killed the snake?" Kellila spoke...

<summary>Dimna explains his plan to regain his status by destroying the ox Senesba, justifying his treachery by listing six ways a king is harmed by poor governance and bad favorites. He argues that cunning and foresight are more effective than physical strength.</summary>
