<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the jackals Dimna and Kalila, where Dimna uses fables to justify his schemes. This page introduces the "Parable of the Raven and the Snake" and begins the "Parable of the Heron and the Crab."</meta>

# The Wisdom of the Ancients. XVIII.

<margin>Parable of the raven and the snake.</margin>
"How was that?" answered Dimna. "There was a raven <note>original: 'rapp'; a common term for a raven or crow in Early New High German</note> who had a nest in a tree. Beneath the tree was a hole in which a snake lay. And as often as the raven hatched his young, the snake poisoned them for him, even as he brought food to his chicks. Because of this, the raven became sorrowful and went to the fox, opening his complaint to him, saying: 'Does it not seem good to you that, when the snake is sleeping, I should peck out his eyes and thereby avenge myself? I beg you, show me your counsel.'</margin>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration divided into two sections. On the left, a raven perched on a low branch speaks to a fox beneath a large tree. The tree contains a nest with two small birds, while a snake is coiled in a burrow at the roots. On the right, viewed through an arched stone colonnade, a long-necked water bird (likely a heron or crane) stands at the edge of a pond where a crab is visible in the water.</image-desc>

### How unfaithful counsel strikes its master, as happened to the bird with the crab.

The fox answered: "That which you intend cannot happen through force or through bold violence. Rather, seek it through foresight, for it must be done with a clever plan <note>original: 'auffsatz'; meaning a trap or a calculated scheme</note>, lest something even worse grow out of it for you, and it happens to you as it once did to a bird with a crab."

<margin>Parable of the bird and the crab.</margin>
The raven answered, "How was that?" The fox said: "There was a bird <note>likely a heron or crane based on the context of the fable</note> who had his dwelling by a lake full of fish. Now, when the bird grew old, he could no longer hunt his food from the fish as he had done before. Therefore, he sat one day very sadly upon the shore of the lake. To him came, by chance <note>original: 'vngschicht'; meaning an accidental occurrence</note>, a crab, who said: 'Neighbor, what is the cause of your sadness?' He answered: 'Good friend, what good or happiness remains after old age?'"

<meta>catchword: My</meta>

# The Book of Wisdom

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a fable. In the center, a large bird (likely a heron) stands in water with its wings spread, carrying a fish in its beak. In the background is a detailed landscape featuring a walled city with towers, a bridge over a river, and various trees. In the water, several fish are visible, and on the right bank, a smaller bird and a crab (crayfish) are depicted. The scene suggests a river or lake environment.</image-desc>

<margin>
One should do good and give counsel even to enemies.
</margin>

¶ "My life has until now been sustained by the food of the fish; however, today fishermen passed by here and said: 'We want to catch all the fish of this lake at once.' One among them answered: 'No, not yet. I know a lake where there are much larger fish; we want to fish those out first, and after that, we will come to this lake.' Now I know that they will do such a thing, and that will be my ruin, for I shall have no food to sustain my life." The crab <note>The German "krebs" can refer to a crab or a crayfish; given the freshwater setting, a crayfish is likely intended.</note> went quickly to a school of fish, his companions, and told them what he had heard from the bird. Then they all came together to the bird and desired his faithful counsel; for a wise person does not hide his counsel even from his enemy who seeks help from him. The bird answered: "You know that I cannot withstand the fishermen by force; but I know a good, pleasant lake in which there is much water, around which many trees also lie, so that one cannot use nets therein. If you wish, I will carry you there." They thanked him and said: "We have no other helper in our need but you." The bird said: "I will do it for your benefit." Thereupon the bird took two of the fish every day and carried them up to a high mountain and ate them. One time the crab came to the bird and said: "I fear to remain here; I pray you, carry me to the lake to my companions." The bird took the crab and carried him to where he had eaten his companions; and when he brought the crab to the heights, the crab saw the bones of the consumed fish. Then he perceived the bird's deception <term>betreiügniß: betrayal or fraud</term> and realized that the same might happen to him.

<margin>
It is fitting for every person to save their life according to their ability.
</margin>

And he thought to himself: "It is fitting for every person to save their life according to their ability," and he placed his claws around the bird's neck and squeezed him so hard that he fell dead to the earth. And he went back into the lake and told his companions of the bird's falsehood, and how he had avenged them upon him, etc.

<summary>This page recounts the fable of the treacherous heron who tricks fish into being "rescued" only to eat them, until he is outwitted and killed by a crab. It serves as a moral lesson on the necessity of self-defense and the dangers of false counsel.</summary>

# The Wisdom of the Ancients. XIX.

I have told you this parable <note>original: "gleichnüß"</note> so that you might note how a treacherous person kills his own master. Therefore, I advise you to fly into a city and take heed of where the women wash themselves upon the rooftops and set aside their jewelry. Take one of these items, and when the people chase after you, throw the jewelry into the hole where the snake lives. When the people dig after it, they will find the snake, and thus it will be killed by them. The raven followed the advice of the fox, and the snake was beaten to death, and he was thereby set at rest.

¶ No one should allow themselves to be moved too harshly by anger, such that they give credit to a false person, lest it happen to them as it did to the lion because of the fox.

->[Image: A woodcut illustration showing a lion on the left standing on the edge of a stone well, looking down into it. Above the well is a wooden frame with a pulley and rope. On the right, a fox stands in a landscape with hills and a sparse tree in the background.]<-

I have told this example to make it understood that foresight and cleverness <note>original: "geschydigkeyt"</note> are sometimes better for accomplishing a matter than the strength or daring of a man. Kellila answered: You speak the truth. If Seneßba <note>The ox, a high-ranking advisor to the King</note> had not now come to such high standing, you might accomplish this; but now he has the practice of his wisdom and support, and is esteemed as the highest and wisest. Be certain that he will guard himself against everything in which he might recognize harm, especially if he notices your intention. Dimna answered: It is true that Seneßba can protect himself through his moral reason, but not from me; for he has entrusted his whole person to me and placed his faith in me from the day that I brought him to the King. But my necessity will require me to bring him out of this world, to—

<margin>E</margin>
<meta>catchword: bring</meta>

<summary>Dimna uses the fable of the raven and the snake to illustrate how cunning can defeat a stronger enemy, justifying his plot to betray the ox Seneßba. He argues that his personal intimacy with Seneßba gives him a unique advantage that wisdom alone cannot defend against.</summary>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the two jackals, Kalila and Dimna, from the "Book of Wisdom" (the German translation of the Fables of Bidpai). Dimna is explaining how a smaller creature can overcome a powerful one through cunning.</meta>

# The Book of Wisdom

the advancement of my status. Thus it must be done with discretion and with cunning, just as the fox did to the lion. Kalila spoke, "How was that?"
<margin>Parable of the lion and the fox.</margin>
Then Dimna answered: There was a lion in a wilderness, around whom lived many animals of all kinds. Now, their pasture and their movement were according to all their wishes, except only for the fear of the lion; for he came every day to harm them, against which they could not defend themselves. Now the fox called them together and gave them a counsel as to how they might escape the lion. And according to the discovery of their wisdom regarding the counsel, they sent the fox—who had also devised the plan—to the lion, speaking thus: "Lord Lion, know that it cannot continue in the long run that you may have food from us every day except with significant labor and hunting. Now we have thought of a way, useful for you and practical for us. If you grant us safety and freedom from worry, then we will willingly send you one animal from among us every day—whomever the lot happens to fall upon—at the hour of your meal for your food. And I will deliver that to you every day." This pleased him, and he promised the fox to keep the agreement. The fox went back and told this to his fellow animals.

But early the next morning the fox spoke, "See that I intend to stand by you with loyalty; I will be the first who shall undergo this adventure." And he set out on the journey to the dwelling of the lion and hid himself there, yet so that he could keep good watch over the lion. And when it nearly approached midday, the lion began to roar with anger, out of great impatience from waiting so long for his food. When the fox saw this—that the lion rose from his place out of impatience—he ran quickly toward the lion as if he had run himself nearly to exhaustion, and fell down before the lion's heart <note>original: "auff sein hertz," meaning at his feet or in his presence with great humility</note>. 

The lion spoke out of anger, "Why do you delay so long in bringing me my food, which was promised to me through you?" The fox answered, "Lord, my companions sent me out at a good time of day with another fox who had fallen to you today by choice for a meal, and who was quite substantial. And as I brought him not far from your dwelling, another lion came to me, asking what I was doing. I told him that I wanted to bring this food to you, my lord. He spoke: he was lord and not you, and such food belonged to him; he would also be a gracious lord to us and protect us well from you; and with that, he took your food from me." 

In great wrath, the lion asked if he could show him where he was. He spoke, "Yes, I followed him to his cave, which is not far from here." The lion asked to be led there; the fox went ahead, the lion behind, until they came to a well that was deep in the earth with water. The fox spoke, "The lion is in this cave." The lion hurried to the well, the fox with him, and [the fox] stood between his <insert>the lion's</insert> forelegs upon the well. The lion looked with anger into the well and saw his reflection in the water, and the reflection of the fox between his legs. The fox spoke, "Hurry! I see the lion, and the fox still standing unharmed with him." Out of the ferocity of his anger, the lion leapt into the well to fight with the other lion, and drowned. Thus the fox went to his companions and recounted how he had acted, and brought the lion, their persecutor, from life to death.

<summary>Dimna recounts the fable of a clever fox who tricks a predatory lion into jumping into a well by showing him his own reflection, illustrating how wit overcomes brute strength. This story serves as a justification for Dimna's plan to manipulate the King (the Lion) and his advisor (the Bull).</summary>

# The Wisdom of the Ancients

**Kellila** answered: "If you can harm **Senesba** in such a way that the **King** suffers no damage from it, then it is all the better, so that no evil comes upon you. For he has caused harm to me and many others at the court. However, if the King should take displeasure at your undertaking, then I advise you not to carry it out. Do not be rebellious against your lord, for that would be the worst of all, and you would face great loss and reproach."

Thus, **Dimna** resolved to avoid the **Lion** for several days. After many days had passed, he came before the King, appearing as though he were deeply sorrowful and restless.

### How Dimna came sorrowfully before the King and accused Senesba, who had become dearer to the King than he was.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing a crowned lion (the king) sitting on a throne at the left, holding a scepter topped with a fleur-de-lis. Standing before him in the center is a large ox (Senesba). The background depicts a landscape with trees and a walled city with towers and gabled buildings under a clear sky.</image-desc>

And when the King saw **Dimna**, he said: "Why have we not seen you for so long? Is all well?" **Dimna** answered: "It is a hidden matter, and a great secret." The **Lion** answered and said: "Reveal it to me; we are, after all, in a private place."

**Dimna** answered: "When a man is made suspicious of a matter in which he is warned that harm might come to him, and yet he refuses to believe it, he should nonetheless not betray the messenger—provided he knows that messenger to be of good conduct, simple life, and faithful counsel <note>original: "güts wandels, einfeltigs lebens, vnd getrewes rhats weiß"</note>. Rather, he should listen to the warning, take note, and observe what good or evil lies within it. For whether there is good or bad in the matter, it affects only the one who is being warned. Furthermore, neither good nor evil comes to the warner himself alone—"

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the jackal Dimna and the Lion King, following Dimna's efforts to sow discord between the King and the ox Senesba.</meta>

# The Book of Wisdom

<margin>Warning / faithful counsel / days of sickness / should not be concealed / and secrecy before friends.</margin>

...that he fulfills the duty and the love that he has toward his lord. Therefore, my lord and King, you are wise and understanding; I wish to tell you something. I know that you do not like to hear this, and forgive me, for you will believe me and yet you would not wish for it to remain hidden from you. For when I consider and see that the multitude of these animals—your people—can have no good or life except in you, I cannot find it in me to remain silent about the truth, but rather must reveal it to you so that you may protect yourself <note>literally: "fulfill yourself therein"</note>, though I fear you will not believe such things. Yet it is not fitting for me to conceal it. For whichever servant hides his faithful counsel or warning from his lord, or hides his days of sickness from the physician, or his secrets from his faithful friend, he does wrong, and the harm shall rightly fall upon his own head.

The Lion spoke: "Say, what is it?"

Dimna answered: "I have been told by one of my faithful companions that Senesba, the counselor <term>rhatpfleger: an advisor or administrative official</term>, has spoken with the highest of your host, saying: 'I have lived with the King for so long, and I have recognized his wisdom and strength, and have tested him, and I find him weak of spirit, of mind, and also of body, and one who cares nothing for his people nor loves them.' Now, since such things have come before me—and seeing that you have shown him honor and grace, and have placed him nearest to you, and made him a Vicar <term>Vicarien: a deputy or high-ranking representative</term> of your realm, and entrusted him with every secret—and yet he should have such treachery within him! For he presumes to make himself your equal, and it is to be thought that he has hope of possessing your kingdom after you, if he might find a way to drive you from it. Therefore, Lord King, do not be negligent in this matter. For the wise say: 'When a king finds some among his people who are eager to rise to his position, and who undertake such power with secret and private counsels and plots, it is fitting for the king to humble them; for if he fails to bring them to nothing, he himself will be destroyed.'

You are a lord wiser and more far-sighted than I, but it seems right to me that you should provide for your person and take hold of him before he escapes your power. Do not be dilatory in this, for through it harm might be inflicted upon you which you could not later withstand."

# XXI.
## The Wisdom of the Ancients

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting two fishermen standing on opposite banks of a narrow waterway or pond. They are operating a large, circular net that spans the water. In the background on the left is a castle or fortified building on a hill, and on the right is a landscape with a large tree and a wooden fence. Several fish are visible in the water and within the net.</image-desc>

<margin>The Lion</margin>
<margin>Three kinds of people in this world.</margin>
For it is said that there are three kinds of people in the world. The first
are far-sighted, and through their wise observations, they are able to
anticipate misfortunes as much as is possible to do, just
as a healthy person protects and guards themselves against sickness. The
others are careful and diligent in the face of adversity and accidents,
and remain undaunted in seeking a way out until they have escaped.
The third are of a heavy spirit and careless ways, who know nothing of
how to manage themselves and are confused in all their undertakings, just like three
fish. The Lion spoke: "How was that?" Dimna answered: "It is said there
were three fish in a pond <note>original: "waag" - a pool or standing body of water</note>; one was lazy, the second was far-sighted,
and the third was sensible. At one time, two fishermen came with their nets
and spread them in the water. The far-sighted one saw this and noticed what
the fishermen intended to do—that they had come for no other reason than
to catch them. He had the foresight to consider how he might save his life, and
he swam quickly out of the pond into the water that flowed into the pond; and though
it was small, he stayed within it so that he saved his own life.
The wise one <note>referring to the 'sensible' fish</note> had stood still until he saw the fishermen
block the outlet of the water. Then he said to himself: 'Now you have neglected yourself;
your life is at stake.' He thought to release himself without delay, considering
that long delays seldom lead to a fruitful end in matters that
will not wait. A sensible person should be reflective in times of need and
allow no fear to approach him so that he falters. For fortune favors
the bold <note>original: "dem gedürstigen" - literally 'the thirsty' or 'the daring'</note>. This fish swam to the surface of the water and floated there
as if he were dead. When the fishermen approached him, they judged him useless
and threw him onto the land; from there, in the midst of their fishing, he
returned to the water and was saved. The third swam aimlessly back

<summary>Dimna uses a fable about three fish—one proactive, one resourceful, and one lazy—to illustrate how different types of people respond to danger and misfortune. The story emphasizes that foresight and quick thinking are essential for survival.</summary>

### and again, what was erroneous in himself, and where he should go, until he was captured.

And the Lion spoke, "I have understood your words; but how can I think evil of Senesba, or that he desires harm against me, given the great love I have for him? For I have honored him and placed him above all others in my court." Dinma answered, "For no other reason does Senesba seek to harm you than because he has never felt, and does not feel, any offense from you, and because you have held no one in such an honorable state in your court; you have let him rule over it. Now he perceives that there is no higher state available into which he might elevate himself, unless he becomes lord over your kingdom. For every unfaithful person who possesses some wisdom and can speak well is to be guarded against. For they strive for ends of which they are not worthy, and even if one grants them a status that is rightfully more than enough for them, they still do not stop their minds from thinking higher—even if they must harm their lord in body or goods to do so. For none of these serve their lord for the lord's benefit, but rather seek their own profit at all times, how they may become rich and rise high. And though he may at first appear to give good and faithful service, <margin>Parable of the dog's tail.</margin> as soon as he comes to wealth and power, he begins to carry out the works that originate from the depths of his evil roots. He is like the tail of a dog, which is crooked. As long as you hold it firmly in your hands, it remains straight. As soon as you release your grip, it becomes crooked again as before. And I tell you, Lord King, whoever does not believe the advice of his counselors in things that are advised for his benefit is not to be praised, even if good should come to him from his own counsel. <margin>Parable of the sick man.</margin> For he is likened to a sick man who ignores the advice of the physician and follows his own cravings. For it is said: the best among the rich is he who is not a slave to his own desires. And the best among companions is he who neither angers nor quarrels. And it is said: a man might rest more securely lying by the water on the shore of the sea, or upon poisonous snakes, than near the unfaithful man who seeks his life. <margin>Parable of the elephant.</margin> Now it seems to me from this talk that the King wishes to be more negligent than is good, and would be well likened to the Elephant, which takes no heed of the things that happen before it."

never found any evil in him, nor noticed any malice, nor any false conduct, not even by a single step, since I took him as my servant and into my oath. And since I have now praised and exalted him before all people, above all my princes and folk, should I change this without manifest guilt? I would rightly be considered a fool within myself, for I would be denying my own loyalty and departing from the path of justice. <margin>Parable of the louse and the flea.</margin>

The King said, "What was that?" Dimna answered: "It is said there was a louse upon a nobleman, on a spot of his body, and it sucked so much of his blood that he never took notice of it. One night a flea came and passed by; the louse said: 'Stay here with me tonight; we shall have enough good food this night and a soft bed.' This flea stayed, and thinking the man slept, he began and bit for his food so hard that the man called for a light to be brought and for the bed to be searched. The louse was found and killed, but the flea, as soon as he saw the light, sprang here and there until he escaped."

"I have told you this example so that you may realize that the wicked man does not leave off from his wickedness; and what he cannot do by himself alone, he calls others to join him in. And I know that he intends to fight you personally and will not command anyone else to do it." "Nothing better than to depart him from this world. For a person who has a rotten or hollow tooth in his mouth is not left by his pain as long as he does not break it out. Thus, regarding an enemy who is to be feared, there is no better way to protect oneself than to bring him from his life." The Lion said: "You have now made me averse to the company of Seneßba. I will send for him and learn what is in his heart against me, and tell him that he should seek another place for himself." # The Book of Wisdom

would speak to him, and hear him in return, he would believe him. Dimna feared that the King might not keep secret what Dimna had brought before him, and spoke to the lion: "It seems to me not to be good advice to send for Senesba and confront him with his guilt. For he thinks you know nothing of it. You certainly have power over him; but if he is summoned and informed by you that you know the secret, it is to be worried and feared that he will seek clever and hasty ways to do you harm, thinking—as one who is guilty—how he might preserve his own life from you. The lion spoke: "Well then, I will take your advice, and if I see what you have given me to understand, I may well believe that you have told me the truth." When Dimna had brought the lion to his purpose, he thought to go to Senesba, so that he might turn his heart against the King and create a hatred between them; yet he did not want to go to Senesba without the King's knowledge, and spoke: "Lord King, if you will, I shall go to Senesba to learn what he does, what his conduct is, and to hear his words, from which perhaps something of these intentions might be understood to tell you in return." The lion spoke: "Go there, and take heed of his practice, and report it to me." Dimna went to Senesba in his house in a sad form, very melancholy. Senesba received him with honorable courtesy and spoke: "Why have I not seen you at court in so many days? Is there some news?"

# XXIII.
## The Wisdom of the Ancients
<note>original: "Der Alten weisen" — referring to the "Book of the Wisdom of the Ancient Sages," a German translation of the fables of Bidpai.</note>

->[Image: A woodcut illustration divided into two rectangular panels. The left panel shows a horned ox standing in a meadow before a town with tall spires. The right panel depicts another ox in a similar landscape of rolling hills and a medieval-style town.]<-

And Dimna began and spoke these words to Seneßba <note>The ox, whose name is often rendered as Schanzabeh in other versions of the Kalila and Dimna fables</note>: "Whosoever is pious abandons his own will and lives according to the will and pleasure of his master, whose servant he is. Although in that same master there is often neither loyalty nor faith upon which a faithful servant might rely for a single hour." Seneßba spoke: "What is this that you speak of? Is there something new to be expected?" Dimna answered: "Yes. But who is he who can prevent what is ordained? Or who is he who attains great honor and dignity and does not thereafter become a tyrant? Or who follows his own lusts without falling? Or who commits himself to the love of women and is not deceived? Or who asks a fool for a gift without regretting it? Or who keeps company with the wicked and remains unhurt? Or who dwells in the courts of princes without his reputation and honor being wounded?"

<margin>The loyalty and love of princes is like a fickle woman.</margin>
Just is the word of those who have spoken: The love and loyalty of princes toward their servants is much like a fickle woman <note>original: "üppigen frawen" — implying vanity, inconstancy, or loose morals</note>; she clings to one after another, and the last is always the dearest. Seneßba spoke to him: "I must understand from your words as if something new has happened to you with the King." Dimna spoke: "Yes, it is true. Yet it does not affect me personally. But would to God that it did affect me, for I would know how to adapt to it. However, because of the friendship that exists between me and you, and the vows and union that I am bound to keep with you—and also because I promised you my companionship from the time the Lion <note>The King of the forest</note> sent me to you—I knew of no way I could more usefully keep that same faith than this: for I swore to you that I would never deceive you or break my vow of union between us. Rather, I wished to reveal everything that became known to me wherein harm might come to you." Then Seneßba spoke: "What is it?" Dimna spoke: "I have been told secretly by a good and faithful companion..."

When Seneßba heard this, he was terrified and became sorrowful, and said to Dimna: "It is not fitting for the Lion to undertake evil against me, for I have never done him any harm, nor anyone else in the world. pious, and simple-minded as he has found in the wicked. He will one day act like a water bird <note>likely a heron or kingfisher</note> who swam on a lake at night and saw the reflection of a star; he took it for a fish and dove under the water, searching for it the whole night, but could not find it. At last, he gave up his search. In the morning light, he saw a real fish, but he thought it was the same reflection he had seen in the night and did not bother to seek it, and thereby suffered hunger. Likewise, if lying words about me have reached the King, and he expects from me what has previously happened to him from others—that such things are also true of me—he intends to destroy me for it. He does not consider how entirely I have submitted to his will and avoided everything that I knew to be

<margin>Anger with cause and envy may be turned aside, but without cause, they cannot.</margin>
contrary to him. Now, when anger or envy arises from a cause, there is hope for improvement, that it might somehow be softened. But when hatred, envy, or anger arise without cause, there is no hope for improvement. For when the cause is presented and not found to be true, the anger is laid aside, or the cause is answered according to a fair opinion or pleaded away through mercy. But what happens through falsehood behind one's back, without a presented cause, lasts until death. Now, I know of no cause at all why the King should hate me so harshly. I do not know that I have ever been angry with him, except that at times in his secret council I spoke against him when his undertaking did not please me. And when he perhaps said, 'I want it thus and not otherwise,' I told him what evil would arise from it and what good might come

For even if the King is pious and faithful, it is nonetheless possible for an innocent man to fall into such snares through several unfaithful companions, from which the others cannot help him. <margin>Wisdom brings envy.</margin>

For in all places one finds more of the wicked than the good; more of the poor than the rich; more of the foolish than the wise; therefore it is not good to make oneself an addition to a crowd. Dimna spoke: "You should know that the enmity which the lion bears toward you is through none of the recounted causes, but through the self-gathered malice of his heart. For its beginning is always sweet honey, and its end is deadly poison." Senesba answered: "Now I believe your words, that they are true; for I have tasted that same honey, and it seemed very sweet to me in the sensation. Now I find that I have come upon the poison of which you told me; for had my desire not stood particularly toward the Lion through his sweet will, conduct, and words, I would not have remained with him, especially since he eats flesh and I eat grass. But my will and lust for the lion have thrown me into this snare. And it has happened to me as it does to the 
<margin>Parable of the bees.</margin>
bees, which at the rising of the sun, when the flowers open, sit therein; and through the sweetness they find within, they remain until the setting of the sun, so that the flowers close again and they perish therein. For he who does not let himself be satisfied with modest nourishment in this time, but follows his eyes after the extravagant honors of this world and does not 
<margin>Parable of the fly.</margin>
consider the future, he is like the fly <note>original: "mucken"</note> which is not content to suck the moisture of the fruits, but instead sets itself upon the bodies of powerful beasts, by which it is crushed. Also, he who with faithful counsel and love burdens his body with labor and the weariness of his mind for one who does not appreciate it, he is like one who tells his secret to one who does not hear." For when a whole gathering of traitors unites against one, even if he is innocent, they may still destroy him, even if he is powerful and strong and they are weak. # The Book of Wisdom

Seneßba <note>Likely a variation of Senesba or Shanzabeh, the name of the ox in the original Kalila and Dimna fables</note>. It is said there was once a lion who had three companions as his servants: a wolf, a fox, and a raven. These four animals all eat flesh. One day, a merchant passed by and left behind a camel that could go no further due to exhaustion; it went and sought its pasture so that it might bring itself back to strength. By chance, it came to the place where it found the lion and his company. To it, the lion spoke: "For what reason are you here?" The camel answered: "I am here to serve the King, if he has need of me, to fulfill his commands." The lion answered: "If our company pleases you, and you wish to be faithful to me, then you may remain safely with us in peace and without fear of any adversity."

So the camel remained with the lion for a long time. At one time, when the lion had gone out to seek his food, an elephant <note>original: "Helffant"</note> arrived who took up a fight against him and wounded the lion with his teeth <note>referring to tusks</note>, and tore him in many places, so that the lion escaped from the elephant in a faint, wounded and bloody, to his dwelling, and could no longer seek his food. It happened that hunger overcame the lion, and he saw also that his companions lacked food, which brought sorrow to the lion, and he spoke to them: "I see and note your want; now I am obligated to provide for you as my young and my servants." They gave answer and spoke: "We mourn not only our own necessity, but much more for you, our lord, considering what depends upon you, with great desire to turn away your suffering..."

it is this: then spoke the raven. "It seems advisable to us that you should take the camel to feed us. For he is not of our kind, nor are we of his; he also does not belong to our council at all." The lion grew angry at the raven and spoke: "Be silent, you accursed one! May God shame you. How base is your advice; there is neither loyalty nor faith in you. How could you be so bold as to give me this advice? Or do you not know what I promised and secured to the camel with my words and by my faith? And that in the world there is no greater justice than to come to the aid of the innocent, whose blood would otherwise be shed without cause <note>original: "vnuerschuldt," meaning undeservedly or without guilt</note>." The raven answered: "Lord King, you speak the truth; but through one body, all the bodies of this house shall be redeemed, as well as the bodies of the entire race and the King’s people, all of whom would be lost through your death." The raven went back to his two companions and told them what he had said and how he had answered the King; and they further took counsel on how they wished to accomplish the matter. The fox spoke: "You see that the lion is only concerned about his oath in this matter. Therefore, we must give this matter such a form that the lion is released from his oath; perhaps then he will follow." The raven spoke: "Lord Fox, we listen entirely to your advice for our salvation." The fox spoke: "It seems good to me that we go together before the camel and recount to him the past kindnesses we have received from the lion without any intermediary, explaining that we are indebted to repay him. Although we could not repay even a fortieth part of what he has done for us, we can find nothing now to give him. Therefore, we wish to surrender ourselves and each of our persons individually to him, so that he may have us for food, each of us speaking thus: 'Lord King, I wish for you to eat me, so that you do not die of hunger.' When one of us three speaks, let the others among us stand forward and say the same word, until it reaches the camel. By this, we shall earn great goodwill from the King; and when it finally comes to the camel and he speaks likewise, we shall all agree to it and testify that no injustice is done to him because of his own consent."

# The Ancient Sages. XXVII.
<sig>f iij</sig>

healthy new blood might be increased; and so the three of them went <note>The three conspirators: the crow, the wolf, and the jackal.</note> together and dragged the camel to the earth, and gave it to the King and to themselves for food, although the Lion showed some mercy in the matter.

I have told this parable to compare it to my King, the Lion, and his companions; for I perceive that they are gathered against me to kill me. And even if the King is not of that mind himself, they nevertheless carry words into his ears daily, so that they may at last accomplish their plot. But the wise men say: The most laudable among all Kings is he who resembles an eagle, around whom there are always many dead bodies <note>Meaning a powerful provider or a formidable warrior.</note>; and the most unpraiseworthy King is he who resembles a dead body, around whom there are always many vultures. For even if the King had a truly peaceful heart and a just will toward me, such useless words might at last turn him around or pervert his goodness. For you see that drops of water fall upon a hard stone for so long until they make a hole in it. Dimna spoke: What do you think is good for me to do in this matter? Then Seneßba answered: I know of nothing better than to seek a way for the salvation of one's person. For I know of no more just struggle than that a man keeps faith with his companion in whom he trusts, and who fights faithfully for his own body against those who wish to shorten his life with falsehood; for two good things may happen to him. One is: if his life is taken from him in the process, he has eternal life, for he died in innocence. The other is: if he can save his life until the falsehood comes to light, then he overcomes his enemy and puts him to shame. Dimna answered: It is fitting for a wise man to give himself to the labor of death only if he cannot excuse himself with other suitable plans; for if he died thus <note>without trying other means</note>, he would be guilty against himself and would sin.

<margin>A wise man should settle his immediate wars before his business.</margin>
For a wise man should settle his immediate wars before all his other business. There is a proverb: That no one should despise his enemy, even if he were very weak and not respectable. For you know the strength of the lion and also his power; for whoever despises the business of his enemy, to him it happens as it did to a bird who was a prince of the Sea, against two water-birds. Seneßba spoke: How did that happen to him?

<margin>Parable of the bird.</margin>
Dimna answered: There were two birds on the shore of the Sea, a male and a female; and when the female produced young, she spoke to the male: "Look about for another place for us that is safer than this, where we may raise our young until they can travel themselves." Her husband answered her: "Is this not a safe place? Here is water and a pleasant shore with herbs, and better than we might find elsewhere." The woman spoke: "Consider what you say; do you not see that the Sea rises up and may carry away our nest with the young?" The man spoke: "I cannot believe that, for the Prince of the Sea would have to reckon with us." The woman answered: "How foolishly you speak! Do you not know that no one in the world is so strong that he is master of the Sea, or who may withstand it and its princes?"

<margin>No more harmful enemy than a man to himself.</margin>

XXVIII
->The Book of Wisdom<-

# ¶ No one should heed mockery when good is to be done for him in a matter, lest it happen to him as it did to the tortoise, who fell to her death because of such a response.

<note>This heading introduces the moral of the fable: focus on the goal rather than reacting to critics.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting the fable of the tortoise and the birds. Two birds, possibly storks or cranes, fly through the air, holding a stick in their beaks. A tortoise is suspended in the air, biting onto the center of the stick. Below them is a landscape featuring a stone-lined well and sparse vegetation. To the right, a large bird wearing a crown (possibly a peacock or a kingly bird) stands on a rocky mound, looking toward the flying tortoise.</image-desc>

The man spoke: "How was that?" Then the woman spoke: "There were once in a field, by a well, two birds and a tortoise living together in companionship. And it happened at one time that it did not rain, and the well dried up, and the earth became very parched. Therefore, the birds took counsel to move from that place to another location where there would be no lack of water. They went to the tortoise to take their leave of her and told her of their lack of water. The tortoise gave them an answer and spoke: 'I know that you may not lack for water, as you can always fetch what you need for your necessity. But for me, poor creature, who must live in the water alone, there is a great lack. I beg you, show me mercy and take me with you.' They agreed to this and spoke: 'Now see to it, when we carry you through the air, if anyone encounters you, do not speak. Or if anyone questions you, take care that you give no answer.' She promised to do this. Then they spoke to her: 'Take a small stick <term>hölzlīn</term><note>A diminutive form of "Holz" (wood), meaning a little twig or stick.</note> in your mouth and hold it very firmly in your teeth; then I will take one end and my companion will take the other end, and so we will fly with you through the air to the place we have chosen.' This was done. And as they carried her high through the air, some of her own kind <note>The text says "seins geschlechts," implying other tortoises or perhaps just other creatures on the ground.</note> saw her and cried out to her, being seized with wonder and looking at the marvel: 'There flies the tortoise through the air between two birds!' When the tortoise heard this, she gave an answer: 'Yes, I am flying here, whether that hurts you or not!' And as she opened her mouth to speak, the little stick escaped from her teeth, and she fell down to the earth so that she died."

<meta>catchword: An example</meta>

<summary>This page recounts the famous fable of the tortoise and the birds to illustrate the danger of pride and the importance of ignoring distractions. It warns that reacting to mockery can lead to one's downfall.</summary>

# XXVIII.
## Of the Ancient Sages.

¶ An example of the bird, showing that the brief counsel of women is at times also to be followed, and is useful therein.

THen the man <note>The male bird</note> spoke to the woman <note>The female bird</note>: "I have understood your words, but I take no heed of the Sea." It happened, when the female bird had hatched her little birds, that the Prince of the Sea <note>original: "fürſt des Mörs"; a personification of the ocean</note> heard that the bird so utterly despised him, and he wished to see how the bird would defend himself, or what counsel he would take in the matter. He caused the Sea to rise so that it carried away the bird's young along with the nest. When the female perceived this, she spoke to the male: "Now your folly has fallen upon our necks, with the loss of our children." The male answered: "You shall yet find that the Prince of the Sea must return our children to us, even against his will." He set out and went to his companions and all the other birds by the Sea, and lamented to them what had been inflicted upon him by the Prince of the Sea, saying: "If this is not prevented, you do not know when the same may happen to you." The birds gave him answer: "We are displeased by what has happened to you, and it seems right to us to offer you help. But how can we fight against the Sea and its Prince? However, such help as is within our power you shall find in us." He answered: "Then my counsel is that we all go together to the Queen of the birds, which is the stork <note>In this version of the *Panchatantra* tradition, the Simurgh or Garuda figure is represented as a stork</note>; she is the one for us to seek." And when they sought her, they found her among her people and lamented to her the bird's loss which had been inflicted by the Prince of the Sea. "Now, you are our Queen, and your husband is stronger than the Prince of the Sea." The female stork took up the complaint and wrote to her husband <note>The King of the birds</note> that he should wage a battle against the Prince of the Sea. When the Prince of the Sea heard this, he knew himself to be too weak against the Prince of the birds, and he ordered the bird's children to be delivered back to him.

I have told you this example because it is not useful for you to despise the King and to fight against him, and thereby give yourself over to the fear of death. Senesba <note>The ox</note> answered: "Nothing seems better to me than that I go to the King and appear quite cheerful before him, so that I show him no ill will; for I shall go to him just as I did before, until I see from him that which is to be feared." When Dimna <note>The jackal</note> heard this, he was displeased by it and thought: "If the Lion does not see the signs in him which I told him of before, then the King might think that my report was suspicious, and thereby my schemes might become manifest." And he spoke to Senesba: "Approach the King, and you may truly find through his gestures what is within him." Senesba answered: "How may I know that?" Dimna answered: "When you come to the King, if you see him standing fiercely and looking toward you as if he were ready to fight, and he moves his head toward you and looks at you with wide-open eyes and with flattened ears, and he strikes the earth with his tail, then know that you are dead; so watch yourself and be ready for defense." Senesba answered: "If I see from the Lion that which you tell me, then I shall perceive that you have told me the truth."

Kellila spoke to him, "For what reason do you come to me regarding your business?" Dimna answered, "Now salvation and good fortune approach according to my desire and your pleasure. For I have created such adversity between the King and Senesba that I know Senesba shall die at the King’s hands."

<margin>He who must dwell in the halls of Kings is like one who lives among poisonous beasts.</margin>
So Kellila stood up, and he and Dimna went to the Lion. At that moment, Senesba also came walking toward the lion. The lion wondered at Senesba’s arrival and thought of Dimna’s words; he looked upon Senesba with anxiety and with anger. And when Senesba saw the signs from the Lion that Dimna had predicted to him—that he rose up against him with angry gestures, <term>laid back his ears</term><note>original: "seine oren schmuckte" - a physical sign of feline aggression</note>, struck the earth with his tail, and looked at him with wide, staring eyes—he then thought of what Dimna had told him before and said to himself: "Unhappy is he who must dwell in the halls of Kings. ->The Wisdom of the Ancients<-

And he <note>referring to the bull, Senesba</note> also thought to look about himself and to stand in the hall with an advantage regarding the speech of Dimna, so that he might escape or evade the lion until someone intervened. When the lion saw Senesba standing defensively and looking about, he first believed what Dimna had told him—how Senesba would behave—and he sprang against Senesba and struck him, wounding him badly. Senesba defended himself as best he could, yet still offering friendly words. But the lion's spirit was so inflamed in anger, and he dealt Senesba such great wounds, that Senesba fell dead. And when the lion saw Senesba lying dead, he gazed upon him and became troubled in his soul that he had brought Senesba from life to death and shed his blood without a lawful discovery of the truth and justice.

<margin>No heart's desire should be too hasty in its deed, lest it be regretted.</margin>
<margin>Evil counsel seeks the shedding of blood.</margin>

Now when Kellila, Dimna's brother, had seen this, he spoke to Dimna: "Look at the end of your works and your speech, which have truly been evil. For you have saddened the Lion and killed Senesba, and turned the hearts of the entire court against the King, so that they have taken fright at this matter; whereas they had always found the King to be kind until now, they have now seen him act so tyrannically. And above all, it is to be feared that your words, for which you have been praised and loved until now, will turn entirely to hatred once they are revealed."

<margin>The outcome of war is doubtful.</margin>

"Have you never heard that it is useless counsel for whoever seeks war or the shedding of blood? For a wise man should protect himself from war as far as he is able. Also, he should not advise his lord to begin one without significant cause. For its outcome is <term>doubtful</term><note>original: "zweiffelhafftig" - meaning unpredictable or precarious</note>."

# The Book of Wisdom

And it is said that no matter requires a higher intellect than war and the love of noble women, if one intends to pursue them without the aid of luck. I tell you, brother Dimna, I fear that your excessive desire for honor and power will lead you to a bitter end through this false plot. In the end, you will gather what you have branched out and reap what you have not sown, and evil shall befall you. I must then also bear a share of this, for when one member of a family has committed an evil deed, that same lineage is seldom trusted with anything better, as it is assumed they are all of the same mind. I have rebuked you from the beginning and revealed to you what would follow once the deed was accomplished—namely, misfortune. For the shedding of blood and the false slandering of people does not remain hidden, just as the proverb says: *No thread is spun so fine that it does not eventually come to the sun* <note>A German idiom similar to "the truth will out" or "what is done in the dark will come to light."</note>. But I have found that my teaching has taken no hold on you, and I rightly follow him who says: Do not trouble yourself to counsel him who will not follow; do not teach him who will not learn; and do not rebuke him who wishes to remain unpunished.

# The Wisdom of the Ancients. XXX.

<margin>Parable of the hard stone.</margin>
Then Dimna said, "How was that?" Kellila answered him: It is said there was a troop of monkeys on a mountain. One night it was cold, and they saw a glow from a little glow-worm <note>original: "nachtwürmlin," literally a little night-worm</note>, and they thought that it was a fire. They gathered much wood and laid it over the glow, and blew all night with their mouths and hands. Now, the glow-worm's light was under a large tree upon which there were many birds; some of them came down and spoke to the monkeys: "You work in vain, for this is not what you think it is." And when they would not desist, one bird rebuked them for their foolish minds. One among the monkeys went to him and said, "Dear friend, do not instruct that which will not be instructed, and do not teach that which cannot learn, and do not rebuke that which will not let itself be rebuked. For a stone that a pickaxe <note>original: "bickel"</note> cannot break, no one should attempt to hew with his good sword; and do not attempt to make a willow-withe <note>original: "wid," a flexible branch used for binding</note> out of that which will not let itself be bent; for whoever attempts this gains no use from it." And when the bird would not turn away from it and would not stop his rebuking, a monkey seized him and trampled him with his feet so that he died.

And as I see that you are of such a mind, my rebuke and teaching help you not, for your heart is embraced by vain honor and deceit, which both give birth to evil habits. 

And know that whoever undertakes that which does not befit him—even if it were done with truth—his undertaking might still fail him, and it happens to him as it did to the magpie <note>original: "atzel"</note>. Dimna said, "How was that?" Kellila answered: There was a merchant in the land of Persia who had a beautiful wife, and she loved another <note>original: "bülte," meaning to have a lover or paramour</note>. The merchant wanted to find out the real truth, and he raised a magpie, which he taught to speak so that it should tell him what happened in his house. One day the man went from the house to his business; immediately the woman sent for her lover. The lover came and stayed with her for a time, and then went again on his way. When the man came home, he asked the magpie, who told him everything she had seen regarding the lover and the woman. Upon this, the man beat the woman very hard. The woman thought that her maids had betrayed her and quarreled with them. The maids told her that the magpie had done it. When the woman heard this, she thought: "If you kill the magpie, your husband will think it was for that reason, and you will have it worse than before," etc.

# The Book of Wisdom

¶ Whoever takes upon themselves that which does not benefit them—even if they act rightly—may nevertheless suffer harm from it; just as the magpie was beaten to death for that reason, as follows hereafter.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a domestic interior scene. A group of five women in Renaissance-era attire are gathered in a room. One woman is seated on a low bench, while another stands near a large birdcage on a wooden stand, which contains a bird (a magpie or "atzel"). On the far right, a man wearing a feathered cap and holding a staff peers into the room through a doorway or window opening.</image-desc>

And at one time during the night, when the husband was again not at home, she <note>the unfaithful wife</note> sent again for her lover <term>bulen: a term for a paramour or illicit lover</term>, and commanded the maidservants to stand around the magpie <term>atzel: a magpie, birds often depicted in folklore as truth-tellers or gossips</term>. She gave a bell into the hand of one, so that she should ring it. To the second, she gave a mirror, which she was to hold before the magpie's eyes every so often. The third sprinkled water on it with a brush. The fourth rolled a log <note>original: "ploch"</note> near the cage. The fifth rocked the cage in which the magpie sat. And while this lasted, the magpie could not perceive the woman’s conduct. In the morning, the husband came home and asked the magpie about his wife. It answered and said: "How could I perceive anything of that, since I have been in such great distress all this night from rain, lightning, thunder, and earthquakes <note>original: "erdbidem"</note>, so that it was feared the world would perish." And when the husband heard that—for it was during the winter time—he thought that all the words the magpie had told him about his wife must be lies. And he took the magpie and killed it.

# The Wisdom of the Ancients

The bird’s loyalty—and although he was a soothsayer <note>original: "warsager"; a diviner or one who predicts the future</note>—it did not befit him according to his nature. Therefore, I have told you this so you may know: whoever takes up matters that do not concern him, even if those matters are true, he will eventually be brought down by them.

<margin>Example of the Deceiver and the Swift.</margin>
And I know that what will happen to you in the end is what happened to a certain man. Dimna said, "What happened to him?" Kalila said: There were once two men who met on the road and agreed to travel together. One was named Deceiver <note>original: "trüigner"</note> and the other was named Swift <note>original: "schnell"</note>. As they traveled together on the way back to their homeland, they found a small sack full of silver. When they drew near to the city, Swift said to Deceiver, "Give me half of the silver." Deceiver answered him, "That shall not happen, for our companionship should still last a long time between us. Therefore, let each of us take only as much of the silver as he needs right now. The rest we shall keep in a secure place, so that when we have need of it, we may find it there." His companion, Swift, believed he spoke from a good heart and followed his advice; they hid the silver inside a large tree, and from there each went to his own home.

# The Book of Wisdom

<margin>
Foolish counsel often fails its master.
</margin>

Now hear the proof of the name: The Deceiver went secretly to the tree and took all the silver, carrying it home into his own power. Afterward, the Swift One came to his companion and said, "Come now, go with me to the treasure, for I have need to take my portion." The other said, "Gladly." And when they dug for the treasure, they found nothing. Then the Deceiver began to tear at himself <note>a gesture of feigned grief or frustration</note> and said, "No one should trust his companion or brother, for you have done this yourself!" The Swift One swore by the living God that he was innocent of it. The Deceiver answered, "No one knew of this but I or you; for no one else knew the spot," and said, "Come, we shall go before the judge, that he may decide the matter for us." When they came before the Magistrate <note>original: "Potestat," a term for a high-ranking civil official or governor</note> and he had heard them, he asked if there were any witnesses. The Swift One answered, "No." The Deceiver answered, "Yes, it is the tree under which we buried it." The Magistrate answered, "Lead me to the tree tomorrow morning to decide your case." Now the Deceiver went home and told his father of his business and said, "If you will lend your help to this, the treasure will remain ours alone, and we may increase it for our great sustenance." The father asked how that might be. The son told him how he had taken the treasure and said, "I wish for you to climb into the tree tonight, for it is hollow at the top, and remain therein through the night. And when the Magistrate comes tomorrow morning and asks the tree who has taken the silver, you shall give answer from within the tree and say: 'The Swift One came and took it.'" The father said, "How often has foolish counsel failed its master? Take care that it does not happen to you as it did to a bird." The son said, "How was that?" The father answered: "There was a bird not far from a body of water who had his nest in a tree wherein he raised his young. He had a snake for a neighbor in that same tree, who ate his young or his eggs. Now it was very difficult for the bird to leave the place because of the goodness of the fish and other delights, and yet it was repugnant to him to be there near such a neighbor. He told this to a crab <note>original: "krepß," likely a crayfish or crab</note>, who answered him and said, 'I know how to advise you so that you may always remain safe from the snake.' And he led him to a hole by the path wherein lived a beast like a dog <note>likely a mongoose or weasel-like predator</note>. The bird complained to him about the snake. He said, 'Go hence and gather so many fish that you may lay them one after another from the shore of the water unto the hole of the tree where the snake dwells.' The bird did this and arranged one fish after another unto the tree and the hollow. In the morning, the beast went up the shore and found the fish and ate one after another, and thereby came to the hole of the snake and killed it; but he also found the hole where the bird was, and ate him too, along with his young."

# XXXII.
## The Ancient Sages
<sig>G ij</sig>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a scene from a fable. In the center is a thick, hollowed-out tree trunk with a face visible through a hole. How many a man’s plot turns back upon his own neck. "Therefore, dear son, I have told this fable so that my gain does not turn into my loss." The deceiver answered, "Father, cease such talk, for these things are very easily done." And he persuaded his father to climb into the tree and remain therein the whole night. Early the next morning, the two went there with the <term>Potestat</term> <note>A high-ranking magistrate or chief administrator, from the Latin 'potestas' meaning power.</note> and his servants, and stood before the tree, and asked who had taken the silver from there. The one in the tree spoke: "The swift one <note>Referring to the partner named 'Schnell' or 'Swift' in the original German.</note> has taken it." The Judge was startled and looked around him and up at the tree; and when he saw no one, he ordered wood to be placed around the tree and set on fire. When the smoke and the heat reached the old man in the tree, he began to scream, and was pulled out of the tree half-dead. When the Potestat saw this deception, he ordered the father and the son to be tortured and gave all the silver to the swift one. From such punishment the old man died; the son took him and carried him home, and remained in mourning through the loss of his father and the silver.

# The Book of Wisdom

->[Image Description: A woodcut illustration depicts a scene inside a room with vaulted ceilings and classical columns. On the left, a man lies in a bed, partially covered by blankets. On the right, a woman dressed in a gown and a decorative headdress stands holding a large, round sieve over a small bowl or container, as if sifting something into it.]<-

you speak with two tongues and carry two faces in your countenance. The Lion is entirely without guilt regarding <term>Senesba</term><note>The ox character, often known as Shanzabeh in the original Sanskrit fables of the Panchatantra</note>; the fault lies alone with your tongue, which has rooted them both against one another. For there lives hardly a person in the world who cannot be persuaded and deceived by flattering tongues. Human hearts cannot always guard themselves against the deception of malicious and flowery words. They are pulled along by the rope of false pretenses, just like the woman who deceived her husband with her contrived words. **Dimna** spoke: "How was that?" **Kellila** answered him: "There was a man who had a beautiful wife, and she was an <term>adulteress</term><note>original: bůlerin</note>. One day, as her husband lay sick, he gave her money and told her to go to the <term>apothecary</term><note>A medieval pharmacist or chemist</note> and buy what was necessary for his health according to the apothecary's advice. The woman went to the apothecary (who was her lover) so that he might give her medicine for her husband. He led her into the pharmacy and stood there with her for a while, and he commanded the apprentice in the pharmacy to prepare that for which she had come in the meantime. The boy, though trained in the pharmacy, was not yet wise; he took dust in a small cloth and tied it up tight, and gave it to the woman, telling her she had what she required. The woman went home and gave the husband the cloth, tied up as it was, and went to fetch a vessel in which such medicine should be kept. In the meantime, the man untied the cloth and found the dust inside, and he cried out to the woman: 'What is this supposed to be? You went for medicine and you bring back dust?' When the woman heard this—having not yet returned to her husband's side—and realized she had been failed by the apothecary's boy, she quickly took a small sieve in her hand and went to her husband, saying: 'Oh, you do not know what happened to me! A running horse came by and knocked me to the ground, whereby the money fell from my hand; and since I could not find it quickly because of the crowd of people, I gathered up the dust and tied it in my head-

<summary>Kellila tells Dimna a cautionary tale about a deceitful wife who attempts to cover up a failed tryst with an apothecary by claiming she accidentally gathered dust after being knocked over by a horse.</summary>

**¶ No one should deceive another for the sake of a reward, lest it happen to him as it did to the host who lost his son.**

<margin>Parable of the water.</margin>
Now I have told you this example so that you may understand that no one can protect themselves from false, unfaithful words; one must eventually place belief in them, whereby the heart is deceived. Just so has the Lion been deceived by your words and has believed them. For know that waters are good before they are mixed or troubled. So also are companions good, as long as no envious or evil person dwells among them. And I know nothing in the world to which I could liken you, except a form from whose mouth poison drips. And therefore I am always terrified of your company. For the Sages say: the company of the unfaithful is to be avoided, even among one's own closest kin.

<margin>The company of the unfaithful is to be avoided.</margin>
But from those who are of a noble mind <note>original: "Edels gemüts"</note> and have a love for justice, you should never part; and guard yourself against the presence of the unfaithful. And even if he be of wise counsel, still guard yourself against his unfaithful tricks, and do not depart from the company of the noble and the just; and even if he cannot create much benefit for you, it should suffice you that you know him to be pious. But flee from the frivolous and the false; therefore I should rightly flee from you and remove myself from your company, for therein is nothing good to be found. Therefore I may well liken you to a fable in which the merchant said: if the earth is such that mice eat iron, then it is possible that its birds carry away children. Dimna said: "How was that?" Kellila answered: "There was a merchant who, among other merchandise, had a hundred pounds of iron; he placed it in an inn in a city for safekeeping, and he traveled to another land for his trade. And when he returned after years, he demanded his iron from this host. The host said: 'Truly, I had placed the iron in a corner of my house, and there the mice have eaten it.' The merchant said: 'Never has it been seen that there is an animal that eats iron, except for an ostrich, and here the mice eat it. But nevertheless, I value all that as nothing, as long as you have guarded yourself from them, so that you have come away from them unharmed.' The host was happy at the merchant's words and invited him to eat with him; and when the merchant came out of the house, he thought that he might steal the host's son, who at that time was a brave young boy and very dear to his father; and when he had stolen him, he kept him in another of his inns, and afterwards came back into the host's house. Then the host said to him: 'Have you nowhere seen my young son? I have now lost him these three days.' The merchant answered: 'The day before yesterday I saw a bird near your house that snatched up a child; whether that was your son, I do not know.' When the host heard this, he cried out with a loud voice and said to the bystanders: 'Have you ever heard that birds [carry away] children...'"

## ¶ How Kalila rebukes his brother Dimna for his evil, false spirit and words.

<margin>The worst and most harmful thing in the world.</margin>
**T**hus all faith has departed from you, Dimna; therefore I have told you this fable, for you have deceived the King. What more is there to trust in you compared to others? Now there is nothing more harmful in the world than to place faith in one in whom there is neither loyalty nor faith, and whoever shows mercy to one who does not acknowledge it, or tells his secret to one who cannot keep silent. And I perceive that you are not to be changed from your habits, but rather you remain in the nature in which you were born; for if a tree with bitter fruit were completely anointed with honey, it would not for that reason yield sweeter fruit. I have erred until now by staying in your company. For he who dwells with the good receives good from them; but he who stays with the evil may pay the price for it. Just as the wind, when

<margin>From the good one acquires good, and from the evil, evil.</margin>
it blows through cold, rotten things, it receives an evil smell therefrom. But if it blows through noble spices <note>original: "wurtz" - roots or herbs</note> and flowers, it receives therefrom a good and sweet smell. Now I know that you have long been weary of my speech. But the world never stops following its nature: the ignorant hate the irrational <note>or "unreasonable"</note>, and the lowly hate the high-born. The evil hate the good. With this, Kalila stopped speaking with Dimna his brother and rebuking him for the sake of his evil dealings, etc.

<summary>Kalila concludes a fable about a merchant's trickery to illustrate Dimna's betrayal of the King, ultimately deciding to end their association because Dimna's evil nature is unchangeable.</summary>

# The Wisdom of the Ancients

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration shows a lion king wearing a crown and holding a scepter, seated on a bench or throne on the left side. Facing him on the right is a bull (Seneßba). The background depicts a landscape with a river or path leading to a walled city or castle with several towers in the distance.</image-desc>

The Lion regretted that he had struck Seneßba <note>The bull, whose name is derived from the Sanskrit 'Sanjivaka'</note> to death. He only reflected on this deed after his anger was stilled and had passed—what he had done—and he said: "My spirit is sorrowful for Seneßba, for he was a person of wise spirit, high reason, just, and of faithful character; and I know of no cause against me why I should have slain him." And he was displeased with his own actions and mourned. When Dimna <note>The jackal, a manipulative courtier</note> perceived this, he went to the King and said: "Lord King, now God has given you victory over your enemy who sought your life, and yet you are sad?" The Lion answered: "Yes, I regret that I have done this. For I always desired his counsel, and would still desire it; for his high reason, understanding, and noble customs, as well as his pleasant company, were to be praised; and I carry regret and pain for it in my heart." Dimna answered: "By no means should the King speak such words. For you should have no mercy for one who was to be feared. For a pious, sensible King very often takes a dislike to someone and drives them from his court without any mercy, and later takes them back and sets them over his business if he finds them to be pious and just."

<margin>Parable of the Theriac.</margin>
"It is like one who has a distaste for Theriac <note>term: Theriac (original: Tiriack). A famous ancient medical concoction used as an antidote to poison; it was notoriously bitter but life-saving.</note> because of its bitterness, and does not consider its power for health. Likewise, the King may dearly love one and honor him with gifts and exalt him above others. Afterward, he drives him away entirely if he must fear his malice—just as a person whose finger has become poisoned cuts it off and throws it away, fearing that the poison from it might enter his whole body." And when the Lion heard this from Dimna, he believed him and took him back to himself, to listen to the speeches that he held with him daily.

¶ How Dimna raised himself up again at the King's court through his falsehood / and at last received his reward.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts a monarch seated on an ornate throne on the left side of the frame, wearing a crown and holding a scepter. # The interpretation of this chapter, briefly summarized / and to be understood broadly / etc.

<margin>Greatest teaching found in examples.</margin>
From you I have now heard / said King Disles <note>original: "Dißles"; the fictional king to whom the fables are told</note> to Sendebar <note>original: "Sendebar"; the philosopher-sage, often identified with Bidpai</note> his wise man / the fable / and the parable / and through it I have understood / the works and history of Dimna / and the deeds of the Lion / and I marvel at this / how Dimna, a despised / base animal / and one not esteemed among other animals / could provoke the heart of the lion against the Ox / and conversely bring the mind of the Ox to hate the lion. And I find that in these words / and examples there is great teaching / for those / who desire to understand them fundamentally. So that they may learn from this / to protect themselves from deceitful people / and not to place trust / where trust is not kept / and to cultivate people who are of good conduct / and faithful counsel / and true words. Conversely, to destroy the unfaithful / and the double-tongued. Through this, the King gains fruitful counsel / peace / and rest in his kingdom.

XXXV.
->The Wisdom of the Ancients.<-

# The Third Chapter: Concerning Dimna, how his
## deeds came to light, and how he was punished for them, and
### what happens to one, and how he is punished, who
### seeks his own profit by harming another.

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial 'B' at the start of the first paragraph.</image-desc>
**B**odily grief and adversity arose from the false deception of contrived words. "That I have well understood," said Disles the King to Sendebar <note>Sendebar refers to Bidpai, the legendary narrator of these fables</note> his wise man, "in the fables that you have told me, up until the lion struck Seneßba <note>The ox, known as Shanzabeh in the original Sanskrit/Arabic versions</note> dead.

Now reveal to me how Dimna’s case transpired, until the time that the lion also ordered him to be killed." Sendebar answered: "Lord King, it is said that after the lion had killed Seneßba, and after several days had passed, he deeply regretted that he had done such a thing so quickly and without good forethought, and that he had not questioned him or allowed him to come before him. For he was mindful of his good, faithful counsel, his intellect, and his noble, measured conduct. And therefore the lion took to sitting much and dwelling in pleasant business, through which he might forget this displeasure and turn it into joy."

<margin>It is impossible that murder and lies do not come to light at last.</margin>
Now among his servants there was one, the Leopard; and because he was the oldest and the most noble among the King's other servants, the King took him into his special and secret counsel in his chamber. He took counsel from him every day and handled secret matters with him, keeping him constantly by his side. It happened at one time that this Leopard was walking through the house at night where Dimna and Kellila <note>The two jackals, Kalila and Dimna</note> lived, and he heard the words of Kellila, how he rebuked Dimna for the great treachery he had committed against the King, with which he had incited and brought him to the slaying of Seneßba. For Kellila knew the whole business that Dimna his brother had practiced against Seneßba without any guilt, and he reproached him, saying that he would never escape the lion unpunished; for it would be impossible that such a murder, which he had driven through his lies and plots, should not at last be revealed and that he should not receive his reward. To this Dimna answered, "No. It has now come so far that it is no longer possible for it to be revealed; therefore, one must henceforth keep silent about these words and seek ways how one might bring the lion's mind back from his sadness and not reflect upon it further. For I myself have regretted—or it has at least touched me—that I was a cause of it, and yet my desire forced me to it." The Leopard heard all of this and went to the lion's mother and told her what he had heard from these two; she promised him to tell no living person.

<margin>Regret after a deed is done helps no more.</margin>
Early the next morning, the lion's mother rose and went to her son, and when she found him sad, she perceived that it was for no other reason than that he had killed Seneßba without cause, and she spoke to him: "Know, my son, that your regret and sighing cannot give back to you what you have lost; rather, they sicken your body and your spirit and cause you to lose your reason. Therefore, reveal to me what is the cause of your affliction. If it is a matter for which it is right to mourn, know that there is no one in your court who does not have compassion for you, and [would help] if it could be remedied with his diligence. If it is only because you killed Seneßba, then it is mani—"

evident to us all that you have done him wrong, and that he died an innocent death. Had you mastered your angry will at that hour and considered the sorrow and pain that you now have because of it, then the truth would have been revealed to you. For the wise say: whoever hates a thing, their soul also feels a terror or a shunning of it. Now tell me, how was your spirit toward **Senesba** <note>The ox, whose name in the original Sanskrit *Panchatantra* is Sanjivaka</note> before you killed him, and how was it afterward? The **Lion** answered: I have now considered this story much in my heart, and in my mind I have weighed the cause against him, and I can find none; therefore I repent of it, and it brings me resentment and mourning. For all the rational members of my court hold it to be true that **Senesba** died innocently, and that whatever was said against him was a deception. Also, that he was of a righteous and simple heart. Yet I was provoked against him by the grim **Dimna** <note>The jackal, known as Damanaka in the Sanskrit source</note>—I mean with lies and with roguery—and he told me things of him that I now believe **Senesba** never took into his heart to do. But **Mother**, I perceive that you have heard something; therefore tell me, what is it? Then the **Mother** answered: I was told by a faithful friend how **Dimna** acted against **Senesba** with great falsehood, and how he led you astray and turned your heart. This was for the reason that he hated **Senesba** because of the honor you bestowed upon him, and because you were so gracious to him. Then the **Lion** spoke: **Mother**, who told you this? The **Mother** answered: He who told me this forbade me to name him. When a secret is entrusted to someone, he should be faithful in that which is entrusted to him. For he betrays his friend who tells it to another. And he gains thereby an evil reputation, so that he is not to be trusted further, and no matter can be hidden behind him anymore.
