### The Wisdom of the Ancients. XXXVI.

<margin>One should not conceal the truth.</margin>
<margin>Shedding blood is a heavy burden before God.</margin>
<margin>He who reveals a secret is rightly trusted with no more.</margin>
"Lady Mother," spoke the lion, "you speak the truth, and it is for the sake of your friends that you report this. Yet no one should conceal the truth from their friends, but rather reveal it, so that innocent blood may be avenged. For he who helps to hide the guilty becomes a partner to the perpetrator; the King should also do no one injustice. I will not torture or judge anyone based solely on your word until I learn the truth, for shedding blood is a heavy burden before God. If I have done evil to Senesba <note>The ox, whose name is often rendered as Shanzabeh in the original Sanskrit/Persian versions of these fables.</note> by killing him without guilt, that is my regret; and the same shall not happen again from me against Dimna <note>The jackal whose schemes led to the ox's death.</note> without witnesses and the discovery of the truth. And I see that you know the one who told you this, for you incur guilt by suppressing it."

The mother answered, "You have judged rightly. But I wish that you would not doubt the things I told you, and that my word alone should be sufficient testimony."

The King spoke, "I consider you to be nothing other than loyal and truthful toward me. But I desire that you reveal the one who told you this; thereby my mind would be more at peace."

The mother answered, "If I am esteemed as loyal and just in your eyes, then punish this grim deceiver <note>Referring to Dimna.</note> just as you punished the other innocent one."

The lion spoke, "No harm can or shall come to you from revealing this to me. However, I would rightly be esteemed as base in the eyes of the one who revealed his secret to me if I betrayed him, and henceforth no secret would rightly be entrusted to me."

And when the lion heard this and perceived that his mother would not reveal it at all, he let her depart from him in a friendly manner. Early the next morning, the King commanded all his Elders and the Wise Men of his people from his entire host to come before him, and he sent for his Mother and for Dimna. Recognize the malice of your deed and take fear of it; understand the sin of your chatter, for you shall not escape without great vengeance. Dimna spoke: "He who sins, yet loves pious people, does not distance himself from wrongdoing, even if he were able to do so." The Lioness answered: "You traitor and rebel! How are you so daring as to speak such things before the King? For I marvel that he has let you live until this day." Then Dimna answered: "This one whom you name is a man to whom good and just counsel belongs and is fitting, so that his enemies fall into his hands—of which I am none. Consider what has happened to men since then from following the advice of women." Then the Lion’s mother spoke: "O you false and untruthful one! Do you intend to escape from such evil as you have done with the lies you brought before the King?" Dimna answered: "He who claims that which is not so, justly suffers that which you have judged. For I have spoken and confirmed the truth, and brought it before the King, and thereby fulfilled my oath <term>glüpt</term> <note>original: "glüpt"; a formal vow or legal oath of loyalty</note>. And in my heart, I never thought to do wrong or to speak lies. And I will hope that my loyalty shall yet be revealed, and my righteousness witnessed, and the innocence of my hands and my simplicity shall come to the light." And when she saw the Lion say absolutely nothing in response, or give any answer to Dimna’s words, she thought in her mind: "Perhaps everything said against Dimna is false and lied, and he is perhaps innocent. For whoever defends himself so openly and so defiantly before the King and his people, and no one speaks against him, it is to be thought that he speaks the truth. For silence in one's own affairs is a form of consent <note>A variation of the legal maxim "Silence gives consent"</note>, and the Wise say..."

sen. He who is silent, confirms; and the lioness stood up and went away from the lion full of anger. Then the lion commanded that chains be placed around Dimna's neck, and that he should be locked in a dungeon, and therein the matter should be properly investigated and sought out with the greatest precision, and then reported back to him; and so he was led into the dungeon.

### ¶ How the lioness warns her son, the King, against the traitor Dimna, that he should not turn to his words.

Afterward, the mother spoke to the lion, her son, and said: "Things have now been told to me concerning this shameful traitor, the likes of which I have never heard before. And the tongues of all the court attendants are in agreement on this, yet he still attempts to pervert it. And his deed is not secret from any sensible person of this court; moreover, it was told to me secretly and hidden behind my back by one who heard his malice and his deception. And if you grant him favor, he will overcome you with his false cunning. And he who told me this is a trustworthy man. And I advise you faithfully that you do not turn to his words. Rather, separate him from you, so that you set your people at peace." The lion answered and said: "Mother, be at peace; I will investigate his matter with the greatest precision, for truly he is wise and prudent. Therefore, I will be diligent in questioning this story and look into it earnestly, and not be negligent therein, so as not to burden my soul, to satisfy all those who have doubts about this matter—whether it be true or placed upon him through enmity. Therefore, show me: who is the pious, truthful man you mean, and tell me his name? For sometimes one hates another and desires the other's position." His mother answered him: "Know that this pious and <term>scholar</term><note>original: "gschrifft weiser" - literally one wise in the scriptures or writings.</note> who told me this secret is the Leopard, your private chamberlain." The lion said: "That is enough; you shall see how my proceedings against him shall happen; whereupon, go your way to your chamber."

### ¶ How Kalila came to his brother before the prison.

Now when Kalila heard that his brother Dimna had come into imprisonment, he came out of pity because of the daily dwelling together, brotherhood, and companionship they had shared daily. And he went secretly to the dungeon at midnight; and when he saw him bound in iron chains and imprisoned so very harshly, he began to weep bitterly and said to him: "You have done a thing for which I must punish you. Do you still remember that which I previously told and advised you? And besides, I would have gladly directed you to the right path of justice, but there was no following it; you only wished to follow your high-mindedness and your own purpose. Now it is said: the unjust man dies before the just time—not always that his life is taken from him, but rather when he is held painfully in such imprisonment."

# The Book of Wisdom

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting an ox or bull trapped behind wooden bars in a small building or enclosure on the left. On the right, a jackal (likely Dimna) stands in a landscape. In the background, there is a fortified town or castle and several trees.</image-desc>

¶ Since you are now imprisoned, where a swift death would be better for a man, [Kellila] spoke: "Woe to your intellect and wisdom, which have caused your fall. For now they have led you to the very door of death, and of you it is rightly said: 'Who takes from the wise man his reason, and gives to the fool wise words?'" Dimna answered him: "I know, brother, from the time that you have known me, you have never failed to give me good counsel, and you have not hidden the truth from me, and you gladly moved me toward the right path and showed it to me. I did not accept your faithful advice due to the desire for wickedness, which overcame me because I gladly desired a higher status in such a dignified state that was too much for me. Therefore, whoever is wounded with the wound of pride <term>hoffart</term> <note>The Middle High German 'hoffart' refers to excessive pride, vanity, or arrogance, often considered the root of all sins in medieval literature.</note>, his end will be nothing but the regret of his works. And now the hour that I have feared has come to me. But what more can I do against the pride and envious desire of my heart, which has broken the senses of many a righteous man, and has falsified righteous judgments, and perverted the wisdom of the wise? Just like the sick man who knows his illness, yet seeks only to please himself through excessive desire for unhealthy food which increases his sickness, and yet he will not resist that same desire until his illness is so increased that he dies from it. Now I am concerned not only for myself, but also for you—that through our companionship, brotherhood, and daily living together, you also might be seized and led to the painful experience of imprisonment and forced to reveal my secrets. And that I, in whom they place their trust, might be killed, and that you, after my death, might not be released from the court."

# The Wisdom of the Ancients. XLII.

Then Kellila answered: "Now I recognize that a person rarely changes their wicked life for a blessed state, except when they fall into temptation and tribulation. Then they are moved to realize their faults—and sometimes more than they have actually done—so that their suffering might be shorter. Now, truly, I will go my way, so that no one from the court finds me with you. And I beg you to follow my advice: confess your evil deed and your dealings, for you are as good as dead anyway. Truly, it is better that you have pain and suffering here for your guilt than to have such things saved for the future eternal punishment." Dimna answered and said: "You are right, Kellila, and you have given me useful advice. But first I want to hear the words of the Lion and wait to see what he intends to do with me." So Kellila departed from him sadly to his house, fearing that Dimna’s malice might also be sought out in him; and in such fear, adversity, and distress <note>original: "schertzen" - here meaning painful trouble or affliction rather than a joke</note>, he was so severely overwhelmed that he died that very night.

## ¶ How Dimna was brought before the court.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing a judicial assembly in the animal kingdom. A lion sits atop a rocky throne in the center, holding a scepter as a judge. Now, near the dungeon in another cell, there was a wolf in captivity who heard the words of both—everything they had said and how one had answered the other—and he kept this precisely in his memory. Early in the day when Dimna had been placed in the dungeon, the Lion’s mother rose up and went to her son and said: "Remember the words that you promised me regarding the matter of the traitor, and what he has practiced against you and to the great shame of your court. For I know of no greater merit that one could earn in these times than to rid this world of such an evil creature; for it is written: 'He who assists the wicked in their works, he is one of them...'"

<meta>This page continues the legal proceedings following the death of the ox Senesba, focusing on the trial of the jackal Dimna for his deceitful role in the tragedy.</meta>

<header>The Book of Wisdom</header>

<note>The "Book of Wisdom" here refers to the German translation of *Kalila and Dimna*, a famous collection of animal fables used to teach political ethics.</note>

participated. Thereupon the Lion commanded the Leopard, and the Judge, and all his councilors before him, that they should sit in judgment, and summon and hear Dimna on behalf of himself and all the court attendants, and take heed of all his words—what was said against him and what answer he would give thereto—and they were to write this down so that they might report it to him precisely, and let no one be deceived therein. Also, they were to show favor to no man's face <note>original: "nyemants antlit darumb ansehen" - an idiom meaning to show no partiality or bias based on status</note>, but rather that all things should be accomplished according to justice. So the Judge and the Councilors went out and sat in judgment in the presence of all the people, and Dimna was brought before the court, bound with two chains around his neck.

### ¶ The Accusation and Dimna's Answer.

Then the Leopard began and spoke: "You men of the King's court, listen. From the day that the King slew Senesba <note>The ox who was the Lion's friend, killed due to Dimna's lies</note>, he has been sorrowful and troubled until now. For that deed happened without guilt, through the craft and seduction of Dimna, and through the hatred that Dimna bore toward Senesba because of the honor and dignity that Senesba had earned from the King. Now, whoever among you knows anything of these matters or has heard of them, let him tell us upon his obligation, so that we may bring it further to the King according to his desire. For it is by no means the King's intention that he should wish to kill anyone else except after sufficient investigation, and that the matter be carried out according to the discovery of the facts and not according to his own impulse." To this the Judge spoke: "Look up and understand well all that the law-wise Lord Leopard has spoken and prepared with you. None of you shall withhold anything therein that is known to him, for many reasons. First, so that none of you shall deem the judgment that will be given—be it bad or good—to be unjust. Even if you do not wish to speak of what is known to you, yet the testimony of the truth, even if it is painful <note>original: "lügel" - likely a variant of 'leid' or 'lüge', here implying the sting of truth</note>, is good for this: that the King kills no one without cause. Secondly, when the evildoer is punished according to his deed, it brings terror to others who hear of it, to guard themselves against the like, and it benefits the Realm. Thirdly, when a traitor or a betrayer, who handles his affairs through lies, is removed from the world, it is a great peace for the people. For the company of a traitorous or lying person brings great confusion and displeasure to the people. Therefore, you must not remain silent about the truth here." When the people heard this, each looked at the other and remained silent. Then Dimna spoke: "Why are you silent? Let each one say what he knows, and do not worry that you will cause me displeasure thereby. For had I sinned as I am accused, I would rejoice in your silence. But I know myself to be innocent. Therefore, say what you know, and be warned therein: for I will speak to each man's case that which is true. For perhaps each man fancies he has seen what he has not seen, or heard what he has not heard; and to him it might happen as it did to a foolish physician." The Judge and the Leopard spoke: "How did it happen to him?" Dimna answered:

# XLI.
## The Wisdom of the Ancients.

¶ Let no man take up a matter of which he knows no foundation, lest it happen to him as it did to the physician who brought poison instead of medicine.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting three scenes in one room. On the left, a king wearing a crown and holding a scepter sits on a throne. In the center, a woman lies in a bed, appearing ill, while an attendant or nurse stands behind her. On the right, a man in a long robe and hood, possibly a doctor or a monk, stands gesturing with his right hand and holding a rosary in his left.</image-desc>

<margin>God grants grace for a pious life.</margin>
For it is said, there was in a city in India a very practiced and learned physician who was held in great affection. God, through his pious life, had granted him grace so that he made all the sick healthy through his art and the help of God. When he died, his successors looked into his books and studied them, so that they might take good teachings from them. At one time, a man came into that same land who gave himself out to be a physician, and yet was none. Now the King of the land had a daughter who was dearer to him than all other children; she was great with child, and as the time of birth drew near, a significant illness befell her, namely, the <term>red harm</term> <note>original: "rhot schad"; likely referring to dysentery or a severe fever accompanied by a rash</note>. The King soon commanded that a messenger be sent to a physician known to him, to come to the aid of his daughter. When the messenger came to him, the physician was blind; and since he could not come, he gave the messenger a writing stating that the daughter should be given a certain drink. The King ordered that a physician be sought who could <term>prepare</term> <note>original: "conficieren"; to compound or mix a medical prescription</note> such a drink. By misfortune, they came upon the one who claimed to be a physician, who spoke to the King, saying he was a master at making it. He ordered the jars to be set before him from the <term>apothecary</term> <note>original: "Apotecken"; a pharmacy or storehouse of medicinal ingredients</note> that had belonged to the good, proven physician who was now dead. And when these were set before him, he seized a small flask in which poison was enclosed, and took from it, and mixed it among other medicines which he had taken from other jars, and gave it to the King, and said: "This is the medicine for your daughter." And when the King saw the quickness of the physician, he thought that this was a proven doctor, and honored him with...

<summary>A cautionary tale about a fraudulent doctor in India who, lacking true medical knowledge, accidentally mixes poison into a remedy for the King's ailing daughter.</summary>
<keywords>physician, India, medicine, poison, King's daughter, red harm, apothecary, fraud, medical ethics</keywords>

giving a significant amount of money and clothing; and he took the medicine and gave it to his daughter. As soon as she had drunk a portion of it, she immediately swelled up so greatly that she died instantly. When the King discovered this, he commanded that the man be seized and that he be given the remainder of the drink to consume. And when he drank it, the same happened to him as had happened to the daughter, and he died. I have told this parable so that none of you may say that he had no knowledge of it, or that he wished to do a favor for others. And yet it is true that every man is persuaded by his own heart to believe the words of a recognized, learned physician <note>original: "gelerten artzet"</note> more than himself. Now, I am indeed innocent of these things which are charged against me, and yet I am in your hands. But love God according to your ability.

### ¶ How the master of the kitchen <note>The "kuchenmeyster" or Master of the Household/Chief Cook, often a high-ranking official in medieval courts.</note> pointed out the traitorous signs in Dimna, and how Dimna gave him an answer.

The King's master of the kitchen heard this matter and surrendered his office <note>stepping forward from his duties to testify</note>, speaking before the others: "Listen, all of this assembly, and understand what I shall say to you. The wise have not neglected to write concerning the signs of the good and the evil; rather, they have explained it all. Now, the signs of malice and falsehood appear most strikingly on this evildoer, matching the evil reputation that has fallen upon him." The Judge spoke: "We have heard your words, but there are few who understand this. Therefore tell us: what have you seen?" The master of the kitchen answered before all the people.

<margin>
Signs on the
body of
traitors.
</margin>
In the books of the wise, one finds: he whose left eye is smaller than the other and moves frequently, and whose nose is curved toward his right side, and who has a wide space between the eyebrows, and whose eyebrows are thick and long, and who dwells among people with a bowed head and looks behind himself often, and is moreover haughty—that man is deceitful, unfaithful, and traitorous. All of this was found in Dimna. Dimna answered and spoke: "It is true that all things signify one another,

<margin>
The judgment
of God is
alone
truthful.
</margin>
but the judgment of God <note>original: "vrtheyl Gottes"</note> is alone truthful over His creatures, and He is righteous in whom there is no falsehood nor untruth, and in whom there is no evil. But all of us who are under heaven stand in God's power." And he said: "You men of wisdom and reason, hear me, for this man might imagine that no one else knows how to speak but he; and one should give an answer to the fool and reveal his folly to him, so that he does not consider himself wise. For whether it be good or evil that happens through a man, if that were judged solely by the signs he had on his body, then it would now be evident that no one could be esteemed as pious for serving God, nor could anyone be blamed or earn merit except according to the signs on his body. Thus, the sinner would not be punished for his injustice, for it would not be in his own will or power, but in the power of the signs which make men blessed or damned. And so men would be forced to do good or evil, and accordingly, I would be forced..."

¶ Let no one mock another, if he himself is worthy of mockery, lest it happen to him as it did to the shepherd's wives.

Dimna answered: A city was once won by its enemies, and when they had slain all the men of that city, they divided the women among themselves. To this the husband gave her answer and spoke: "Shame be upon you! For you leave your own body uncovered, yet you mock her who would gladly hide her shame."

### ¶ How the master of the kitchen pointed out the treacherous signs on Dimna, and how Dimna gave him answer.

<margin>Signs on the body of traitors.</margin>
The King’s master of the kitchen <term>kuchenmeyster: the chief steward or head of the royal household</term> heard this matter, and setting aside his duties, he spoke before the others: "Listen, all of this assembly, and understand what I shall tell you. # The Book of Wisdom

I wonder greatly why your heart has been moved to speak, seeing as you find within your own person the very things you imagine you have seen in me. Your body is so unclean and your works so evil and unvirtuous. It is not fitting for you to hold office in the King’s kitchen, nor to touch his food with your unclean hands and body. I, and others, have known of your impurity long enough, though I kept it to myself for the sake of your honor. But now, since your hatred toward me has been revealed—in that you have spoken against me with lies and falsehood regarding matters unknown to you—I tell you that you are not worthy to serve the King in any business, nor to dwell in his court, and especially not near his food. The Master Cook answered: "Dimna, are you speaking these words to me?" Dimna said: "Yes, I say this to you. For much evil is gathered within you; you limp, you are broken, your hands are scarred, and you have a scabby rash upon your <term>genitals</term><note>original: "gmecht"; a Middle High German term for the private parts</note>, and you scratch yourself there at all times. Your skin is full of scales, and it would be only right that you should no longer go about this office for the King."

### ¶ How many a righteous man’s speech is driven back by an unrighteous man through courtly, false talk.

When the Master Cook heard what Dimna had spoken against him, his face became distorted, his tongue hung out of his throat, and he began to weep because Dimna had slandered him so before the people. When Dimna saw that he wept, he said to him: "It is right that you weep and are sorrowful day and night. For if the King knew what was in you, he would cast you far from him, even to the ends of his land." All of this was written down by the public scribe of the court, in whom the King had special trust, exactly as it had been spoken. And when this was brought before the King, he ordered that same Master Cook to be dismissed from his office and from the court, and sent away. When this was done and the recorded things were sealed, Dimna was led back into the dungeon, and everyone departed for the day, each to his own dwelling, etc.

## The Wisdom of the Ancients

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a scene from a fable. Now, among the King’s servants there was one named Rozbah <note>original: "Reßba"</note>; he was a kinsman <term>geſippter: a blood relative or close family member</term> of Kalila, and held an honorable position at the King’s court. He went to Dimna at the prison and told him that his brother Kalila was dead. When Dimna heard this, he suffered great sorrow, and his pain was doubled; he wept sincerely and said: "Woe to my life! For I am now cut off from my brother, and from all the delight and hope of my life. Now he spoke truly who said: 'He who is struck by one adversity shall thereafter encounter unpleasant things at all times.' Just as now so much misery has fallen upon my neck: the confinement of the prison, and that all the people stand against me and give all their counsel toward my death. I am abandoned and robbed of all my friends and brothers, and all those who ever gave me good advice. But now I praise God that my brother has left you, his friend, behind for me, through whom the fall and death of my brother Kalila may be comforted. I trust in the mercy of God, for I have noted that you show earnestness in your dealings, and that you have turned to me in loyalty to take the place of my brother. Now, we two had brought together a sum of money <term>barſchafft: liquid assets or cash</term> and kept it in a secret place for our necessity, should we have need of it; and it seems good to me that you should fetch it from that place." Rozbah hurried to the place and found it. And when he had gathered it all together, he brought it before Dimna, who took a portion of it. The other portion he gave to Rozbah and said to him: "Prove and strengthen your heart in goodness toward me and in loyalty, and help me to bring my case to a good conclusion. And also take heed of what the King shall say when he hears the transcript <term>auffſchreibung: the written record of the legal proceedings or testimony</term>, and what he might say in response to my words, or what demeanor he might have."

# The Book of Wisdom

This woman had intelligence, and she noticed that this situation had been orchestrated against her by the house servant <note>German: "haußknecht"; refers to a domestic steward or high-ranking servant</note>, and she spoke: "Sir, investigate this matter thoroughly, and you will find this liar full of falsehood. First, ask the pilgrims—who understand the speech of birds and how to talk with them—whether they know any other words in that language. For I perceive that your unfaithful servant, who wished to satisfy his lust with me, has taught the birds this. And when I would not grant him his desire, he pushed this blame onto me." The merchant entreated the men to speak with the birds; but whatever they said to them, the birds knew no other words than those they had spoken before. By this, the man recognized that the servant was at fault, and he summoned the servant. To him the woman spoke: "Woe to you, you rogue! How you wished to make me unfaithful in the eyes of my husband." The servant confessed his guilt, and the master ordered him to be put to death because he had given testimony through the birds—whom he had trained—concerning things he had never seen nor heard. I have told you this fable <note>The text uses "fabel" to denote a moralizing animal story or parable</note> so that whoever does as this man did shall rightly receive his reward for it. After this speech, the judge again had written down what had been said to Dimna, along with his answer and his rebuttal. Then Dimna was led back into the dungeon, and he spoke much behind his back against the judge as he was led from the court, but his words were heard no further. The judge and the others all went into the King’s court. There the lioness spoke again to her son: "If you let Dimna live, even though he has done this, he will yet bring about much evil, by which he will bring downfall to you and the people, against which you will not be able to protect yourselves."

# The Wisdom of the Ancients. XLVI.

Now it came to pass, as the lioness repeated these words daily to her son, that the King <note>The Lion</note> took them to heart and discovered how she had learned of the matter. The Leopard, who had overheard the conversation between Kellila and Dimna while passing by their house, was brought forward. The Wolf was also sent for; he had been lying in the dungeon next to Dimna and heard what Dimna had said, how Kellila had answered him, and how Kellila had rebuked him. The Wolf gave testimony to this. Thereupon the Lion placed his belief in these accounts and recognized that Dimna had led him astray against Senesba <note>The ox, whom the Lion had killed due to Dimna's lies</note>. He commanded that Dimna be brought before him and, in the presence of the witnesses and the people, be beaten to death; and so it was done. # The Fourth Chapter / concerning the Dove / the Mouse / the Raven / the Tortoise / and the Stag / and it is about faithful companionship.

After this, King Discles <note>original: "Diskles"; likely a corruption of the name Dabschelim</note> spoke to Sendebar <note>original: "Sendebar"; the philosopher-sage Bidpai</note>, his wise man. Now tell me of the pious people who dwell together and gain love for one another—how their love and friendship may be strengthened, and how one shows goodness to the other in faithful companionship. Sendebar answered the King: A wise, understanding person is a helper to his friends. of a raven. Once upon a time, the raven sat in his nest and saw a huntsman approaching the tree to spread a bird-snare <note>original: "vogel garn"</note>. The raven was terrified by this and thought: "Now you do not know whether the huntsman has prepared this gear for you or for someone else," and he said, "I will stay and watch." In the meantime, the fowler spread seeds upon the earth and set his snare accordingly, and stood and waited behind the net. After a short while, a dove came with a notable flock of doves, of which she was the leader; and when they saw the seeds there and took no heed of the snare, they fell into it and were all covered by the net. When the fowler saw this, he was joyful. Now when the doves found themselves captured, they fluttered to and fro to free themselves. To them spoke the dove, their leader: "Let not each one rely only upon herself, and let none among you hold herself dearer than the others; instead, let us all soar upward together. Perhaps we may lift the snare so that it follows us, that we may fly away with it, and thus each one shall deliver herself and the others with her." They obeyed and hoisted the snare above them into the air and flew away beneath the net. At this, the fowler wondered greatly and followed after them, watching them in the hope that the net would soon press them down to the earth. The raven thought: "You shall follow to see what will become of this wonder." And when the leader of the doves saw the pursuer following, she said to her companions: "Look, the huntsman follows after us to seek us; if we persist in flying on the straight path, we remain in his sight and may at last not escape. But if we fly over mountains and valleys, he cannot keep us in his eyes and will the sooner cease his pursuit, for he will doubt finding us again. Not far from here in a valley is a cave wherein dwells a mouse, my companion and friend; I know that if we come to her, she will gnaw through our net and release us." The doves acted according to the advice of their leader until they were out of the fowler's sight. And when he could no longer see them, he despaired of finding them and turned back on his way. The raven flew slowly behind to see what the end of the story would be—whether they could loosen themselves from the net and by what means—so that he himself might learn from it, should it ever be necessary for him to use such means.

There was a man who had a snake living in his house, which was very well treated by his wife, and her food was prepared for her daily at her usual time. The snake had her dwelling in a hole in the wall, very near to the hearth <note>original: "feürstatt" — the central fireplace or cooking area of the home</note>. The man and the wife believed, according to the custom of the land, that they would have much good fortune from the snake. It happened on a Sunday that the master of the house <term>householder (haußwürt): the male head of a domestic establishment</term> was not feeling strong in his head; therefore, he remained lying in his bed in the morning and commanded his wife, child, and servants to go to the divine service <note>original: "Gotts dienst" — church mass or worship</note>. When they had all gone out and it was quiet in the house, the snake came out of the hole and looked around intently. The man, whose chamber stood open toward the fire, became aware of the snake and her looking about differently than had been her habit. He took heed of this, and when the snake had explored every corner and also looked into the householder's chamber and found no one—for the householder had diligently hidden himself—the snake went to the fire and hung her tail over the pot <note>original: "hafen" — a ceramic cooking pot or jar</note> and let her poison fall into it, and quickly hid herself in her hole. When the householder saw this, he stood up immediately and buried the pot with the food and poison in the earth, so that no one would be poisoned by it. And at the time when it was customary to give the snake her food, the householder stood with

# The Book of Wisdom

### Page XXIV

...enduring things, which belong to those of noble heart; and the love that exists among faithful companions shall never be severed. Even if it should ever cease, it is soon restored; for that which is good is long-lasting.
<margin>A comparison to golden vessels.</margin>
Just like a golden vessel <note>original: "guldenẽ geschirr"</note>, which lasts longer than a fragile glass; and when the glass breaks, it cannot be restored, but when the golden vessel is damaged, it is easily repaired to be as it was before. Thus, a rift among unfaithful company is closer to total severance and far from restoration; but faithful company is far from severance and close to restoration. Therefore, a man
<margin>Like associates with like.</margin>
of noble spirit also loves another noble spirit, once he recognizes it but a single time. But the friendship of one who is of an ignoble spirit exists only for the sake of profit and greed. And I recognize you as being of a noble spirit. Therefore, your company is dear to me, and I have need of it, and I shall not depart from the threshold of your door, nor eat nor drink, until you promise me your companionship.

However, I desire to be of use to all who seek it, according to my ability. Therefore, do not gloat <note>original: "glozier," likely from the Latin 'gloriari' meaning to boast or brag</note> against me, saying: 'Aha, I have found a mouse without any foresight or reason, who could not protect herself from my deception,' lest it happen to me as it did to the rooster with the fox." # Of the Ancient Sages.

### ¶ No one should let praise blind them / lest it happen to them / as it did to the rooster / who was strangled by the fox.

It was in winter time / during a cold night / that a hungry fox went out seeking food / and heard a rooster at his master's house / crowing for the day from a cherry tree <note>original: "krießbaum," a regional term for a wild cherry tree</note>. The fox drew near to the tree / and said: "Rooster, why do you sing in this cold and dark night?" The rooster said: "I am announcing the day / which I recognize by my nature as coming soon." The fox said: "Rooster / then you have something divine within you / that you know of future things." The fox began to dance / and when he was asked by the rooster the reason for his dancing / the fox answered: "When you, wise Philosopher <note>original: "Philosophus"</note>, sing / then it is only right that I should dance / for one should rejoice with those who are joyful. And he said: "O rooster / you prince of all birds / you are not only gifted so that you may fly in the air / but also to proclaim future things just like the Prophets. How nature has gifted you above all other animals! Might I not be worthy of your company? And if you will believe me / then grant me just this once the chance to kiss your wise head / so that I may tell my companions / that I have kissed the head of a Soothsayer <note>original: "Weissagen"</note>." The rooster allowed himself to be swayed by the fox's kind words / and flew down from the tree / and offered the fox his head. The fox seized it in his mouth / and took it for his food / and said: "I have found the Wise One / to be without any reason."

### ¶ How the Raven and the Mouse pledged themselves to loyal companionship.

I have not told you this <note>The mouse is speaking to the raven, referencing the previous story</note> so that the rooster might have considered the enmity / between the fox and him / and that it was the fox's intent / to carry out his will upon him / for he was the food / and the fox the devourer. Therefore, the fox gave him mocking words / because he had not guarded himself against him. But I will trust you / that your words are not spoken with a double tongue / and with that, the mouse went just to the edge of her hole's door. The Raven <note>original: "Rapp," an archaic term for a raven</note> said: "Why do you stand there by the door? What makes you too timid to come out to me? Is there any fear in you toward me?" Then the mouse answered:

<margin>
Two kinds
of friend-
ship.
</margin>
Two kinds of friendship exist among the people of this world. The first / the giving of persons. The second / the giving of the hand. When persons are given / those are the loyal friends / who join themselves together with heart / and love in faithful companionship. But those who join together by the giving of the hand / those are they who keep company / each for his own profit. And they are like a fowler / who throws wheat to his birds / not for their benefit / nor out of pity for their hunger / but for his own profit / to bring them to be food for his body. Therefore, the companionship and gift of the person is better / than the gift and union of the hand / and thus I have now placed my faith in your person / for you have pleased me / and nothing hinders my coming out

Thus the pilgrim <note>original: "waller" - a traveler or pilgrim</note> dug toward my cave until he came to the gold; and when he took it, he spoke to the hermit: "This mouse was not able to spring into the basket so joyfully except by reason of this gold, from which she received such power and spirit to be more capable in her whole being. And I heard these words of the pilgrim and knew that he spoke the truth; and from this, pain fell into my senses and spirit, and I thereafter felt the sluggishness of my practice and the weakness of my strength. In the morning, all the mice—my companions—gathered to me again as they were accustomed to do, to wait for the food from me which I had shared with them every day, and they said: "Now hunger has beset us, and we have no food. Until now, you have been our refuge and confidence, and by you we have lived. Diligently see to it that we may have something for our hunger." I betook myself to the end near the basket and wanted to jump into it according to my habit, but I was not able to do so. And when the mice saw that I could not jump into it and provide them with food, I was completely despised by them and held in contempt by them all; and I heard one among them say: "Now her soul is entirely useless and she can do nothing at all; it is required among us that everyone goes their own way, and it is no longer fitting for us to wait upon her, since we see that she can no longer do what she did before. For truly you will find that it will soon come to the point that she will need our help; therefore it is good to leave her in time, and let everyone consider their own path." And so they all departed from me and left me alone, and they looked upon me no more.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the hardships of poverty and the loss of social standing, following the previous discussion on the fickleness of fortune.</meta>

# Of the Ancient Sages.

When a rich man comes to poverty, he is to be regarded as worse off than a man who has always been poor and in need of others' help; he is like a planted tree whose bark is subsequently stripped away from all sides. For poverty is the beginning of all sadness, pain of the mind, and adversity. It robs the great lords of the land of the bravery of their hearts, preventing them from accomplishing what is in their minds. It misleads the counselor into folly; it deafens the mighty; it takes the speech from the wise, good counsel from the discerning, and wisdom from the elders. It drenches <note>or "stifles"</note> life, it withers the blood, and it makes every adverse accident feel heavy. And whoever is struck by the wound of poverty is robbed of all gentleness and provoked to harshness. For he who is not gentle lacks nobility, and he who lacks nobility is easily led into injustice. He who does injustice sins, and he who sins mourns; he who mourns loses his understanding and forgets wisdom. 

And I found one who became poor, of whom everyone thereafter expected only evil; he was blamed for the deeds that others had done. And there are not as many reasons in the world for which a rich man is praised as there are reasons for which one who has fallen into poverty is scolded. For if he was generous, they say he was a wastrel; if he overlooks something, they say he is incapable. If he is of a noble mind and bountiful, they say he is too cunning and unbearable. If he is of modest behavior, they say he is simple-minded. If he has a needy heart, they say he is a fool. If he is talkative, they say he is a chatterer. If he is silent, they say he is a beast. 

And therefore death is better than poverty, which brings a man into restless temptation and contempt, so that he must beg—especially when he must beg from a fool or a miser, for they shame him in his poverty. It is more fitting for a poor, ruined nobleman to put his hand into a serpent's mouth, take out its poison, and drink it, than to seek help from a fool or a miser. And it is said: he who suffers constant sickness without relief, a wanderer in a foreign land who is welcomed by no one and given nothing but must live without help, and one who has come from riches to poverty—for these three, death is better than life. For to live thus is called being dead. For poverty sometimes brings about theft, murder, betrayal, robbery, and other evils. The Sages say that it is better to be mute and truthful than a speaker and a liar. And it is better to be a silent man than a chatterer, even if he always tells the truth.

<margin>A Maxim:</margin>
And as I stood in a cave where the wanderer <term>waller: a pilgrim or traveler</term> found the gold pieces which he took, I saw that he shared them with the hermit <term>einsidel: a religious recluse</term>, and each took half and they parted from one another. And the hermit laid his portion at night under the pillow whereon he slept. And I thought to take some of it for myself, perhaps so that my spirit and strength might be increased thereby, and that my friends and companions might turn to me again. And so I did not hesitate. And while the hermit slept, I crept there; but he woke at my walking and struck at me with his staff and hit me, the pain of which I felt upon my head...

the Turtle <note>original: "Schildkrött"</note> said: "Deer, if you are thirsty, then drink; here you need fear no one who could harm you." So the Deer <note>original: "Hirz," an archaic form of "Hirsch"</note> went to her and greeted her, and the Turtle asked him from where he had come. And if you wish to dwell here with us, we will share our company with you and pledge ourselves to you, for there is good pasture around this water." The Deer desired their company and stayed with them; and they made him a dwelling in the field from the branches of the trees, where they came together every day for their company and conversed <note>original: "fabulierten," meaning to tell stories or chat</note> there about the affairs of this world.

### ¶ How good, faithful company should assist one another, as follows hereafter.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration showing a stag (Hirz) caught in a rope net on the ground. To the left, a hunting dog leaps toward the trapped animal. On a certain day, the Raven <note>original: "Rapp," an archaic term for "Rabe"</note>, the Mouse, and the Turtle were together, and they missed the Deer. And because he remained away so long, they were afraid that he might have encountered something from the hunter, and they sent out the Raven to fly through the woods to see if he might become aware of him. The Raven was swift and flew to the forest and found the Deer in a net, and he soon returned and told this to his companions. As soon as the Mouse heard this, she hurried and asked the Raven to carry her there; and when she came to the Deer, she spoke: "Brother, who has felled you into this net? You should, after all, be one of the rational and sensible animals." The Deer answered: "Sister, no one can protect themselves against the judgment <note>original: "vrtheyl," referring here to fate or divine decree</note> that [comes] from above..."

And as they were thus speaking with one another, the tortoise also came walking up. The deer spoke to her: "Sister, why have you come here, or how can your presence help us? For only the mouse can gnaw through this snare to set me free. And if the hunter should come, I am light-footed enough to flee, the raven can fly away, and the mouse can easily hide herself in one of the many holes here. But you alone are of a slow gait, and not fit to flee or to hide yourself, and you might perhaps be captured." The tortoise answered: "A rational person in faithful company should not value his own life after abandoning his companions; and where he cannot provide help, he should at least provide comfort according to his ability. For a faithful companion should pull his own heart out of his body for another and lay it down for him; for when good, faithful company is severed, their life is diminished and their eyes are darkened." And while they were still engaged in this talk, the hunter arrived. Now the mouse had just then gnawed through the net; the deer fled, the raven flew to the forest, and the mouse crept into a hole by the root of a tree.

<margin>Fortune makes one wait for the next good thing, but misfortune comes in heaps.</margin>
<margin>A noble spirit is better than any love.</margin>
<margin>Parable of the sore.</margin>
<margin>A maxim.</margin>
In the meantime, the hunter came and found the net gnawed through. At this he was startled and looked around him, yet found no one but the tortoise. He took her and bound her tightly together, which the raven and the mouse saw, and they became very sorrowful. The mouse said to the raven: "I see it no other way; if we escape the net, we fall into the pit, and we go constantly from one mishap into another. The word of him who said this is true: 'While a man experiences good fortune, he waits for the next. But when a mishap comes, he cannot defend himself against the next one.' For the misfortune and the loss of my treasure <note>The mouse previously lost a hoard of gold in an earlier chapter.</note> and my companions would have been enough for me; now I have rescued my companion the deer with terror, and thereby lost my brother the tortoise, who was dearer to me than all my companions and friends. He came here solely through love and faithfulness, and through his noble spirit, which is better than the love of a father for a son; for this love does not end except with death. Woe to this body, which runs from one misery into another, and to which so much adversity is granted. I perceive that no one may remain constantly in one state. Just like the stars: now they shine, then they are faded; now they are surrounded with light, then with darkness. This adversity which I suffer through my companion is like a person's sore <note>original: "geschwer," meaning an abscess or ulcer.</note> which a doctor strikes; then he gains a twofold pain: the pain of the sore and the pain of the blow." The deer and the raven spoke to the mouse: "Your mourning and ours helps the tortoise not at all. Now consider and find schemes for her release. The wise say: in adverse matters you test a friend; in trade, the faithful; in accidental matters...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the conflict between the crows and the owls (referred to here as eagles), a classic fable from the Book of Wisdom, also known as the Fables of Bidpai.</meta>

# The Book of Wisdom

### ¶ How one should always consider how to resist one's enemies with wisdom.

And during one night, the King of the Eagles <note>original: "Aren"; in this context, the text refers to the owls, though the German word "Aar" typically means eagle. In the Bidpai tradition, this is the war between crows and owls.</note> rose up with his people and fell upon the crows, wounding many of them and killing several out of the envy that existed between them. And the King of the Crows did not perceive this raid until the morning when he had risen. And when he saw such damage and the loss of his own, he mourned and thought to take revenge for it. He summoned all the wise and sensible members of his council and revealed this event to them, fearing that such a thing might happen again if it were not prevented. And he spoke to his advisors: ¶ "Take heed of your words and do not hurry in your minds to decide on this counsel until you have sufficiently considered this dealing and matter."

¶ Now there were five of his advisors among them, wiser and more learned than the others. These the King summoned to him and held a private council with them, and he spoke to the first crow: "What is your advice in this matter which has happened to us from this opposition? Especially since we are in fear that it might happen to us again." The crow answered: "Lord King, this is my advice, which the wise have spoken: When something happens to you from your enemy who is of superior power and whom you cannot resist, it is better that you remove yourself from him and give way to him; and your heart should not presume to fight with him."

The King then asked the second crow: "What do you think of this advice that you have just heard?" He answered and said: "This is not good advice that this crow, my friend, has advised. For it is neither praiseworthy nor good that we should leave our dwelling, or that we should first bow beneath their feet. Rather, we want to be bold and ready, and undertake to protect ourselves from our enemies. When they come, let them find us more wary and prepared for defense; we shall defend ourselves against them as long as we are able. And we should have guards and spies in all places, so that whatever they see of the Eagles, they shall announce it to us. And if they come to battle with us, we will march out against them and take our advantage, and truly fight against them. Perhaps God will give us the victory, and then we shall do to them as they have done to us. And I will never advise that we flee at the start and leave behind us wives, children, servants, all our goods, and the pleasant dwelling which our ancestors have long possessed. And should they overcome us, then we shall flee when we see that we can do nothing against them."

Furthermore, the King spoke to the third crow: "What do you think of the advice of your two companions?" He answered: "I have understood them both, and both speak well, each in his own opinion. But how can we stand to fight before them after this event that has happened to us? There is no better advice in me than this: that we send one of us who is understanding and sensible, who may approach them and learn their words—what their intention is—and report that back to us. And if they wish to come to terms with us and take a yearly tribute from us, then we may lament the past damage and settle ourselves therein, so that we..."

For the wise say: When a King encounters harm from his enemies which he cannot withstand, and he fears for his people and his land, it is better to accept peace and pay tribute <note>tribute: a tax or payment made by one ruler to another as a sign of submission</note> so that the war [ends]; and with this advice you should hurry and not delay. <-

The King further asked the fourth raven what he thought of these speeches. He answered: "They have said nothing upon which the King may build a foundation. It does not please me that we should so despise our persons and noble nature and venture ourselves against those foolish, coarse people <note>referring to the Owls</note> or fight with them. Also, regarding the suggestion that we should give them tribute: it is better that we withdraw for a time from our dwelling into another land where we are safe, until God wills to ordain these things differently. For the wise say: He who makes himself forced and subject to his enemies helps them against himself. For I know that as soon as they perceive our spirit—that we are willing to pay tribute—they would treat us so that it would be unbearable for us, and bringing this [offer] would be betraying ourselves; it is proper that we be cautious and warned in this matter. If they should come, let us hasten against them and fight them in good hope; perhaps God will be merciful to us so that we do not all perish."

Thereafter the King asked the fifth raven what he wished to say to this. The raven spoke: "It seems to me that all these speeches lack a foundation that would be useful to the King and his people. For I cannot advise the King that we go to battle with those who are too mighty and too strong for us. For whoever does not consider his own power and reason, and does not recognize his enemy who is mightier than he, yet wishes to wage war with him, will be deceived with loss; for I fear the Eagles <note>original: "Aren" - an archaic term for eagles, here likely used as a metaphor for the powerful Owls</note>. For no one should despise his enemy, even if he is not strong or wise. And yet, it also does not please me to accept such a peace, but rather that we in no way make ourselves subject or tributary. For if we should send to them to seek peace, despite the wantonness they have inflicted upon you, Lord King, and your people, we will become frivolous in their eyes and shame ourselves. For the wise say: One should have little to do with enemies and not draw too near to them, except with an advantage <term>vortheyl: a strategic advantage or superior position</term>. He knows then how to overcome his enemy, which is not the case for us, as we are not as mighty as they. Therefore, it is proper to deal with them with advantageous designs and good words until we may fulfill our desire. Just as a man who has a wicked wife: he may bring her to his will sooner with good words and flattering gestures than with blows. And therefore, Lord King, I cannot advise that we should fight against the enemy who is stronger than we. We should also not let it be noticed that we fear them. But this is my advice: that we be ready and warned for battle, and deceive them with a stratagem. For the wise man should see his harm and guard against it before it comes. For when it comes, it cannot be recalled. And he who fights much may escape with difficulty; but one may prevent war

# The Book of Wisdom

### ¶ How a prince should follow his advisors / and how secrets should be kept.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration showing a meeting between birds. On the left, several ravens are perched on the branches of a bare, gnarled tree. On the right, a large crowned bird, representing a king, stands on a rocky pedestal or outcrop. In the foreground, a swan swims in a body of water. <margin>A wise King should follow his advisors.</margin>
The King spoke: "It seems good to you not to fight. With what intention, plans, or designs do you think they should be sought?" Then the raven answered and spoke: "Lord King, hear my advice. When a King conducts his affairs and business according to the counsel of his wise men—in whom he recognizes wisdom and who understand all matters, whether they be great or small—then he may triumph over his enemies, and his kingdom and power shall endure well, and he shall be strengthened and increased. But if the King chooses to disdain his advisors and follow his own will and purpose—even if he is already wise himself—he may seldom come to a victorious end in his affairs, and his kingdom will be weakened thereby. The King should also have the counsel of all the wise men in weighty matters until a unanimous or majority decision is reached. But he should not take counsel from one whom he knows does not love him; for even if such a person gives good advice once, he will not do so always. Now, my advice is this: First, that we abandon all fear and strengthen our spirits so that we do not become subject to them <note>referring to the enemy</note>, for that would be an eternal shame to us and our children."

<margin>A Maxim.</margin>
Now the wise men say: It is better to have died honorably than to have lived shamefully and in adversity.

<margin>A secret that enters the mouths of more than two people becomes public knowledge.</margin>
"However, Lord King, I have something secret to tell you in these matters that no one else should hear. For a secret, once it passes into the mouths of more than two people, becomes public. Therefore, the King should be silent regarding his secret counsels; through this, his affairs and undertakings are strengthened, and he thereby achieves his..."

Since we have chosen the Eagle <note>original: "Aren"</note> to be our King. To this the

I say this: even if all the species of noble and good birds were lost,

other kinds of birds remaining, even then we should not take a King from the 
Eagles, for he carries evil customs. He is also of an evil color, of a per-

merciful. For he is of a lineage of evil mind and wicked works, and 
of a treacherous heart, and many other malices he has within him, which I will now 
pass over for the sake of brevity. But I say to you: choose for yourselves no 
King from that lineage, but seek for yourselves among other fowl; and though 
they be simpler in mind and in works, yet that same 
simplicity is better for you than this cunning. For if a King is a total fool 
and yet were compliant, having wise counsels and pious companions, then his 
affairs proceed well, and his kingdom shall be exalted, just like the King of the Hares. Although 
he was not wise, yet he was compliant to his wise counsels, and that 
turned out well for him. The birds all spoke: What did he do then? The

**M**an says that there once came a great famine <note>original: "theiirung," referring to a period of scarcity or high prices</note>, and

Moon, and there was pasture and water to their heart's desire. When they heard that, they 
set out and moved there. And when they came to the well, 
the hares were housed there with their King, and had many burrows around it for 
their comfort. And as the elephants walked about there, they stepped on many of the 
burrows, crushing them together, and trampled with their feet many of the hares who 
were inside. When the hares discovered such damage, they gathered before 
their King and complained of it to him, and were sad to leave their dwelling, 
and asked their King for counsel. He called to him all his wise counselors and spoke: 
"I confess that I do not have the wisdom that is necessary and 
needful for you and my kingdom. Therefore I wish to follow your counsel, as the wise ones. 
Therefore, counsel faithfully in this matter for your good and mine." 
To this spoke an old hare, who was esteemed as wise and learned: "If it pleases the 
King to send me to the King of the elephants, and with me one 
whom you also trust, who may listen to what I negotiate and say, and [report] that to the King..."

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the birds' assembly to elect a king, specifically focusing on the crow's intervention against the owl and the resulting enmity between their species.</meta>

# The Book of Wisdom

<margin>
It is better to lose a case with the truth than to win it with lies.
</margin>
"And I am old, dear friends; my eyes have become dim and my ears hear poorly. Come closer and raise your voices so that I may understand your words." They went closer to him and recounted their case. And when he heard them—for they trusted him—he spoke to them: "I have understood you and will give you counsel and instruct you in a path of righteousness <note>original: "gerechtigkeit"</note> so that I may give a just judgment. It is proper to seek the truth of your case. For it is better to lose a case with the truth than to win it falsely with untruth. For there is nothing in this world that a person carries with them into the future world except their works, for the salvation or damnation of their soul, and whether one grants their neighbor good or begrudges them evil." And when they heard these good words from the judge, they had faith in him and went to him in his chamber to receive his decision. There, the marten <note>original: "maußhund," literally "mouse-dog," a term for a marten or weasel</note> seized them both and ate them.

<image-desc>A horizontal ornamental woodcut divider depicting a central handle or architectural element flanked by two grotesque mask-like faces with open mouths and stylized acanthus leaves.</image-desc>

->¶ No one should boast of their wise words,<-
->lest it happen to them as it did to the crow,<-
->who died of sorrow because of it.<-

"I have told you such a fable so you may know that an unfaithful person, and one who sustains himself through malice, is not to be trusted. Since he and his ancestors have behaved like a wolf among the animals, he is not to be trusted. And you should not elect him as a king for any reason." And when all the birds heard this together, nothing came of the election, and they did not want to have the owl <note>original: "Aren," here referring to the Eagle or a large bird of prey, but contextually the candidate being blocked</note> as a king. Then the owl, whom they had wanted to appoint as king, spoke to the crow who had hindered it: "Know, crow, you have grieved me, though I am innocent toward you. I do not know that I ever showed you any ill that you should have shown me such disgrace. Say, what cause do you have against me, or what have I done to you, that you have moved against me so? But I swear to you by the truth, and it is true:
<margin>
Shameful words cannot be healed.
</margin>
A tree that is struck with an iron tool may grow back together. And a wound that is cut through flesh and bone with a sword heals. But the wounds of the tongue do not heal, and their damage does not end. And these words of yours are like a sword that does not stop, but goes through and through again without end. Fire may be extinguished with water, strife with silence, the bite of snakes with theriac <note>term: "Tiriackers" - an ancient herbal jam or electuary used as an antidote to poison</note>, and sadness with hope. But the fire of enmity that is poured in through the tongue can nevermore be extinguished. Therefore, you flock of crows, today you have planted a tree between you and us that shall endure from age to age, through our lives and the lives of our children's children." Thus all the birds departed, so that they appointed no king at that time. Afterward, the crow thought of the disgrace and the words he had spoken against the owl...

# LXI.
## Of the Ancient Sages

done to the eagles, and said in his heart: "Truly, with this warning, I have brought eternal hatred upon myself and my companions. Among all the people, there was never anyone who wished to speak against it. And yet there were many among them who saw as much of the matter as I did. But they mastered their tongues <note>i.e., they kept silent</note> so that no harm might follow them; they considered future things, but I did not, and therein I erred. For a wise man should not rely so much on his wisdom, nor a strong man on his strength, that he takes enmity upon himself for anyone's sake. For he who does so is like one who drinks poison and afterwards hopes to expel it with treacle <note>original: "Tiriackers" - theriac, an ancient herbal jam used as a universal antidote</note>."

¶ Now I perceive in myself that it is not enough to be capable of good and sensible works, but rather he who applies himself with diligence may succeed most blessedly in his affairs. And therefore I consider myself today the most unwise of the entire flock of birds, because with my mouth I have established eternal enmity and adversity with the eagles through words alone. For the Sages say: <centered>It is the most harmful and extravagant loss which is gained through words alone.</centered> ->Sentence<- <note>A "sentence" or "sentenz" refers to a moral maxim or proverb</note> And the raven thought on this so much, to his great distress and temptation, that he became sick and died. Lord King, that is what you asked me concerning the cause of the enmity between the eagles and the ravens.

The King said: "I have understood this, and would to God that the raven had never come upon the earth, for he has brought us into this distress; our fathers have eaten sour grapes, and our teeth must be set on edge <note>A biblical reference to Ezekiel 18:2, meaning children suffering for the sins of their ancestors</note>."

¶ "Now let us leave this talk," said the King, "and tell me how we shall finish our business, for I know that their minds are not yet satisfied with what they have done to us." The raven answered: "I told you before why it is not good to fight against them. But if we could find a way to wipe them out entirely from the world, that would be the thing for the King to do. For with <margin>Cleverness does more than strength.</margin> cleverness <term>Gescheidigkeyt</term> <note>shrewdness or strategic intelligence</note> and order, a man does what another could not do with strength. For I have been told that some men, through their wisdom and deception, tricked a hermit so that he denied what his own eyes had seen."

The King said: "How was that?" The raven answered: "There was a hermit who had bought a goat <note>original: "geiß"</note> and wished to sacrifice it to God according to the old law. And as he was carrying the goat, he met three deceivers who took counsel on how they might take the goat from him. One deceiver said: 'Look at this hermit carrying a dog; what does he want to do with a dog?' The second said: 'I see this hermit—if indeed he is a hermit, for I do not see his head <note>likely referring to a tonsure or hood</note>, nor do I think he is one—for if he were a hermit, he would throw that bloody dog away from him and wash his clothes and his body.' The third said: 'Hermit, do you want to sell the dog?' And as all three spoke thus, the hermit who was carrying the goat thought it was a dog and said to himself: 'Perhaps the one who sold this to me deceived me.' And he threw the goat away from him and hurried to his house and washed himself and his clothes. Then these three took the goat and shared it among themselves. I have told you this so that you may consider: though the eagles are clever and powerful, yet we may overcome them with our own cleverness, so that we may kill them all.

¶ Now take heed of my advice, and see that you are satisfied with it. First, then..."

# The Book of Wisdom

First, behave as if you are a great enemy to me and hate me fiercely before all your people. Tear and wound me until you have covered me almost entirely in blood, then cast me down upon the earth and leave me lying there by the tree where our common dwelling is. Then you and all your people shall depart from there to another place, so far away that you cannot be seen, until I come back to you with a true answer. The King <note>The King of the Crows</note> did according to his command. Afterward, the Eagles received intelligence and came with power to this tree, the dwelling of the crows. And when they found no one there, they turned to go back home; but one among them saw the bloody crow and flew to him, in sight of his companions, and asked him who he was, where he came from, and where the host of crows had gone.

The crow gave answer and spoke: "I am such-and-such a person, and the son of such-and-such. But as for your asking after the crows—do you not see my pain and wounds? One who knows their secrets should rightly be treated thus." And when the King of the Eagles saw him, he said: "Know that this crow is a prince of the King of the Crows, his Secretary <term>Secretarius: a high-ranking confidential advisor or minister</term>, and his first in council. Therefore, I wonder how, or by what means, this adversity has befallen him." The crow was then questioned, and he gave answer: "Evil counsel and foolish understanding have brought me to this." They asked, "Why?" He answered and spoke: "When you did to the crows all that you have done, because of which many of them lay dead, our King called us before him after your departure and sought counsel from us regarding the matter, whether he should fight against you again. To this I said: 'It seems to me best not to fight against them, for they are mightier than we, and harder, and of bolder heart. My counsel is that you make an agreement with them, seek to make peace, and become subject to them. Do not be rebellious against them, but give them tribute—whatever they lay upon you—for your protection, and take counsel regarding this with your cities and lands.' They were all entirely against this and became unanimously resolved to fight against you, whether the outcome be good or evil. But I spoke against it and advised them to make themselves subject to you, saying:

<margin>
There is no better way to escape an enemy's hand than to become subject to him.
</margin>

'Do you not know that no one can better escape from his enemy's hand than by making himself subject to him?

<margin>
Parable of the hay.
</margin>

For you see the hay <note>or grass</note> upon the meadow; it remains safe before the wind because it gives way in every direction and humbles itself before the wind.

<margin>
Parable of the strong tree.
</margin>

But the tree, which is high and hard, is broken by the wind because it does not humble itself to give way or bow; instead, it resists, so that it is broken by the wind.' And when they heard this from me, they said to me: 'Are you perhaps one of the host of our enemies? We see now that you stand by our enemies and promote our loss, so that you might earn favor with them and that they might exalt you to power among them.' And with that, they fell upon me and committed against me all that you see." When the King of the Eagles heard this, he spoke to one of his council: "What do you think of this crow?" He answered: "I think nothing other than that he should be killed. For he is far more knowledgeable than we are. He is one of the noblest and wisest of the King of the Crows' counselors, and his destruction would be..."

# LXIII.
## The Wisdom of the Ancients.

### ¶ How a thief wanted to steal a cow from a hermit, and the devil wanted to strangle the hermit, and how the two fell into disagreement over who should go first, whereby the hermit was warned.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration shows a bearded man sleeping in a small stone hut with a visible window. To the left, a cow stands near the side of the building. To the right, a well-dressed man (the thief) walks and talks with a monstrous, hairy-legged devil with horns and claws.</image-desc>

It is said there was once a hermit <note>original: "einsidel"; a religious recluse living in simplicity</note> to whom a cow was given for God’s sake. A thief saw him leading it home and thought about how he might steal it; he followed after the hermit, and on the way, the devil met him in the form of a man. The thief spoke to him, "Who are you, and what is your intent?" He answered, "I am the devil, and I intend to attempt to strangle the hermit tonight; I am walking here to wait until the people around him have fallen asleep." The thief spoke, "And I have the intention to steal the cow." So they went together to the hermit’s house. Now, the hermit had tied up his cow and laid himself down in his bed to sleep. And when they came into the house, the thief thought to himself: "It is best for you to hurry so that you steal first. For perhaps when the devil tries to choke the hermit, he will cry out, causing the others in the house to wake up and come to his aid; they might then find and catch you. Therefore, it is good that you steal the cow first." So the thief spoke to the devil, "Stop and wait a while; let me steal the cow first, and after that, you may fulfill your desire." To this the devil answered, "I will do no such thing. I will choke him first; after that, you take what you want." The thief said, "No, I will be the first." And so, in their dispute and conflict, they became so divided that the thief called out to the hermit <margin>Discord brings harm.</margin> and warned him, saying, "Hermit, stand up! This devil wants to strangle you in your sleep!" And so the hermit and his household <note>original: "haußgesindt"; refers to servants or others living in the same complex</note> stood up. With that, the devil and the thief fled; thus the hermit escaped death and kept his cow.

<sig>m iij</sig>
<meta>catchword: And there</meta>

<summary>A thief and a devil fail in their respective crimes against a hermit because they cannot agree on who should act first, leading the thief to wake the hermit out of spite or self-preservation. The story serves as a moral lesson on how discord among the wicked can result in the protection of the innocent.</summary>
<keywords>hermit, devil, thief, cow, discord, warning, fable, morality</keywords>

<margin>A Proverb.</margin>
<margin>One should be well on guard against the traps of the enemy.</margin>
<margin>A rational person does not let themselves be moved by words.</margin>
And therefore I have told you this fable <note>original: "fabel"</note>, so that a wise man might sometimes draw near to his enemy’s companion, to see if he might learn from him what his enemy has in his heart. And when this advisor gave an end to his speech, the first one began and spoke to the King: "O, how this raven seduces you and draws you in with his shining words, so that you believe and trust him! Now the wise say: one must beware of humble and shining words. If you wish to lose yourself and all that is yours, then believe him; but I say to you, abandon this counsel and look wisely into this matter, as those who ought to recognize the traps and temptations of their enemies. Let your heart not turn away from useful advice and undertake something harmful to you and your people. If my advice is to be followed, then I desire that this raven be killed. For I fear, should he remain with us, that our end will be evil. For a rational man does not let himself be deceived with words when God gives his enemy into his hand. But the unwise man is deceived by flattering words. Lord King, do not be like the negligent, whose hearts are deceived by words and are brought to the point that they believe words more than what they have seen with their own eyes. As it once happened to a carpenter, who denied what he had seen with his own eyes." The King spoke, "How was that?" This advisor answered:

The woman was glad of this, and so after the meal he went out and commanded his wife to keep the house well and to be merry. And as he went out, his wife watched after him until she could see him no longer, and then she went back into her house. In the evening, the carpenter crept back and hid himself not far from the door of the house; and at night he went into the house and lay down under the bed where he usually slept with his wife. The woman thought her husband was away and sent for her lover <note>original: "bülen" - a paramour or illicit lover</note> and sent word to him that her husband had gone out. The woman discovered the husband under the bed and woke her lover and spoke: "I know that my husband lies under the bed. Now I want you to ask me with a loud voice which of the two I love most: you or my husband; and do not stop your asking until I have told it to you." And when he began to ask her, he spoke with a loud voice so that the carpenter could easily hear. She answered and spoke: "Why do you ask me? Or do you think there is anyone I love more than my lawful husband? We adulteresses <note>original: "bülerinn"</note> love our lovers for nothing else than to fulfill our lust with them, and we do not care for their persons at all; and when our will is satisfied, they are in our eyes like any other man. But a lawful, faithful husband is better to his wife than father and mother, brothers and child. Therefore, cursed be the woman who does not love the life of her lawful husband more than her own life." And when the man under the bed heard these words, he gave more credit to the trust of his wife's love than to what he had previously seen and felt. And he spoke in his heart: "Now I know that my wife loves me with her whole heart and soul." And so he remained lying there until the day had fully risen. Early in the morning, the lover went his way. The woman lay as if she were sleeping. Then the man crawled out from under the bed and found the woman sleeping, and sat down at her side on the bed and blew in her face because of the heat, and began to kiss and embrace her so that he woke her from sleep. Then he spoke: "My will and intent is to relieve you of all your sorrow; lie still and sleep, for you have not slept well this night; and if I had not spared you, I would have killed that scoundrel long ago in the night for the unrest he caused you, so that I might have freed you from him."
