# The Book of Wisdom

Now, the crows have not been able to do us any harm up until this day; but what may happen in the future, and whether this crow’s presence is useful, or virtuous, or why he has come to us, or if he is a danger to us, I doubt. Therefore, my final advice is to kill him. For know that I never feared the crows until I heard you speaking of this crow. For as long as we had no crow among us, they were unable to inflict any harm upon us at all. The King would not incline his ears to this saying; and because of his foolish, headstrong mind and his lack of wisdom, he commanded that the crow be led into his hall, and that he be tended with good care and his wounds be healed. Afterward, the Eagle <note>original: "Aar" - an archaic term for an eagle</note> who had given the advice to kill the crow spoke to the king: "Lord, if you have no will to kill the crow, then at least regard him as your enemy, and protect yourself and your people from him; for without doubt, the crow is clever and rebellious. And I suspect that this fellowship which the crow seeks with you is to our detriment." The King took no heed of these words at all; instead, he despised him and his words, and did not cease to do good to the crow and to honor him in all things. Now, this crow was of wise and sensible speech, and he soon grasped what was pleasing to the King of the Eagles or what displeased him; and every day he spoke in fables and talked of strange and wise things. By this means, he ensured that the King became more favorable toward him from day to day and grew to trust him. Meanwhile, he spoke playful and delightful words to the bystanders and the King’s courtiers, so that they all grew to love him.

And one day, as they stood before the King—the crow and many of the eagles, and also the eagle whose advice it had been to kill the crow—the Crow spoke: "It is true that the crows have harmed me and wished to kill me through their envy. Therefore, I have resolved not to depart from this company and never to have rest or joy until I fulfill my will. And when I consider this closely, I find no one who can better deceive and overcome them than you; but would to God that I might overcome them and bring them to nothing. Now, I have heard somewhere that whoever offers himself as a sacrifice shall receive what he desires; now I would gladly let myself be burned, so that through my invocation I might become an eagle, that I might avenge myself on my enemies, the crows, and cool my heart upon them and satisfy my revenge."

To this, the eagle who had advised killing him gave answer and said: "I liken you—with your sweet-tasting words which you make public and the evil within you which you hide—to wine that has a good smell and a beautiful color, yet when one drinks it, it tastes like vinegar. For know, even if we burned you a thousand times, and even if another bird came out of you each time (if that were possible), nothing would ever come out of you but the nature of a crow, even if your form became different. Just like the little mouse <note>original: "meuͤßlin"</note> of whom it is told: when she was led through the whole world to the most powerful ones to choose a husband, she rejected them all, always desiring one more powerful, until she came back to her beginning." The King asked how that was?

The Eagle answered:

# LXV.
## The Ancient Sages
<column-break/>

->[Image: A woodcut illustration depicting a meeting between a hermit and a young woman. The hermit, wearing a hooded robe and holding a staff, gestures toward the woman who has long, flowing hair and wears an ornate dress. They stand outdoors near a stone building with arched doorways; inside one arch, a statue of a figure stands on a pedestal. The background features a landscape with a tree, rolling hills, and a distant city skyline under a sky with clouds.]<-

It is said there was a <term>hermit</term><note>original: "einsidel"; a religious recluse living in solitude to serve God</note> who served God, and because he was a perfect man, God heard him in his prayers. At one time, he sat by a body of water, over which a sparrowhawk flew; it carried a <term>little mouse</term><note>original: "meüßlin"</note> in its talons, and the little mouse fell from the hawk before the hermit’s feet. The hermit took pity on her and bound her in a soft little cloth, having a desire to raise her in his house. Yet, he feared that his household servants would find it unpleasant. And so he prayed to God that He would let the little mouse become a little daughter. God heard this prayer, and the little mouse was transformed into a beautiful <term>maiden</term><note>original: "meydlin"</note>. 

The hermit led her home into his house and raised her, and told his household nothing of the fact that she had been a little mouse; for they thought that this child was his kinswoman, or that he had purchased her. And when the little daughter became of marriageable age, the hermit thought: "This daughter cannot be without a husband to rule her and from whom she may have joy." And he spoke to the daughter: "Choose for yourself whomever you wish for a husband." She spoke: "Yes, but one to whom no one is equal in power and lordship." 

The hermit spoke: "I know of no one equal to the <term>Sun</term><note>original: "Sunnen"</note> in power and lordship." Thereupon the hermit purified himself and prayed to the Sun—an enlightener in all the world and powerful over all other creatures—to take his daughter. The Sun gave him an answer: "It would be impossible for me to say no to you, who are so mercifully heard by God. But I am not the mightiest. Instead, go to the powerful leader of the <term>clouds</term><note>original: "wolcken"</note>; he is mightier than I. For when he wishes, he withholds my light so that I cannot shine upon the <term>earth</term><note>original: "erdtrich"</note>." And when the hermit came to him, at the edge of the sea where all clouds arise, he prayed just as he had prayed to the Sun. He answered: "It is true, God has given me the power..."

<summary>A hermit rescues a mouse and prays for it to be transformed into a beautiful girl, later seeking the most powerful being in existence to be her husband. The Sun directs the hermit to the Clouds, claiming they possess greater power by obscuring the light.</summary>

# The Book of Wisdom
<note>original: "Das bůch der Weißheyt" — This is a German translation of the "Directorium Humanae Vitae," a collection of fables known as Kalila and Dimna.</note>

Then one day he flew away stealthily and came to his king and the host of crows. To them he spoke: "I announce joy to you, for God has delivered our enemy into our hand." They were all happy at his arrival, for they all thought they had lost him—except for the King, who had kept this secret hidden. And the King spoke: "How does it please you to act?" The Crow answered: "Know that the eagles <note>original: "Aren" — while literally 'eagles,' in the context of these fables, they often represent the owls, the traditional enemies of the crows.</note> generally all live together in a cave of a mountain, in which they gather every night; and the cave is narrow at the front and wide inside, as a protection against the violence of an attack. Therefore, Lord King, take from all your people whoever can fly, and let each one take a piece of dry wood in his beak, as large as he can carry. I will take a fire, and let everyone fly after me this night. And when we come to the cave, let them all drop the wood onto the opening of the cave, and I will drop the fire into it. And by the air from all our wings, which we shall constantly move, the fire will begin to burn. Whoever then wants to come out will be burned or singed by the fire so that he is easy to overcome; but whoever remains inside will suffocate from the smoke and the heat." They followed him and acted according to his teaching. And with such a plot and the instruction of the crow, through his intelligence, the King of the Eagles died along with all his company. And through this, the crows became safe and lived henceforth in their dwelling without fear of their enemies.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the King of the Ravens and his advisor regarding their infiltration of the Owls (referred to here as Eagles). The advisor explains the necessity of patience and deception when living among enemies.</meta>

# The Wisdom of the Ancients. LXVII.

Then the King spoke to this Raven of his council: "I marvel how you were able to remain so long among the Eagles <note>original: 'Aaren' (Eagles). In this cycle of fables, the conflict is usually between Ravens and Owls; here the text uses 'Aaren'</note>, for the Sages say:
<margin>A Maxim.</margin>
It is more gruesome than the bite of serpents, and the burning of fire is more tolerable, than for one to dwell with his enemy and have fellowship with those who are not well-disposed toward him." The Raven answered:

<margin>How one should behave when one must dwell among enemies.</margin>
"It is true what you say, Lord King. But when a rational man must dwell among his enemies, it behooves him to consider how he may carry himself through with his words and gestures, and with great, clever patience. He should not use angry words, but be mild with a flattering countenance, and be gentle with the gruesome, and behave cautiously with the flatterers; his shoulders must be broad for all burdens of adversity, which are sometimes more painful to him than the shot of an arrow. Like the wise sick man, who takes the bitter Theriac <note>original: 'Tiriackers'. An ancient herbal jam or electuary used as an antidote to poison and a cure-all</note> for the sake of the health he receives from the medicine." The King asked, "What was the wisdom of the Eagles, and their understanding?" The Raven answered:

<margin>How one should behave with the enemy, and what damage folly or contempt brings.</margin>
"Among them all I found no wisdom nor understanding, except in one who advised that I be killed; but through the folly and ignorance of all the others, they chose not to follow his counsel. For they thought that no one was wiser than they, and that no one should or could place power against them; they entirely despised the counsel of that one man and did not consider the outcome of their affairs. Now it is said: He who finds his enemy in high honors of power should guard himself against him. As with the serpent, one can never trust him, and as much as he is able, a man should guard himself and withdraw from his enemies. Have no dealings with him in any water. Do not lie in his bed, do not wear his clothes, do not ride his horse. Take no medicine with him. Do not eat with him at his table, for it is a doubtful thing for a man to have fellowship with his enemy; for a wise man should guard himself against all doubtful things. But the Eagles, through their great folly, revealed all their secrets to me and hid nothing from me." The King spoke:

<margin>Pride brings contempt.</margin>
"I perceive that the Eagles' great pride and lack of wisdom led them into contempt. For who shares his counsel with the unjust and reaches a good end? Who is poorly defended and suffers no pain? Or who places his faith in the unfaithful and is not betrayed? Who eats to excess and does not become sick? Which King has foolish counselors and does not pay the price for it?

<margin>A Maxim.</margin>
But it is said: He who misleads himself with his own counsel lacks good wisdom. But liars and deceivers have no faithful fellowship. And the greedy man lacks all good things. The King who hoards, sins. And the King who is negligent and despises his laws, loses his essence." Then the King said to the Raven: "Great adversity has come upon you, that you had to be subject to the Eagles and be chastised by them." The Raven answered: "I suffered all this patiently, for I thought of the future good that should come to us all from it. For anyone who suffers adversity for the sake of the good that may arise from it, that should not be heavy for him. For as it is told, a serpent once became the servant of a frog, and became his horse when it was necessary, and that was for the sake of his own benefit." The King asked how that could be? The Raven spoke:

<meta>catchword: It was</meta>

There was a snake that had become very old and sick, so that its strength had left it and it could no longer hunt for its food. One day, it forced itself to crawl until it reached a well where there were many frogs. And when the frogs saw it, they said, "What is the matter with you, that we see you so sad and humble?" The snake said, "What good comes after old age? Know that when I was young, I terrified you at all times and ate of you every day. Now my strength has entirely left me, so that my flesh is consumed, my bones are powerless, and my skin is withered, so that I cannot frighten you. Even if you climbed upon me, I could not defend myself against you. Now go and tell this to your king." And when they told this to their king, the great frog, he came himself and wanted to see this, and said to the snake, "What makes you so sad?" The snake answered, "I have been chasing a frog all through the night into a hermit's <note>German: *einsidel*; a religious recluse</note> house. He has a dear son, and as he was walking in the house, he stepped on my tail, and I bit him. And when the hermit saw that, he searched for me, and when he could not catch me, he spoke a prayer over me so that God took away my strength. And now I am the most wretched of all the species of snakes. Lord King, now I am in your power; do with me according to your will, and if you wish, I will gladly be your mount <note>German: *fürung*; literally 'guidance' or 'conveyance'</note> to carry you wherever your will desires." And when the king of the frogs heard this, he thought that it would be a great honor for him to ride upon a snake. And so he rode for many days upon a snake. One day the snake said to the king of the frogs, "You know that I am a poor person, sick and not powerful enough to fetch my food for my necessity. The good that you would do for me now, you would do to the honor of God. And I beg you, provide me with food for the maintenance of my life's necessity." The great frog answered, "I acknowledge that you cannot live without a proper provision of your food, especially since you are my mount. I will provide for your nourishment," and he commanded that it be given two frogs every day for the nourishment of its life. The snake was satisfied with this and remained the frogs' mount for the rest of its life.

The King said, "One thing among all others wonders me: how you could be patient among them when you encountered so much adversity or insult, that you could suffer it all so patiently. Tell me, did anger never enter your mind so that they might perceive it in you?" Answered

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the King of the Ravens and his clever advisor (the Crow/Raven) following their victory over the Owls (here referred to as Eagles). It concludes the fifth chapter of the 'Book of the Ancient Sages' (the Kalila and Dimna tradition).</meta>

# The Ancient Sages. LXVIII.

The Raven <note>original: "der rapp"; in this context, the protagonist who infiltrated the enemy</note> spoke: My Lord King, I have softened their hearts with gentle words and a humble tongue, which I displayed to them, and with a laughing mouth, so that they could perceive no angry spirit in me. For it is said: Whoever is among his enemies whom he fears, he should turn them with gentle words, wisdom, and offerings, and guard himself so that he does not speak deceitfully against them or appear with pride. And, Lord King, know that when a king has a pious, sensible council, and whatever he handles with that council—even if it is delayed—it will come to a good end. But when he has an evil, unjust council, even if he is wise, clever speech is nothing without deeds. And even if the King occasionally succeeds in a matter with them, it will ultimately fall apart. And I am glad that you have come to rest, and for the deliverance of us all. For the time of your fear and displeasure allowed us neither to eat, nor drink, nor sleep, because of your sorrow. For it is said: Sleep is not restful for the sick man until he is recovering. Or for him whose desire is a sum of money promised by the King, until he receives it. Or for his worry who fears his enemy, until he overcomes him. And it is said: he whose sickness ends wins rest for his body; and he who lays a heavy burden from his shoulders, his loins rest. And he who overcomes his enemy wins rest for his heart. The King spoke: Tell me, how did the King of the Eagles <note>original: "Aaren"; archaic German for eagles, though the fable traditionally features owls</note> conduct himself in his government, and in his council, and among his people? The Raven answered: The King of the Eagles was a fool, lazy and proud, and his advisors were all fools, except for the one who commanded that I be killed.

<margin>What a good advisor should do.</margin>
The King spoke: What did you think of the one who commanded you be killed, that you praise him so? The Raven answered: Lord, he was wise and sensible, and considered matters from the beginning, what their end might bring. And he loved his King, and withheld nothing from him that was to be feared, nor did he conceal from him what was good. And when he saw that the King wished to do something unjust, he spoke what was on his mind, and showed the King the path of justice, and opened the truth to him, and gave him such counsel as he would have given to his own soul or body; moreover, he had many other good customs and wisdom in him which I cannot now recount. The King spoke: Truly, I find you a man of deeds, and the others men without deeds. After this, Sendebar <note>The legendary philosopher-narrator of these fables</note> spoke to King Disles: See what the Ravens have done to the Eagles, though they were weaker than the Eagles.

<margin>Mercy causes one to be shown mercy in return.</margin>
Now, according to this teaching, every man should fear his enemy and should not believe or trust him; even if he shows him love, it is not in his heart. But whoever increases his own friends and well-wishers, that will benefit him until the end of his time and in the time of his adversity. And whoever shares mercy in this time, he finds it here and in eternity.

# The sixth Chapter / concerning the Ape and the Tortoise / and it speaks of him / who has a good and faithful friend / and does not know how to keep him.

King Dißles <note>original: "Künig Dißles"; likely a corruption of the name Dabschelim from the original Sanskrit/Arabic sources</note> spoke to Sendebar <note>original: "Sendebar"; the name used for the philosopher Bidpai in the Western tradition of these fables</note> his Wise Man. I have understood your words / which you have told me / how a man should conduct and protect himself / when he encounters something from his enemies. Now tell me / whether it is better and easier to acquire wealth / or to keep it. Sendebar answered / Know, Lord / that things are lesser and easier to win / than to keep. For one finds those seeking wealth / and they find it / And thereafter they do not know how to keep it / until they lose it again. Then they stand shamed in their spirit / and in their will. Just as the Tortoise did before the Ape. The King spoke. How was that?

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting a fable. In the center-left, an ape is perched in a large tree overhanging a body of water. A tortoise swims in the water below the tree. The background on the left shows a city with tall Gothic spires. On the right side of the illustration, a separate landscape scene shows a fenced-in garden or orchard with another tree and a distant building on a hill.</image-desc>

Sendebar answered him / It is said / that when the King of the Apes grew old / and entirely lost his strength and power. Then he was ousted from the Kingdom by one of his servants / and driven from the land / and he <insert>the servant</insert> took the Kingdom. And the old Ape fled / and came to the shores of the Sea / There he found very many good, fruitful trees / of figs and other sweet things / there he took up his dwelling / and climbed upon the fig tree / and ate of the fruit. And a fig fell from him into the water. A tortoise saw this / and took it for her food. And the Ape was pleased by the sound of the water and by the fall of the figs. And he threw many figs therein / one after the other. The Tortoise took them

<summary>King Dißles asks Sendebar whether it is harder to gain or maintain fortune, prompting Sendebar to begin the fable of the aged Ape King and the Tortoise. The Ape, exiled by a usurper, befriends a tortoise by accidentally, then intentionally, dropping figs into the sea.</summary>

-> The Wisdom of the Ancients <-

she took them and ate them, and did not know that the monkey was throwing the figs down for her. And when the tortoise <term>tortoise</term><note>original: "Schiltkrott"</note> put her head out of the water, she saw the monkey on the tree, and the monkey saw her in the water, and they were both startled by one another. They drew closer together, and each pleased the other, and they offered to be companions, and they pledged themselves to one another, and henceforth they spent their time together. The tortoise stayed so long with her companion that she forgot to go home, and took no care at all for her spouse <note>original: "gemähel"</note>, or that he might long for her. And when his wife saw that her husband did not return, she became sad and lamented this to one of her companions <note>original: "gespilen"</note>.

### ¶ How a faithful wife cannot be compared with gold or silver, and of her virtues.

And when her companion heard her lament, she spoke: "You should not doubt or be sad about your husband for any reason, for I have been told he is by the shore of the sea in the company of a monkey, and they eat and drink good food there in joy; that is the reason for his absence. Follow me: have as little longing for him as he has for you, and since he has forgotten you, do the same in return. Then, if you can kill the one who has thus drawn him away from you, through a trap and secretly before your husband, do so." She said, "How shall I do that?" "Do this: eat and drink little, and walk much in the sun, and stand in the wind and air, and avoid the water. And when your husband comes, I will give good advice." She did what her companion told her, and her skin became as dry and hard as the bark of a tree, and she became quite thin. In the meantime, the tortoise <note>The text here uses the masculine "die Schiltkrott" referring to the husband</note> thought to travel home to his household, and he came home and found his wife thin and dry, and her body quite changed. And when he asked her what sickness she had, she remained silent and showed a face and demeanor of anger. And her companion answered and spoke: "Know that this sickness is very evil and dangerous; yet she could be helped, but it is impossible for you." The tortoise answered and spoke: "Dear one, tell me with what medicine <note>original: "arzney"</note> she may be helped; perhaps I may seek to find it. God and my wife know that if I could help her with my own soul, it would not be denied her." The companion spoke: "Know that this sickness may not be treated with any medicine except with the heart of a monkey, that she might eat it. And if she had that now, she would soon be healthy." When the tortoise heard that, he thought: "It is impossible for you to obtain a monkey's heart unless you betray your dear companion and bring him to his death—which God forbid, to load such sin upon me—and yet it is hard for me to avoid my wife dying from this. For a faithful, honest wife cannot be compared with gold nor with silver, nor with any kind of thing. For she is a help to a man's life in this world, and helps him keep his soul for eternity. And it is fitting for me to redeem her from death." And he set out to go to the monkey, but thought again in his mind: "How shall I [do this]..."

<summary>The tortoise's wife, neglected while her husband visits a monkey, plots with a friend to feign a deadly illness that can only be cured by a monkey's heart. The husband, torn between loyalty to his friend and his duty to his wife, decides he must sacrifice the monkey.</summary>
<keywords>fable, loyalty, marriage, betrayal, medicine, monkey, tortoise, deception</keywords>

...to do evil against my good, faithful companion for the sake of my wife. And so he went, lost in these thoughts, until he reached the Monkey. And when the Monkey saw him, he rejoiced and went toward him, saying:

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut ornament strip featuring a symmetrical Renaissance design of intertwining vines, leaves, and large stylized flowers, possibly lilies or pomegranates.</image-desc>

## ¶ Concerning true friendship and the repayment of good deeds among friends.

"What has moved you to be away from me for so long, and what is the cause of it?" The Tortoise answered, "Nothing but the shame that I have never been able to reward your kindness, nor make repayment for your generosity and noble spirit; therefore, I was ashamed to come to you again." To this the Monkey replied, "You should not be ashamed before me because of that, nor even mention it to me, for I do not desire such things. I only desire your company to show the affection of my heart toward you, and I am content that I may thereby forget my sorrows and the adversity that has befallen me since the day I was cast out from my wealth, my children, and my friends." The Tortoise answered and said, "Yet it is only right that one friend and companion should show a will to repay the good of the other. And that can best be done through a friend and companion. <note>The Tortoise is using the language of social obligation to lure the Monkey into a trap.</note> ¶ Now, you have never yet been to my house or dwelling, so that I might show you my wife, children, and friends—which is the greatest friendship one companion can show another. That this has never been shown to you by me is rightly to be esteemed a shame on my part."

<margin>One should desire nothing more from a friend than that he opens his loyalty.</margin>
The Monkey answered, "No one should desire more from his friend than that he opens his heart and loyalty to him, and loves him as himself; for anything else is a superfluity <term>üppigkeyt</term> <note>In this context, "üppigkeyt" refers to unnecessary material excess or vanity.</note>. Horses, oxen, and camels are tied together when they eat their fodder."

<margin>One should not desire friendship for the sake of gifts or profit. Parable of the nursing calf.</margin>
The Tortoise said, "You speak the truth, for no one should desire more from his friend than his friendship and that he keeps faith with him. And this should not be for the sake of gifts or the profit he might have from him. For whoever desires to have too much from his friend or companion, it happens to him as it does to the calf that wants to suckle its mother too hard: the mother finally kicks it with a foot so that it falls to the ground. Therefore, I must seek you out and repay you according to my ability and according to the works you have shown me. And for that reason, I want you to come with me to my house. For it is in a place full of good trees and fruits. And I want you to sit upon my back, and I will carry you to my dwelling, etc."

<meta>Catchword: ¶ How the...</meta>

<summary>The Tortoise, feeling guilty but driven by his wife's demands, convinces the Monkey to visit his home under the guise of hospitality and "repaying" the Monkey's past kindness. The Monkey argues that true friendship requires no material repayment, only loyalty and shared company.</summary>

# ### ¶ How many a man allows himself to be parted from faithful companionship through evil counsel, as the Turtle intended to do to the Monkey.

-> ❧ * ❧ <-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration composed of three vertical panels. The left panel shows a large bird (possibly a heron or stork) on a riverbank with a smaller animal on a hill in the background. And when the Monkey heard of the good fruits and trees, he was overcome by his desire and also by his good companionship, so that he sat upon the Turtle's back. The Turtle carried him into the water, and when she reached the middle of it, she thought of the great murder she intended to commit against the Monkey and considered: "Should you commit this treachery against your companion for the sake of your wife?" For it is said that gold is tested in the fire; men by their business; animals by heavy burdens. But a woman <note>The original German text reflects the era's common misogynistic tropes in wisdom literature, suggesting women are unpredictable or cannot be easily 'tested' for loyalty.</note> may be tested or known by nothing. And in such thoughts, she stood still and swam no further. 

And when the Monkey saw that his companion stood so still and did not swim onward, he thought: "The Turtle perhaps has evil in her mind or thoughts against you. Who knows whether the heart of my companion has turned toward evil against me, and perhaps intends to harm me?" For in the world there is nothing more moveable and prone to change than the heart. And the Sages say: "When the heart of a faithful companion, father, mother, or wife turns, it cannot be hidden, but must be revealed through gestures, manner, or words." And he spoke to his companion: "Friend, why do you not swim? Is there something you fear in your thoughts?" The Turtle answered and said: "I am burdened that I cannot offer you such honor in my house as is becoming and as would be right, on account of the illness of my wife." The Monkey spoke to her: "Know your [discomfort...]" <note>The sentence breaks off here and continues on the next page.</note>

# The Book of Wisdom

pleased him, and yet he was unable to do it. For in him there was no power nor strength, and so the donkey escaped from the lion unharmed. And when the lion let the donkey go thus unhurt, the fox spoke: "Lord King, why have you let the donkey go from you? If you have let the donkey go from you by your own will, despite your highest oath, then you have inflicted great fasting <note>original: "fastigung"; refers here to forced starvation or deprivation</note> and hunger upon my body. But if this has happened because of the weakness of your body, then woe to us! For if there is no more strength in our ability, it would be more useful for us to die than to live."

¶ Whoever is once deceived in a matter should not allow himself to be moved again by any smooth words, lest it happen to him as it did to the donkey, as follows hereafter.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration framed in a black border. In the center, a donkey lies on the ground with its mouth open and tongue visible. Then the lion thought: "If you should say that you let him go out of weakness and the frailty of your body, the fox will perceive that you are of no use at all, and he may dare to desert you. But if you say that you have done such a thing intentionally, he will take you for a fool who begrudges him his share of the food, and it will also lead to a bad end for you." And he spoke: "If you can bring the donkey back, then I will reveal to you why I have done this." Then the fox answered: "Now he has become aware of my deceit. And if he has ears to hear or a heart, he is rightly more clever <note>original: "gewitziger"; meaning more experienced or cautious through having been 'witted' or taught a lesson</note> so that I may no longer deceive him. Nevertheless, I will go and tell him a reason <note>original: "vrsach"; a pretext or cause</note> that will move him to come back here." And he went to the well and waited there again for the donkey, until his master came there and unloaded his cloth.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the fables of Bidpai (The Wisdom of the Ancients), specifically the conclusion of the story regarding the Monkey and the Turtle.</meta>

# LXXII.
->The Wisdom of the Ancients.<-

And as the donkey went to pasture, the fox showed himself. When the donkey saw him from afar, he said, "Fie on you! What would you try to do with me now?" The fox answered, "I wish to do nothing with you but all that is good. It is true, I led you to the other donkeys of whom I told you. And you saw what they did to you; out of the great love and pleasure they took in you, they had great joy and delight in your company. But you were terrified and doubted me, thinking I had done this to harm you. But truly, had you persisted a little longer, you would have seen honor bestowed upon you, and such a gathering of your own kind—the donkeys—as you have never seen before."

And since the donkey had never seen a lion in all his days, nor knew what it was, he went a second time with the fox; for he desired to be among his own kind, the donkeys, of whom the fox had spoken. And when he came again to the lion, the lion seized him and tore open his side, and said to the fox, "Guard and protect him until I go to the water to wash myself, so that I may afterwards eat his heart and ears for my health." <note>In medieval medicine, specific organs were often prescribed as cures for ailments; here the lion follows his "physician's" advice.</note> And while the lion went and washed himself in the water according to the advice of his physician, the fox ate the ears and the heart of the donkey.

And when the lion returned, he said to the fox, "Where are the ears and the heart of the donkey?" The fox answered, "Lion, you should rightly know: had this donkey possessed ears, he would have been able to hear, and had he a heart, he would have been able to understand. And if that had been the case, he would never have returned to you after he had escaped you the first time."

¶ Whoever foolishly neglects his affairs through oversight should quickly think to remedy it, just as the Monkey did with the Turtle.

"I have told you this fable so that you may know that I will not act like the donkey. You wished to betray me with your traps, but I escaped through my schemes and reason. For it is said: What the fool mocks through his folly is restored through wisdom." The Turtle answered, "You are just in your speech, and I praise the fact that a wise man diminishes his words and increases his works. And what he overlooks in folly, he knows how to atone for in his wisdom. Like a skilled wrestler: when he is thrown to the ground, he knows thereafter how to guard himself against the same." Thereupon King Dissles <note>original: "Dißleß der Künig"; likely a corruption of Dabschelim, the king to whom the philosopher Bidpai tells these stories.</note> said to his wise man, "I have understood by this fable that whoever acquires a good or useful thing for himself should take care not to lose it through a base cause; for a man is not always granted the opportunity to recover something better or good after its loss."

## End of the sixth chapter, concerning the Monkey and the Turtle. And concerning him who cannot keep his good friend.

King Disles <note>The king in the frame story of the "Seven Wise Masters" or "Book of Wisdom."</note> spoke to Sendebar his wise man: "I have taken note of your words, that a person sometimes loses a thing that is important to him. Not that it must be lost, or wants to be lost, but only that the owner cannot keep it because of his own lack of wisdom. Now tell me about this: when one hurries in his affairs and cannot consider the future therein, and what comes to him from it." Sendebar answered: "Whoever completes his affairs in haste and does not consider the future in his dealings, repentance follows him. And the end of his business will be like the merchant with the dog, whom he struck to death through no fault of the dog's." The King spoke: "How was that?" Sendebar answered:

The man spoke: How was that? # The Book of Wisdom

...and he began to speak to himself: "When this little jar is quite full of honey, I shall sell it for five guilders. With that, I will buy myself ten good sheep, and they will bear lambs twice every year. And so in one year there will be twenty, and from them and what may come from them, in ten years there will be a thousand. Then I will buy a cow for every four sheep, and buy oxen and fields along with them, and they shall increase with their fruits; and then I will take the oxen to work the fields. And from the other cows and sheep I will take milk and wool; before five years have passed, it will increase so much that I will have attained great possessions and wealth. Then I will buy myself menservants and maidservants, and build high and handsome buildings, and after that, I will take a beautiful wife from a noble lineage, and I will lie with her in joyful love. And she will conceive and bear me a beautiful, fortunate, and God-fearing son, and he will grow in learning, in the arts, and in wisdom. Through him, I shall leave a good reputation after my death. But should he not be obedient and disregard my punishment, I would strike him across the loins with my staff." And he took his staff—which was normally used to make the bed—to show himself how fiercely he would strike his son. And he struck the earthen jar that hung above his head into pieces, so that the honey dripped down over his face and into the bed. And nothing came of all his thoughts, except that he had to wash his face and his bed.

### ¶ No one should be hasty in their affairs, lest they do that which they later regret, like the man who struck his faithful dog to death, as follows hereafter, etc.

"And I have told you this fable so that you should not speak or prophesy about that which you do not know. For it is said: *You should not boast of tomorrow, for you do not know what may befall you today.*" <note>A reference to Proverbs 27:1: "Boast not thyself of tomorrow; for thou knowest not what a day may bring forth."</note> And when the man heard this, he was silent. And in the natural course of time, the woman gave birth to a delightful son, at which they both rejoiced. And when she was able to leave her childbed, the woman said to her husband, "Stay here and watch the child until I present myself in the church and receive the purification according to the custom of women." <note>Refers to the "Churching of Women," a blessing given to mothers after childbirth.</note> And while the father sat alone with the child, and the wife had just gone away, the King’s messenger came for the man.

Now, he had a dog in his house that was very dear to him. This dog saw a snake crawling out of a hole toward the child, intending to harm it. He seized it with his teeth and tore it to pieces. And when the man came back into his house, the dog went to meet him; and when he saw its mouth was bloody, he thought that his child had been eaten by it, and he struck the dog so that it died, without considering the matter. And afterward, he went back into the house, and when he found the child alive and the snake dead beside him, he realized that the dog had killed the snake, and...

# The Book of Wisdom

## The eighth Chapter / Concerning the Weasel <note>original: "Maußhund" - literally 'mouse-dog', a common term for a weasel or mongoose</note> and the Mouse / and it is about the enemy who seeks peace with his foes in times of necessity.

The King spoke to his master / I have understood your words / concerning him / who does not handle his affairs with foresight / and what happens to him in the end. Now tell me of a weak person / when he falls into the hands of his enemies / by what cleverness <term>gescheidigkeyt: shrewdness or tactical intelligence</term> it is fitting for him to escape. Sendebar answered / when a man falls into the hands of his enemies / it is fitting for him to seek out strategies / and sometimes to reconcile himself with one / and promise himself into fellowship with him / so that he may deliver himself from the other. For just as at all times a friend may become another's enemy for many reasons / so also at all times an enemy may become another's friend for a reason. For when a man sees / that his enemy may be of use to his life / or his well-being / it is quite fitting for him to seek him as a friend; likewise / when he sees his friend intending him treachery / it is quite fitting for him to leave him. For whoever can reconcile with his enemy at the proper time / and leaves his friend / when he finds him once in untrustworthiness / he may well protect himself from harm. As once did / the mouse and the cat <note>The text uses "Maußhund" (weasel) and "katz" (cat) somewhat interchangeably for the predator here</note> / who delivered themselves from death through their mutual reconciliation. The King spoke / how was that? The master spoke.

There was a large tree by the shore of the sea in a great wilderness. Many animals lived near it / and upon the tree's branches were many dwellings of birds. Down in the tree was a hole / wherein lived a weasel / who was named Peridon. Nearby was a crevice / wherein lived a mouse / who was named Ron. And after hunters <note>original: "weydleüt"</note> came to hunt many times / it happened one day / that the hunters spread their nets / and the weasel fell into them. And as the mouse goes out of her crevice to seek her food / she sees the weasel caught in the net / and rejoices in his misfortune / and does not know what approaches her / and as she looks behind her / she perceives a dog / who desires her. And as she looked above her / she perceives an eagle <note>original: "Aaren"</note> upon the branch of the tree / who had his sight set on her. Then the mouse thought / if you run backward / the dog will catch you. If you go forward / the eagle will take you. If you flee into the meadow / you must go toward the weasel / then you are truly in the hands of your rightful enemy. And when she saw / that she was surrounded by her enemies / she spoke in her heart. From these anxieties no one can deliver me / except reason and cleverness. Now my heart shall not be terrified by that / which might bring me sorrow / For a wise / bold man's reason and foresight should not fail him / in adversity. For the foresight of the wise / may deliver them in times of misfortune / more than strength can.

This mouse said, "It is necessary for me to guard myself on all sides, to look behind me, to think ahead, and to look upward to see how I may protect myself. I perceive nothing better for me than to approach this <term>mousetrap-dog</term> <note>original: "maußhund"; likely referring to a marten, polecat, or a cat-like predator used for catching mice</note> and seek peace with him. For I see that help is as necessary for him as it is for me, to release him from his misfortune and the fear of death, in which no one can be as useful to him as I. Perhaps if he hears my faithful and upright words which I will speak to him, he will believe me and promise peace, seeing that he may be liberated thereby, and I may thus be spared through him." And she went to the mousetrap-dog or cat and said, "How do you appear here?" He answered, "You see me in distress." The mouse said to him, "I do not lie to you, nor will I speak deceitfully with you, but in total truth. For I have desired to live to see the day I found you in such distress, and yet I am concerned—

# The Book of Wisdom

And thereafter the mouse went out of her hole and saw the **mouser** <note>original: "maußhundt" - literally "mouse-dog," an early German term for a cat</note>, who spoke to her: "Friend, why is this? Will you not come to me, seeing as you have shown me such great virtue? It is fitting that you eat the fruit of your merit. Therefore come to me and fear nothing at all. For you have preserved me from death. Therefore you shall receive a reward from me and my lineage. And all that is mine shall be yours." And the mouser swore to him by Him who lives eternally <note>A common medieval formula for an oath to God</note> that he should believe in his words. To this the mouse gave him answer and spoke: "He who does not know how to deal with his enemy as with his friend—and especially with his enemy whom he requires as a friend through necessity, so that he must lay his dear hands under his feet <note>An idiom for extreme submission or service</note> and thereafter recognize his nature—he lives just like a sleeper standing before an elephant. For the wise have called the friendly a 'friend' through the friendship that may happen to him. And the contrary one the 'enemy' through the cause of hostile things that he expects from him; and the enemy does not live in the world whose enmity I do not fear. Now every person uses his own counsel for his own good. Now the counsel of one's own foresight gives one cause to fear his enemy; and yet a wise man should at times give way to enmity and show him kindness, because of the good that may come to him from it. Likewise, he may leave a friend and avoid his company because of future harm that might fall upon him from it. Just like the child who hangs upon the mother when it desires to suckle. And when it stops suckling, the child turns away from the mother again; likewise all those who love one another for the sake of gifts. Thus should a rational person also choose his profit from friends and enemies. For at times good may happen to him in friendship that is made between him and his adversary. But a rational man should not abandon his old companionship with his friend for nothing, even if he may not be useful to him at the time, but should always keep him in friendship and trust him. And he should always fear his enemy. For whoever has become a friend through love and through some profit and gain (which he might receive or have therefrom), when that same profit or cause of love has taken an end, then the friendship also ends. And it returns to the first of its natures. Just as water, while it hangs over the fire, is warm by cause of the heat of the fire. And when it is taken away from there, it becomes cold, as it was before in its nature. Now you are to me the greatest enemy among all creatures. But through the good that each of us might take from the other, it happened that we became companions. But now the fear that forced our minds together has an end, and our minds are henceforth free. Therefore the old hatred, which we have toward one another by right nature, has quickly fallen back into place. For since I am the food and you are desiring to eat, and I am weak and you are strong, how then could there be companionship between us without anger? And especially on my account. I cannot devour you as you can me; for whoever trusts his enemy falls at last into his hand. For the wise have spoken: When a weak person neither trusts nor believes his enemy, he is most secure of all. Likewise when

# The Ninth Chapter / Of the King / and
## the bird / and it is of the companions / who secretly
## hate one another / and how one should not

So after that, the king spoke to his wise man. I have understood your fable, how two joined together through necessity until they had escaped their harm. Now tell me of two who love one another, and through the dealings they have with one another, come to hate each other; how much should they trust one another after that? Sendebar <note>The philosopher-narrator of these fables, often identified with the Indian sage Bidpai</note> the master answered: Sensible and prudent men should not trust their enemies, even if they show them good will and change their ways. Just as the bird did to the King, whose son's eyes he had scratched out. The King spoke, "How was that?" The master answered.

# LXXVIII.
### The Wisdom of the Ancients

He brought from there two dates of very noble flavor, and gave one to the young bird and the other to the King’s son. This fruit gave the bird and the child such great strength that they increased remarkably in power and stature. One day, when the elder Pinza had flown out for his food, the young Pinza sat in the young King’s lap and played with him. And the boy grew tired and annoyed by the jesting, and when the bird would not stop according to its habit, the child grew angry and threw the bird against the ground so that it died.

And when Pinza the elder came from the mountain with the food, according to his custom, and saw his young Pinza lying on the earth, he mourned and said: "Cursed be all Kings, young and old, for in them is neither loyalty, nor faith, nor mercy. Blessed is he who finds his nourishment outside of the Kings' courts, for they treasure nor regard no man’s service or friendship, no matter how faithful he may be to them. And they love no one except those from whom they expect or intend to receive profit, and where their perverse, extravagant will occasionally inclines them. And when that happens, they thereafter have no regard or love for them, and all their works are deceitful and grasping." And Pinza the elder thought to himself: "I shall not rest until I have avenged myself against this my grim enemy, in whom there is neither faith nor loyalty, who gives up his own servant and despises those who eat at his table." And in a rage, he hurried to the young King and scratched his eyes out, and flew away with that and sat on high. # The Book of Wisdom
<meta>catchword: The Tenth</meta>

...now speak words with your mouth that are not in your mind. For I know that you desire my life and to separate my soul from my body. But the soul fears death and flees from it. Now, one seeks to catch birds for three reasons: for food, or for pastime <note>entertainment/sport</note>, or to destroy them. Therefore, you do not seek me for food, for my flesh is not healthy; nor for pastime, for I cannot sing; but you seek to kill me because of your son. Therefore, your company is of no use to me at all. For if we were companions now, and you thought of my deed against your son, and I thought of your son's deed against my young, then both of our hearts would be turned to bitterness. The King answered: "It is not praiseworthy for one who has no power to let envy or hatred out of his heart, and also to place companionable love therein. For reason and understanding are given to man so that they should be powerful enough to cast the evil out of their hearts and to receive the good." Pinza spoke: "Enmity is like a sore on a foot; the more one leans on it to walk, the more his sickness increases. Or like a weak eye; the more one touches it, the worse it becomes. So it is for one who hates his enemy; the more he approaches him, the more his heart is burdened. And whoever goes a bad way or starts an affair without knowing the end of either, he deceives himself. He who cannot digest and yet eats much, or swallows a larger mouthful than his throat is wide, is himself to blame—just like him who lets himself be paid with the good words of his enemy and believes him; he is his own enemy. For it is fitting for a sensible person to walk in a perilous place only if he can escape from it where he lacks no good thing. For there are five kinds of customs. Whoever keeps them may thereby reach a good and desirable end, and gain friends and companions. First: that he be of innocent hands, so that he desires to inflict harm on no one. Second: that he knows how to distinguish good from evil. Third: that he guards himself against all dishonorable things. Fourth: that he be of noble works. Fifth: that he be God-fearing. For that is a wicked wealth which is not shared with the poor; and a wicked wife who is rebellious against her husband; and the worst among companions is he who is a traitor. The worst among Kings is he who kills the innocent. The worst thing on earth is where there is no peace or order. And I have no faith in you, and cannot find it in myself to join you." And with that, Pinza greeted the King and departed from there, etc.

LXXX.
->The Wisdom of the Ancients.<-

# The Tenth Chapter / Concerning King Sedras /
## and Billero his Prince. And it is about him / who restrains
## his anger / and thereby overcomes
## his own vices.

King Disles spoke to Sendebar his wise man: I have taken heed of your speech / which you told me / regarding one who angers a lord / or the lord him. Now tell me how the king should conduct himself in unsociable times / for the protection of his body / and the benefit of his kingdom / and how he should show himself with a generous hand / or with the strength of his deeds / and the necessity of his heart / or with noble conduct / or with the restraint of his anger. The master answered: There is nothing / by which the king may so well protect / and enlarge his kingdom. Than that he, in all his works and undertakings / avoids injustice and sudden anger / and this virtue is found only in high / and noble minds. And also that the King bases his counsel upon understanding and pious men / who are experienced in the matter. But the best among all these is / that he restrains his anger / when he is angry with someone / so that he does not punish them thoughtlessly in haste. And although this is useful for all people to do / it is the very best state for the King. When he handles his business with men of reason and justice / and applies himself to honor / and makes use of justice / and certain works. Thus his kingdom increases / but when a man does not restrain his anger / and does not consider what he wishes to do / and takes counsel with the frivolous / and those not of noble mind / then his affairs do not succeed. For a wise man should have his counsel with the wise / and if the King is sensible / and faithful to his counselors / and does not act secretly apart from them. Then he may elevate his affairs / and live in joy / and withstand his troubles / or his enemies. And when his counselors correct him / let him receive that with a laughing spirit / and avoid his anger / just as Sedras the King of India did with Billero his Prince. King Disles spoke / How was that? Sendebar answered.

There was in India a mighty King of the city of Trilingum <note>Likely referring to Telingana or a legendary Indian locale</note> and mighty over all kings / named Sedras / who had among his other princes a prince / named Billero. This man was wise / sensible and God-fearing / and a fulfiller of the King's services / and of humble sweet words / submissive / obedient and faithful as the heart / and a prudent provider for the court of his King. One night the King lay on his bed / there he saw a dream in his sleep / and awoke from it / and when he fell asleep again / those same dreams came back into his memory / and the dreams were these. It seemed to him / there stood two red fish before him on their tails / and two water-birds flew one after the other / and fell into his hands.

In the morning, when the King <note>original: "Künig"</note> rose from his bed, he was troubled and ordered the interpreters of dreams and the scholars of scripture <note>original: "geschrifft weisen," referring to those learned in law or holy texts</note> to be summoned. They were all from a city that the same King had previously deceived and besieged, and in which he had slain twelve thousand men and thereafter conquered it. And when they came before him, he spoke and revealed his dream, and begged them to interpret it for him. They gave him an answer and spoke: "This is a great wonder-sign, and no man has seen the like in dreams. If it pleases you, we will all gather together and search among ourselves, and provide an explanation of your dream and announce it to you." And since this pleased the King, he set a time for them on the seventh day. And when they all came together, they spoke among themselves in great secrecy and with promised oaths which they swore to one another. And therewith the eldest among them began and spoke: "We know what this tyrant <note>original: "Tirañ"</note> has done to our friends with his grim dealings, and how he has slain twelve thousand men among us. Now the time is given to us that we may avenge ourselves upon him. And we have the cause to fulfill it upon him."

# The Ancient Sages

bring. Therefore we shall go to the King and make the matter of the dream heavy for him—evil, great, and fearful in his eyes—until we bring him to such fear that he promises to do whatever we command him. And when we notice that he is inclined toward us out of fear, then we shall tell him of the blood-shedding that he has done, which he cannot avoid, and that vengeance cries out over him until such is amended with his body and with his kingdom. He must then give us several of his advisors to be put to death; with that blood, the vengeance may be stilled, and not otherwise, but only through his body and his kingdom. And when he asks us which ones we want: then we will first name **Helebat** <note>The Queen's name in this version of the Kalila and Dimna fables</note>, the Queen, who is dearer to him than all other women. We also want his brother's son, his private secretary, whom he trusts above all chancellors. We also want **Billero**, his prince among the knighthood, who is his most secret advisor. We also want his sword, the likes of which there is none on earth. We also want his white elephant, which he rides into battle, and we want him to give us **Kimeron**, his holy friend; with these we may take vengeance upon him, if he grants us all of that. Then we shall say: "King, these shall be your ransom, with which we may redeem your kingdom and your life." For when that happens, we may thereafter worship the gods and pray for you. Then your left side shall be anointed with the blood of the slain, and afterward washed with blessed water, so that you would be cleansed of your sins with which you earned this wrath. And afterward, your face will also be anointed with the noble oil; then you shall go back into your hall and be cleansed before the heavy judgment which the wisdom of the gods has passed over you. And we shall say then: "Will you now follow our advice? And does it please you to give us these persons, so that you may thereby buy back your life and live henceforth without adversity? But if you deny this, then the heavy judgment that appeared to you shall follow, and you shall shortly die or be driven from your kingdom." We must also for a while sustain ourselves with fasting and the reduction of our being, so that we appear with more misshapen faces, as if we have brought ourselves to such a state through fasting and great prayers and the study of our books. Then we may persuade the King of this, so that he will give us all of this. Thus, his body and land cannot escape us, and we may well avenge ourselves upon him according to our will. This advice of the old man pleased them all very well, and they thus concluded their counsel to present it to the King in the worst possible form on the appointed day. And when they came before the King, the old man began before them all and spoke: "The King lives, and may a rational spirit remain in his royal body. Now we have read our books and have sought out the King's dream with great diligence, with much fasting and prayer, which we may announce to the King, as to what may be useful to him and his kingdom." And they told the King their advice, as stands written above. And when the King heard that, he became sorrowful and spoke: "It is better for me to die than to live, if I should give all those to be killed who are the joy of my life. # The Book of Wisdom

The masters gave the King an answer and spoke: "Lord, if it does not displease you, we wish to reveal to you that your answer is not suitable—that you cherish others more than your own body. For you would more rightfully preserve your own body and your kingdom and do what we have spoken; thus you would experience a good end. And let yourself be satisfied with your own person, and exchange the one for the other. For take heed: if all those kinsmen and companions perish, you may find others. Do not fear to leave a small thing behind, lest you lack a greater one because of it, or lose yourself for the sake of another’s honor or benefit. For know that every human being by nature desires to live for themselves. And as life enters the body through much labor and sorrow, so also is it kept therein through much protection and care. And remember that your life has been the acquisition of your kingdom, into which you came with great war, labor, adversity, and the sweat of your body through much long time and years; and that you wish to abandon in a single moment. And yet, all is as nothing compared to your person. Therefore, it is advised to you without division or any middle ground: to give us what we have demanded for the ransom and preservation of your life and your kingdom."

And when the King heard this, he was terrified, and his heart became sorrowful, and his pain was increased. He went into the Hall and lay upon his face in grief, and turned himself like a fish that is thrown out of the water onto dry land. And he thought in his mind: "With what justification shall I do this great evil, or what should I choose—whether I should look after my own person, or give up my dearest and most faithful ones? And if I did that, how long might I then remain in rest and peace in my kingdom? For it is not eternal. For today or tomorrow I shall die anyway. And therefore, in this matter, careful consideration is truly necessary for me. For what will my pastime be anymore if I kill Helebat, my spouse? What joy will I gain to see with my eyes if I lose my brother’s son and my own son? And how may my kingdom endure if I give up Billero, the prince of my knighthood <note>likely the commander of his army</note>? How may I be usefully established in my kingdom if I give up my white elephant and my good sword? And what a shameful king would I be called if I lost all of this. And how can I be kindly and godly instructed if I let my holy man Limeron be killed? And how shall I live after the loss, or what joy can I have?" And herewith

# The Ancient Sages. LXXXIII.

The Queen [spoke]: "Even if I were to appear to be jesting in your eyes, no person should be so despondent that they cannot receive comfort for their sorrow through good and faithful friends. For just as no person has committed a sin so great that they do not have the power to cast it off through repentance, so too should no person have such great dismay that it cannot be softened by the presence of good friends and their consolation. For if it concerns a thing already passed, which is irrecoverable, then the advice of the wise is to let the sorrow go. If it concerns future things, then future things are changeable. For [by grieving] you gladden the hearts of your enemies with your own sadness, and that is unwise."

The King spoke: "Now I have compassion for you, and it is not good that I should reveal this to you. For it is such a matter that because of it, you, my son, and my best and most faithful friends must die; how can I be anything but mournful? Know this: I summoned the wise masters of the city that I conquered <note>These are the 'Wise Masters' of a defeated enemy, suggesting their advice may be a trap</note> so that they might explain my dream to me. They have commanded me to kill you, and my son, and Villero my brother’s son, the prince of my knights, and others of my faithful ones. How can I be happy now, when I shall see you no more and must kill all the others with you? Is there anyone in this world whom this concerned who would not mourn?"

When the Queen heard this, she would not show any mourning at all, and spoke: "Lord King, you should not trouble yourself about this for any reason; if my life can prevent your death, I will be willing for the King’s sake. For there are more women destined for you, and those you have now who are better than I. But one thing I wish to entreat of the King, for the sake of the love I have shown him: that after my death he does not believe the unfaithful men, and kills no one after me until he experiences and learns of this matter thoroughly and specifically. And consider that you should not have to repent of such a thing, for the King can restore no one to life whom he has killed. For it is said as an example: He who has precious stones should not throw them away unless he shows them to one who recognizes them <note>A metaphor for seeking expert, loyal counsel rather than biased opinions</note>.

"Therefore the King should consider that these men do not love the King. For he has slain their friends and kinsmen and subdued them and their city with force; they may not have forgotten all of this yet. Therefore it was not fitting for you to show and reveal the secret of your dream to them. And even less fitting to use their advice, for you understand it to be immoderate that you should kill your most faithful counselors, who brought your kingdom to its rise through their wisdom and governance; that you should shed the blood of your son, who is your life; that you should lose the prince of your knighthood; and that you should give your elephant <note>term: elephant (likely referring to a powerful war beast or a symbol of royal strength)</note>, your good sword, upon which your defense and the sustenance of your body rests, into your enemy's hand—and the Prophet and Holy Man. I say nothing of your wife, for I desire to die before them all, provided that the King reflects better afterward and recognizes what is rightfully fitting for him. For if you kill us all, you lose all the delight of your eyes, the body of your kingdom, and the fortune of your victory. Then they may overcome you, take revenge upon you, drive you out of your kingdom, and settle themselves back into their power peacefully. But one thing I can advise you in confidence: the wise and learned Rimeron, who is a holy man—go to him and reveal your secret, and conclude your counsel with him; he knows without all—"

And when Kimeron received the King, he spoke: "What cause has driven the King to me today, and why does he have a sorrowful face and no crown upon his head?" The King answered: "Once while I was sleeping in my bed, I heard eight voices. And when I fell asleep again, I saw eight dreams. And when I revealed them to the masters—your brothers from the city that I previously conquered—they gave me an evil interpretation. Consequently, I fear that misfortune is near, and that I shall be slain in battle or driven from my kingdom by mishap." Kimeron answered him: "The King should not be terrified, nor fear these visions, for these appearances will very soon manifest for the King’s good end, honor, and benefit. And the meaning of your dream is this: The two red fish that stood near you on their tails <note>original: "auff jren schwenzen"</note> signify the two Kings of Arabia and of Emlach <note>Emlach likely refers to Elam, an ancient civilization in what is now Iran</note>, who shall send you two large bowls full of precious stones. # The Ancient Sages. LXXXIIII.

The birds that you saw flying behind you, and which fell into your hands, signify that the Emperor of Greece <note>original: "Keyser von Kriechen"</note> will send you two horses, the likes of which have never been seen in these lands. And the snake that you saw passing through your left foot signifies that the King of Tharsis will send you a sword, the likes of which has never been better made on earth. And where you thought your body was sprinkled with blood: there the King of Sheba <note>original: "Seba"</note> will send you a precious red purple robe for royal dignity. And when it seemed to you as if you were being washed: there the King of Thabar will send the precious white linen cloth that is called byssus <term>byssus: an extremely fine, costly silk-like linen from antiquity</term>. And where you seemed to walk upon a mountain that was white, it signifies that the King of Edom will send you a white elephant, such as no horse can match. And the pillar of fire upon your head signifies that the King of Kedar <note>original: "Cedar"</note> will send a golden crown for your head. But the white bird, which you thought was digging into your head with its beak—that I will not interpret for you now. But have no doubt at all that it is anything evil. However, you shall be slightly angered against your dearest friend; and these gifts shall all be sent to you within these next seven days. 

And when the King heard this, he bowed himself before the holy white man <note>referring to the sage's white hair or purity</note> even down to his feet, and came back home into his house, and said: "On earth there is no wiser man than the holy Rimeron, and I will wait for his words until the appointed time." And on the seventh day, the King had dressed himself in royal garments according to Rimeron's command, and sat in the Hall of Joys upon his royal throne. And around him stood the wise men of his kingdom in precious attire, just as Rimeron the wise had instructed him. Then the messengers all arrived, one after the other with their gifts. And when the King saw the noble gemstones, golden jewelry, and the great gifts, his heart was lifted up in great joy, and he said in his heart: "I have rightly found <note>likely meaning: "I have learned my lesson"</note> that I revealed my secrets to the unfaithful."

After this, the King spoke: "I did not consider my affairs wisely when I revealed my dream to the unfaithful; and had the grace of God not protected me, along with the counsel of the Queen, I would have destroyed myself and my kingdom, and sent my dearest ones to an innocent death. Therefore, it is not fitting for anyone to be hasty in their affairs, but rather to seek the advice of their faithful ones and follow them. For following the advice of the Queen <note>The text refers to Helebat, the King's wife</note> led me surely to the holy man Kymeron, who through his discernment preserved me in my kingdom. Thus, I did not put my dearest child and most faithful friends to death, which would have resulted in the loss of my realm."

And thereafter, the King summoned his son, and Billero, the Prince of his kingdom and his most intimate confidant, and spoke to them: "It is not fitting to lay these gifts in my treasury, but rather to divide them among you, who would have offered your bodies for me, and with Helebat, who through her counsel kept me in my kingdom and protected me from great harm—and kept all of you alive—turning my great pain into great joy."

Billero answered: "It is no great thing that we all should have given ourselves unto death for the King. For what faithful servant would not give himself unto death for his Lord? Indeed, whoever would not willingly face death with his whole heart would not be a man of right reason. Therefore, the King should not wonder that his faithful servants were willing to die for him. We are also not worthy to take from these gifts; you should give them only to your son with a free spirit."

The King answered: "Now the gods have bestowed great goods upon us. Therefore, take from them without any shame whatever your heart desires for your portion, and have joy with it." Billero answered and said: "Let it be done as it pleases the King; yet let the King first take from it what he desires."

And so the King took the elephant <note>original: "helfandt"; likely a reference to an ivory carving or a literal elephant sent as a gift</note> and the sword, and gave the horse to his son, and the other <insert>gift</insert> to his brother's son, Billero. And the white garment he sent to Kimeron the holy man, deeming him worthy of the white robe. But the crown and the red purple garment were worthy only for women to wear. And he spoke to Billero, his prince:

And out of desire, Billero signaled virtuously to Queen Belebar with his eyes that she should choose the purple <note>original: "purper"; referring to a royal purple garment or cloth</note> instead of the crown. Now the King stood there and watched Billero closely <note>original: "vnder seine augen"; literally "under his eyes," meaning to watch someone's facial expressions intently</note> to see if he would give the Queen any secret sign. Whenever Billero went before the King after that, he would wink with his eyes as if he did it out of habit, so that the King would not harbor any evil thoughts in his heart regarding it.

Now it was the custom of the King that when he slept one night with the Queen, he would sleep the next with the concubine <note>original: "diernen," here referring to the secondary wife or mistress</note>. And one night, when he intended to sleep with the Queen, she had prepared a meal of rice for him. She took a golden bowl containing the food and went to the King wearing her crown. The concubine <note>original: "kepß weib"</note> saw this and was moved to envy against the Queen; she put on her purple robe and entered the hall where the Queen and King were. The hall was illuminated by the purple robe and its magnificence. When the King saw this, he was moved with love for her and said to Nelebat <note>The Queen's name</note>: "Truly, you have acted foolishly by choosing the crown and leaving behind the precious robe, the likes of which we do not otherwise possess." When the Queen saw that the King praised and loved the servant-woman above her, and scorned her, and was unmindful of her faithful counsel—which had preserved him in his kingdom—she became enraged at the King. She took the bowl of food and poured it over the King's head, so that it ran down his face and beard onto his clothes. With this, the final dream was fulfilled: that the white bird had bitten him in his head.

# The Wisdom of the Ancients. LXXXVI.

before the face of the King and spoke to himself: "I will not kill her until the King’s anger has softened. For a wise, pious woman is an honor to the King and the realm. Furthermore, there is none like her among all those whom the King loves, and he cannot be happy for a single hour without her. Thereupon Villero took his sword and smeared it with the blood of a lamb, and went sadly before the King. And he spoke: "I have fulfilled your command and killed Nelebat the Queen."

### ¶ How the King came into repentance that he had ordered his Queen to be killed, and how Villero told him a parable.

<note>The woodcut initial 'A' begins the following paragraph.</note>
But a short time thereafter, the King thought of the beauty of the Queen, and there fell into his mind her prudence and the manner which she possessed, and how she had lived wisely and so companionably with him, and had only recently warned him of great harm and shame. And a heavy repentance for her death came upon him, yet he was ashamed to ask Villero whether he had killed her or not; and yet he hoped that Villero’s wisdom had not permitted him to do it. In this moment, Villero spoke to the King: "The King should not be sad or troubled. For mourning and weeping do not give back what is lost; rather, it brings diminution to the soul and injury to the body, and furthermore makes sad all those who love the King. And it makes those who envy the King joyful; and whoever hears of this will not consider the King wise, seeing that he has done a great thing without well considering what might come of it. And it brings the King into contempt and to be judged of great folly, so that he is esteemed all the less because of it. Therefore, I must advise the King that he should not trouble himself over a matter that is irrecoverable. For the wise say: 'The most notable wisdom is to forget that which cannot be brought back.' And if it pleases the King, I will tell him a parable of this." The King answered, "I desire to hear a parable of it." Villero spoke: "It is said there were two pigeons, a male and a female, and they had filled their nest with grains of wheat. And the male spoke to the female: 'I desire that we do not eat of this gathered wheat until the winter, when we can no longer find any in the field or in the barns.' The female spoke: 'I...'"

Now it was the custom of the King that when he slept one night with the Queen, he would sleep the next with the servant girl. And one night, when he intended to sleep with the Queen, she had prepared a meal of rice for him. She took a golden bowl in which the food was placed and went to the King wearing her crown. The concubine <note>original: "kepß weib" - a secondary wife or mistress of lower status</note> saw this and was moved to envy against the Queen; she put on her purple robe and entered the hall where the Queen and King were. The hall was illuminated by the purple robe and its magnificence. And when the King saw this, he was moved with love for her and said to Belebar <note>The name of the Queen in this version of the story</note>: "Truly, you have acted foolishly by choosing the crown and leaving behind the precious robe, the likes of which we do not otherwise possess." When the Queen saw that the King praised and loved the servant above her, and scorned her—forgetting her faithful counsel which had preserved him in his kingdom—she became enraged at the King. She took the bowl of food and poured it over the King's head, so that it ran down his face and beard onto his clothes. With this, the final dream had come true: that the white bird had bitten into his head.

### ¶ How the King commanded the wise man Billero to behead the Queen, and how he allowed him to believe it had been done, yet let her live.
