# The Wisdom of the Ancients. LXXXVI.

before the face of the King, and spoke to himself: "I will not kill her until the King’s anger has softened. For a wise, pious woman is an honor to the King and to the realm. Moreover, there is none like her among all those whom the King loves, and he cannot be happy for a single hour without her. For through her wisdom, many people have now been delivered from misery, as has the King himself. We all hope for mercy from the King through her, and I know that the King would certainly hate me if I hurried with her death. I know that the King will yet honor me for this above all other wise men when I keep her hidden and do not kill her. And the King will yet become wiser through this matter, so that he will not hurry in any of his affairs without temperate consideration. But if he should no longer think of her or long for her, then I may well fulfill his command." And so Villero led the Queen into his house and arranged for noble persons to wait upon her in a chamber, and to provide for her and show her honor as his Queen, until he discovered what was in the King’s mind. Thereupon Villero took his sword and smeared it with the blood of a lamb, and went sadly before the King and spoke: "I have fulfilled your command and killed Nelebat the Queen."

### ¶ How the King came into repentance that he had ordered his Queen to be killed, and how Villero told him a parable.

<note>The woodcut initial 'A' marks the beginning of the King's change of heart.</note>But a short time thereafter, the King thought of the beauty of the Queen, and her prudence and the manner she possessed came into his mind; how she had lived wisely and so companionably with him, and had only recently warned him of great harm and shame. And a severe repentance for her death fell upon him, yet he was ashamed to ask Villero whether he had killed her or not; and yet he hoped that Villero’s wisdom had not permitted him to do it. In that moment, Villero spoke to the King: "The King should not be sad or troubled. For mourning and weeping do not give back what is lost; rather, it brings diminution to the mind and damage to the body, and moreover makes all those sad who love the King. And it makes those happy who envy the King; and whoever hears of this will not take the King for a wise man, seeing that he has done a great thing and did not well consider beforehand what might come of it. And it brings the King into contempt and to the mockery of great folly, so that he is esteemed all the less because of it. Therefore, I must advise the King that he should not trouble himself over a matter that is irrecoverable. For the wise men say: The most notable wisdom is to forget that which cannot be brought back. And if it pleases the King, I will tell him a parable of this." The King answered, "I desire to hear a parable of it." Villero spoke: "It is said there were two pigeons, a male and a female, and they had carried their nest full of wheat grains. And the male spoke to the female: 'I desire that we do not eat of this gathered wheat until the winter, when we can no longer find any in the field or in the barns.' The female spoke: 'I...'"

I have understood you and will keep your command. Now, the grains that they had carried in had become damp from the rain and had swollen up because of it. And when the sun began to shine hotly, it dried the wheat so that it appeared to be very little. After many days, the man <note>In this fable, the "man" and "wife" are birds, likely pigeons or sparrows, as indicated by the mention of the beak later.</note> came back to the nest and saw the wheat; as he thought it had been diminished, he spoke to his wife: "You know that I commanded you to take nothing from the wheat until the winter, when we find nothing in the fields or the mountains." The wife answered: "I have not tasted of it, but through the sun and the dry winds, they have shrunk so that there appears to be little of it." The man would not believe this and bit her through the head with his beak until she died. And when the winter came, along with the rains and the damp winds, the wheat became damp again and swelled up so that the nest was full once more. Then he recognized that his wife had spoken the truth and that he had killed her in her innocence. And he began to mourn so painfully that he would neither eat nor drink until he died. Therefore, Lord King, it is fitting for a wise man that he should not be hasty in his affairs when he has not considered future outcomes. Therefore, Lord, do not seek that which is not to be found; rather, keep what you still have, so that you do not lose everything and that it does not happen to you as it did to the Monkey with the Lentils. # The Wisdom of the Ancients. LXXXVII.
<note>original: "Der Alten weisen." This is a German version of the "Kalilah and Dimna" fables.</note>

There was once a man who carried a sack full of lentils to market. And when he came into the forest, he set the lentils down away from him and lay down to sleep. An ape saw this from a high tree; he climbed down and took a handful of the lentils. But as he wished to climb back up the tree, a single grain of lentil fell out of his hand. The ape was not satisfied with the other lentils in his hand and climbed back down to the earth to fetch that single grain; and as he had to grasp one branch after another, he lost all the others in addition to the one that had first slipped from his hand.

And Lord King, you have over a hundred wives, and with none of them do you wish to be happy, yet you long for the one whom you cannot find. And when the King heard this, he believed that Billero had killed the Queen, and he spoke to Billero through an accusation: "That for which I commanded the Queen to be killed, you have done, and you did not delay a moment to consider what you were doing." Billero answered: <margin>The word of the King may not be revoked.</margin> "Let the word of the King and the word of that which may not be revoked be the same." The King said, "Who is that?" <margin>Two of whom neither is great.</margin> Billero answered: "There are two, neither of whom is great and who have little joy: he who says there is neither merit nor punishment after this world, and he who has shown no mercy here." The King spoke, "If I could see Helebat, there would be nothing in this world for which I would mourn." <margin>One should not mourn for two things.</margin> Billero answered: "There are two things for which one should not mourn: he who shows mercy every day, and he who has never sinned." The King spoke, "It is not possible that I shall see Helebat again." <margin>Two things that should not be seen.</margin> Billero answered: "There are two who should not see: a blind man and a robber. For just as the blind man does not see with his eyes, so the deaf man <note>The text likely means 'spiritually deaf' or 'foolish' here</note> cannot recognize good from evil. For it is written: the fool walks in darkness." The King spoke, "If I saw Helebat, my joy would be great." Then Billero answered and said, "There are two things that see: he who has eyes, and he who is rational." Then the King spoke, "If I saw the face of Helebat, I would never be satisfied." Billero answered and said: <margin>Two things that are never satisfied.</margin> "There are two things that are never satisfied: he who sets his mind only on gathering wealth, and he who craves a food that cannot be his." The King answered, "Should I not follow you in all things?" <margin>Two things that one should never follow.</margin> Billero answered: "There are two things that one should not follow: he who says there is no future judgment over all men, and he whose ears are set to listen to that which is not for him to hear." Then the King spoke, "I have become helpless for Helebat's sake." Billero answered: "There are three things called helpless: a house by a water, a land without a Lord, and a woman without a man." The King spoke, "You rebuke me well today." Then Billero answered: "There are three things to be rebuked: he who is unfaithful in his heart, he who knows the laws and does not keep them, and he who does good to one who does not recognize it." The King spoke, "You have..."

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the King and his advisor Billero regarding the fate of Helebat. The previous page likely established the conflict surrounding Helebat's judgment.</meta>

# The Book of Wisdom

You have neglected Helebat, in that you have not brought her justice to light. Billero answered: There are two who neglect justice without guilt: One who puts on a silk garment and goes barefoot, and one who takes a virgin in marriage, and afterwards puts her away from him, and after a long time takes her back again.

<margin>Three things are worthy of hard punishment.</margin>
The King spoke: You are worthy of hard punishment because of your deed. Billero answered: There are three things that are worthy of hard punishment: He who does harm to one who has not angered him; he who sits at a stranger's table uninvited; and he who asks his neighbor for that which he knows the neighbor does not have, and will not cease from his asking. The King spoke: You should be silent until my anger has passed.

<margin>Three things [that] are silent until one's anger passes.</margin>
Billero answered: Three things are silent until one's anger passes: The snake in the hand of its charmer; he who wishes to catch fish at night; and he who contemplates high <note>metaphysical or divine</note> things. The King spoke: I see Helebat.

<margin>Three things desire what they do not find.</margin>
Billero answered: There are three things that desire what they do not find: The wicked man who desires to be companioned and esteemed with the good; the murderer who desires to be in the state of a hermit; and he who looks upon God's face and yet sins grievously daily, and yet desires grace from God and relies upon it. The King spoke: I am esteemed very poorly in your eyes.

<margin>Three who despise their Lords.</margin>
Billero answered: There are three things that despise their Lords: The servant who speaks angrily against his lord without cause and is not silent before him; the servant who is richer than his Lord; and the servant who is fed too well by his Lord. The King spoke: I perceive that you mock me.

<margin>Four things that one should mock.</margin>
Billero answered: There are four things that one should mock: He who boasts of having been in many battles and having slain many people and having done much good in the field, and yet has no wound upon him; and he who gives himself out to be a spiritual man <note>a monk or cleric</note> with much fasting and prayer and chastisement of his body, and yet is fresh, ruddy-colored, strong, and fat; and a virgin who mocks a woman who has a lawful husband or may take one; and he who laments past things which are impossible to bring back, and desires that which is not, which has nevertheless happened, and to be that which is impossible. The King spoke: You have not acted rightly in that you have killed the Queen.

<margin>Three things that do not truly fulfill their cause.</margin>
Billero answered: There are three things that do not truly fulfill their cause: He who lies and is believed; he who is a glutton and therefore does no work; and he who cannot tame his anger so that it gains the upper hand over him. The King spoke: If you had truly acted unjustly, you would not have killed the Queen.

<margin>Four things that act truly and rightly.</margin>
Billero answered: There are four things that act truly and rightly: The servant who prepares a good meal for his Lord, for which he himself has a great craving, and yet prepares it for his Lord and saves it for him; and a man who lets himself be satisfied with one wife; and a King who does his business with his faithful and wise [advisors]; and he who can powerfully subdue his anger. The King spoke: I still fear you.

<margin>Four things are not to be feared.</margin>
Billero answered: There are four things that are not to be feared: There is a small bird that puts a foot over its head at night so that the heavens shall not fall upon it; and a magpie <note>original: "azel"</note> stands upon one foot and fears the earth shall fall down under her; and a toad that fears she will have a lack of earth <note>to eat or live in</note>.

The King spoke: "It is no longer fitting to remain." Billero answered: "There are eight things that do not belong together: night and day, the righteous and the unrighteous, good and evil, life and death." The King spoke: "Now my enmity against you has grown in my heart because you have killed Helebat." The King spoke: "You have entirely destroyed your wisdom with the death of Helebat." Billero answered: "There are four things that destroy wisdom: he who ruins the good works he was accustomed to with baseness; a heart that offers more honor to its servant than is necessary; a father who does not love his obedient child more than his disobedient one; and he who tells his secrets to a traitor and a chatterbox." Then Billero answered and spoke: "There are two things that bring anguish upon themselves: he who lifts his heels high from the earth and walks on his toes, for he is never safe from falling; and a fool <note>original: "blöder" - here meaning someone weak-minded or foolish</note> who boasts that he fears no enemy or strife, yet when he is forced to fight, he is shamed by his flight." The King spoke: "I have made a vow that I will put you to death." Then Billero answered and spoke: "There are four things a man should believe in and never let go: a good horse, upon which the lord has ridden well and safely; an ox that pulls well; a wise woman who loves her husband; and a faithful servant who performs his service truly and fears his lord." The King spoke: "I find no equal to them." Billero answered and spoke: "There are four things that have no equal: a woman who has known many men and afterwards is satisfied with one; he who recognizes all lies and has committed them, yet afterwards always speaks the truth; he who has counsel for all matters of his body and was never ill-advised; and he who was a tyrant <note>original: "wütterich" - a bloodthirsty or cruel ruler</note> all his days and afterwards became a hermit." Then the King spoke: "You considered neither justice nor sin when you killed Helebat." Then Billero answered and spoke: "There are four who consider neither justice nor sin: the sick man who is overburdened with illness; the servant who does not fear his lord; he who seeks to take revenge on his enemy who has caused him harm against honor and right; and the base man who respects no good deed." Then the King spoke: "Now you have poured pain into my heart." Billero answered and spoke: "There are three things that cause themselves pain: first, he who starts a war when he could well have avoided it; he who has no child or kin and yet craves wealth, gaining it through usury <term>usury: the practice of lending money at unreasonably high interest rates</term> and forestalling <note>original: "fürkauff" - an illegal trade practice of buying up goods before they reach the market to inflate prices</note>; and an old man who takes a young daughter as a wife." The King spoke: "Henceforth we shall have no more faith in you." Billero answered: "There are four in whom one should place no faith: the adder snake, the wolf, the unfaithful lord, and the man who lies on his deathbed." ### ¶ A parable told: when adversity befalls someone, they should consider in patience whether they have also deserved it.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration divided into two panels by a thin vertical line. There was a lioness who had given birth to two young cubs in a cave, and she had gone out to bring them food. In the meantime, a hunter went about his <term>venery</term> <note>original: "weydwerck" - the art or practice of hunting</note> and found the cubs in their cave; he killed them, stripped the skins from their bodies, carried them away, and left the flayed lions lying there before the opening in the rocks. And when the lioness returned to her cave and saw her cubs dead and flayed, she was terrified by great sorrow and let out many a great cry. A fox, who lived not far from her dwelling, heard this and went to the mourning lioness and asked her what she lacked. She told him and showed him her loss. He said: "You should not torment yourself so, nor lay such pain upon your heart for this matter, nor punish yourself so. Rather, you should judge yourself with a righteous judgment and consider that the hunter has inflicted this adversity upon you for no other reason than that you should remember what adversity you have inflicted upon many others. For now you may consider what suffering those had who [received] harm

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the King and the Sage, following the previous chapter's moral lessons. The King now asks for a story about those who abandon their natural habits for things they cannot master.</meta>

# The Book of Wisdom

King Diskles <note>original: "Diskles der Künig"</note> spoke to his Sage: "Master, I have understood your fable. Now tell me of one who leaves his inborn habit and takes up that which he cannot do, until he forgets his first habit, or loses that which God has provided for him, and seeks that which he cannot obtain." The Master answered:

It is said that in a certain land there was a Hermit <note>original: "Einsidel"</note>, to whom a pilgrim <note>original: "waller"</note> once came. And the Hermit gave him lodging and set before him very good dates, which they both ate. Then the pilgrim spoke: "O, how sweet this fruit is! I wish that these were also in our land, although we have many other good fruits there, such as figs and grapes." The Hermit answered: "It is not unreasonable that he who desires what he cannot have suffers regret when it cannot be his. Therefore, as a wise man, you should be content with what is granted to you by God and disdain that which cannot be yours." And as the Hermit spoke in Hebrew <note>original: "Hebraisch"</note>, the language pleased the pilgrim so much that he desired to learn it, and he begged the Hermit to instruct him in the language; he stayed with the Hermit a long time to become accustomed to it. The Hermit said: "It is likely that what happened to the Magpie <note>original: "Atzelen"</note> with the dove will happen to you, if you wish to renounce your own language and learn another." The pilgrim asked: "How was that?" The Hermit answered him:

It is said that the Magpie had a very proud gait, more so than all other birds. And she saw a dove walking; that same gait pleased her better, and she joined the dove and begged to be taught it. She forced herself with hard labor and long habit to learn it. In doing so, she withdrew from her first inborn gait. And when she left the dove, she could not retain that gait; and when she returned to the Magpies, she was confused by the dove's gait and wanted to take up her inborn gait again, but she had truly forgotten how to do it correctly. And so she went about hobbling <note>original: "gnappen"</note> with her body and feathers, and was thereby mocked by all other birds. I tell you this parable <note>original: "gleichniß"</note> so that it is right that you never forget my language. For he is rightly called a fool who seeks that which does not suit him and which his ancestors have not practiced.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the King and his sage, Sendebar, following the previous discussions on governance and morality. The catchword from the previous page likely led into this inquiry regarding the treatment of loyal advisors.</meta>

XCII.

# The Ancient Sages
<note>original: "Der Alten weisen" - This refers to the "Book of the Sages," a collection of fables often associated with the Bidpai tradition.</note>

The King spoke to Sendebar, his wise Master: "I have listened to you. Now tell me of a King and his courtiers—how one might be accused, and if the King becomes his enemy but afterwards discovers his innocence, how he should conduct himself toward that man." The King’s master, Sendebar, answered: "When a King has a faithful counselor who is slandered <note>original: "versagt," here meaning denounced or spoken against</note> to him, and his innocence later becomes known, if the King does not then set aside his displeasure, it is to the King's own detriment. However, the King should consider the man's service: whether his deeds are beyond reproach, and whether he is not daily begging for favors or overindulging in gifts. And whether he loves the King by nature or for the sake of God. Then the King should not easily believe evil against him, nor for a small cause bring him to ruin without a great misdeed; and even if a misdeed occurred, he should let it pass if the man is otherwise just, sensible, and wise. For the King's fortune and the expansion of his realm depend upon his wise and faithful counselors. For wisdom is not virtuous without loyalty, nor is loyalty and devotion of any use without wisdom. Since the affairs of Kings are great and many, and truly faithful counselors are few, the King should choose and cherish those above the others, and distance himself from those of evil habits and works, and learn to distinguish them from one another. He should not leave the righteous unrewarded for their works, nor the wicked unpunished. For if the King does not do this, the righteous man will become weary in his works, and the wicked man will be strengthened in his purpose. There is a parable of this concerning a Fox and a Lion." The King spoke, "How was that?" The master answered:

In a land in India, in a certain province, there was a Fox who was sensible, wise, a faithful counselor, just, and learned. And though he was among other animals, he did not partake in their nature or deeds. No kind of injustice was seen in him, so much so that he shed no blood and ate no meat, such that his companions often quarreled with him, asking why he did not live according to his nature and the species into which he was born. To them he gave answer and spoke: "No sin shall cling to me from your company; for as long as I can keep my spirit from sin, your company does not hinder me. For sin is not received from a place or from company, but from the will of the heart or the fulfillment of the deed. Just as those who are in holy places do not all perform good works, so too those who dwell in decadent places do not all perform evil works. For if all works were good simply because they happened in holy places, and all were evil because they were done in decadent places, then whoever struck a man to death in a church would have done well, and whoever struck a man to death in a battle would have done evil. Thus, I am also accustomed to being with you in body, but not in my spirit or in the imitation of your deeds." And he remained thus in his way of life for a long time.

# The Book of Wisdom

¶ How envy and hatred are employed in many ways at the courts of princes and elsewhere, for the sake of high status, etc.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration divided into two scenes. On the left, a fox is trapped inside a stone tower or prison cell, peering through a window. And when the fox was thus locked in the dungeon, one of the King's servants—who had previously joined the plot against him—spoke before the King and others: "I marvel at the King's reason and wisdom, that the fox's wickedness and malice have remained hidden from him for so long." The other spoke: "That is not to be wondered at. I do not see, despite all that has been told to the King and in part become known—for which reason the rogue was put in prison—that the King cares to examine him with <term>painful questioning</term> <note>A contemporary euphemism for interrogation under torture</note>. For he may yet find many similar things in him." Thereupon the King was moved, and sent two to him to speak with him. And first, they were to ask him verbally how he might excuse himself of these things. They came back and entirely distorted the fox's words of excuse which he had given to the King, so that his case appeared worse than before. For they said the fox claimed the King had given him absolutely nothing to keep. At this, the King was moved to anger and ordered him to be led out and executed, as he found this to be the advice of the bystanders who had plotted against him. ¶ Now, among the other animals of the court household, there was a polecat <note>original: "yltiß"</note> who was not guilty in this matter, and who had perceived the treachery being used against the fox. He hurried to the King's mother, the Old Lioness, and spoke to her: "Lady Queen, my Lord your son, the King, is enraged at his <term>Viceroy</term> <note>original: "vitzthumb"; a high-ranking administrative official</note> the fox, and has ordered him to be put to death, and they are leading him away. And I fear that the King is not truly informed, and that these things against the

XCV.
->The Wisdom of the Ancients<-

the Fox was done out of treachery and envy. Therefore, Gracious Lady, be wary of this, and let the matter be investigated deliberately, and let the King hear the Fox in person; thus you shall find the Fox’s innocence. And when the Lioness heard this, she perceived that they had used a plot in this matter, as it had proceeded with such haste. She sent the polecat to those who were leading him out, ordering them to stand still and do no evil or deathly harm to the Fox until she herself could go to the King, her son. And she went to the Lion and spoke:

> Son, for what misdeed have you commanded the Fox to be killed?

The Lion told her the business. The mother spoke:

<margin>Haste brings repentance.</margin>
> Son, you have hurried too fast to kill the Fox for such a matter. Or do you not know that he who completes his business in haste is followed by repentance afterward? When a man handles his affairs gently and deliberately, no reproach can follow him.

<margin>Gentleness suits no one better than a King toward his servants.</margin>
Now there is no one in the world whom long forethought, proper discernment, and a gentle undertaking of his affairs suits better than a King toward his servants. For as a wife finds pride in her husband, a son in his father, a teacher in his master, and a people in their leader, so it is with the servant toward his King.

<margin>Hope of the King should rest in God alone.</margin>
But the King’s hope should rest in God alone, in whose fear he should remain in all justice. And the head of government should be the discernment of his servants, knowing how to place each in his rightful station. And he should not take heed of every word, but rather strengthen peace among them. For it is the nature of the King’s court that they envy one another for their status, each seeking to oppress and cast out the other. Therefore, you should not believe all things; it does not become you—after you received the Fox into special favor, placed him above all others, and entrusted the entire government to him, finding him righteous in all his ways and conduct—to now rage so grimly against him over such a matter based on ignorant counsel. You have not yet discovered the true facts, nor heard him personally regarding it. If the Princes of your realm, who have seen the Fox raised by you to such great honors, should see that you are enraged against the Fox over a piece of meat <note>The "piece of meat" refers to the specific stolen item the Fox was framed for taking.</note> and moved to kill him for it, you would rightly be held as base and frivolous in their minds. For it may be that slanderers of malice have brought you to this, who are his enemies because of the honor you bestowed upon him. A King, when he appoints servants to high office and sets them above others, and they are accused before him, should investigate with great diligence, so that his hasty judgment does not later bring him heartache. And he should sometimes [hesitate to] believe even what he has seen with his own eyes or heard with his own ears. For it sometimes brings much evil; many a lie lies hidden, which can be rightly known neither by hearing nor seeing, nor by its appearance, but only through deliberate testing. Just like wine, which sometimes appears beautiful in the glass and sounds well [when poured], yet when one tastes it, it has no flavor at all. Or like a man who has a hair in his eye and imagines it to be a whole lock of hair. Or the little worm that shines at night <note>A glow-worm.</note>, which the fool takes for a fire; but the sensible man lays it in his hand and finds no heat therein.

# The Book of Wisdom
<sig>ij</sig>

Therefore, my son, you should consider the nature of the fox in your quiet mind, and then you may well say: "How should he have done that, since he eats no meat?" And I have appointed him over all my court and treasure, over drink and food, and have otherwise never found him unjust. How could his heart have deceived him into stealing from me that which I gave him with my own hand to keep, so that he should carry it like a thief into his house and finally deny that you gave it to him? Therefore, son, inform yourself better. For you should know that from the beginning of the world, the wicked have always hated the good, and fools the wise, and peasants are inclined to drive out the nobility. Moreover, I have heard from you yourself that from the first day of his appointment, you never found him otherwise than faithful, just, and without self-interest. And I know, if you would make a thorough investigation of these things, you would find that this was a plotted counsel against the fox by those who envy him. And that this meat was hidden in his house secretly without his knowledge. For when the hawk carries a piece of food between its claws, the crow flies after it to take it. So do those who desire the fox’s office; for this did not happen for the benefit of your court, but through self-interest and envy. Therefore, consider also what is useful to you, for it becomes a wise man to guard himself against two things: that he does not alienate himself from the pious and wise, and that he does not deceive his companions. And two things he should maintain: to flee from the false, the hard-hearted, and the cruel fools. Now the fox is the closest of your courtiers to you, and is subject and faithful to you, and has never taken advantage of you, and has suffered much gossip and adversity for the sake of your benefit, and never concealed anything from you. Therefore, you may well place your trust in him and not be quick to believe anything against him.

And as the Lioness spoke with her son, the Weasel <note>The Weasel is one of the conspirators who framed the Fox.</note> heard it—he being one of those who had conspired against the fox—and he became terrified by this speech. Thus he feared that the King, upon his mother’s words, would conduct an investigation and discover the truth. And so he confessed his guilt to the King and begged for mercy, telling the King of the conspiracy and how they had made the plots against the fox from beginning to end. And when the Lioness heard of the fox’s innocence, she spoke to her son: "Since the innocence of the fox is now revealed to you, and how the matter was handled by those who laid such great sin upon you—and who wanted the fox to be killed by you despite his innocence—you should rightly banish them all from your land and thereby make your innocence known. Through this, you will be magnified, and you will prevent such lies from being practiced in your court or falsely brought to you. Also, henceforth no such company of false alliances may be gathered in your court. For I tell you, anxiety, secrecy, and conspiracies are to be prevented to the best of one's ability. For look: when one binds herbs and grass together, one makes a rope from them with which one may bind a camel. And you should now restore the fox to his place in his office. And do not think that he will make you pay for what you have shown him in this matter; he knows how to put everything in its place. And just as one finds that enemies may become friends, so too can relatives and friends become enemies. The ungrateful, the concealer, the cruel..."

# XCVI.
## The Wisdom of the Ancients

->The despiser of good works. He who does not believe in a future life where good and evil shall be repaid. He who cannot restrain his mind from anger. He who is self-willed and cannot forgive after being angry. He who is inclined toward taking advantage. He who deals in deceit. He who cannot withdraw his mind from unchastity or gambling. He who lets himself be overcome by wine. He who thinks well of no one. He who is stubborn and lacks shame. In such as these, one cannot rely on steady, faithful company. And after a friendship has been cast off, no fruitful mind is to be expected from them. Therefore, every person—and especially the King—should dwell among the good and pious, who are to be trusted. So that after a past enmity, they may be trusted in all friendship. Such a one performs good works and does not break his vows; he loves to do right and hates wickedness; he is peaceable and avoids a <term>lying tongue</term><note>original: "irzung" - likely a variation of "Irrzunge," referring to deceptive or wandering speech</note>; he is gentle and not of an envious heart. He gladly forgives his fellow man for things owed, persists in friendship, and is ashamed of all misdeeds. One of these very types was your faithful fox. The King answered: "I have tested the fox many times and found in him good conduct and faithful, wise counsel, and everything that befits an upright person. Therefore, I may follow your advice to take him back into my confidence and restore him to his station." Afterward, the lion summoned the fox, and when he came before him, the King confessed to him the guilt he had committed against him. And he told him how the matter had been brought against him, and desired that he receive his office anew.

¶ A beautiful example to note: whether a reconciled friend, or one who has been angered, has entirely forgotten the matter and whether he is to be trusted.

# The Book of Wisdom

The fox answered the Lion and spoke: "Lord, you may believe that you should not fear that I will let you pay for the untruth done to me. And yet, it is not well-fitting for a King who has been angered to be trusted, especially those who are of a light faith <note>meaning: easily swayed or gullible</note>; and it is also permitted for the King to think every day: 'the fox has not forgotten his hatred toward me, and hides it in his heart until he may repay such.' And Lord King, it befits you to take better heed of my works, whether I should act toward others, or otherwise than before. But the King may perceive all that in me, and I not in him; for he may dismiss his servant whenever he wishes. Thus I must serve the King as long as he wishes, although my fear toward him has more cause than his toward me. For if a servant angers his King more, yet the wise have spoken: 'You shall place no trust in the King.' Now the King wishes to banish from his mother's land those who have so incited him against my innocence. If that happens, the King takes their kin and those who are related to him, who also will not cease, but rather sound in his ears how they might bring me into his anger. Therefore, if the King wishes, I can endure that he does not drive them away. For the King may then recognize them rightly through me; if they have virtue in their minds and fear of the King, they will be ashamed of their deeds and hold me for the King's chosen; but if they speak evil of me to the King, then the King knows that he should believe only upon certain experience, and then let their worth go entirely because of it." The Lion answered: "Now I recognize your righteous heart, and I appoint you as my highest official; for one should forgive a pious, righteous man a hundred misdeeds because of his piety, even if he had committed them. And I know that my misdeed committed against you will be forgotten, and our love will thereby be strengthened toward one another." And so the fox was placed in his office, and honored higher than before by the King, who also grew to love him more, and trusted him very well until his end.

# The Wisdom of the Ancients

<margin>No creature is nobler than man.</margin>
And tell me a parable regarding this: The master <note>The sage Sendebar</note> answered, "Know, King, that the natures of creatures are not alike, but are distinct. And among all creatures that are created—whether they be four-footed, two-footed, or winged—none is nobler than man. And yet one finds among them the righteous and the unrighteous; and one sometimes finds among the irrational animals those who have more recognition of the kindness shown to them than man himself. But the King is obligated to show mercy in his time, especially to those who recognize it. And he should first recognize the properties and character of the man: whether he is one who keeps faith and recognizes grace." <margin>Virtue is higher than nobility of birth.</margin> "And he should not regard nobility and birth above virtue <note>original: 'erbarkeyt', implying honor, integrity, or moral worth</note>. Yet he should not despise the poor when showing grace." <margin>Grace should be shown to the poor.</margin> "Also, a man who is not of the King's court, yet is wise, pious, and of good character—and if this be known—should not be despised, but rather taken to court and honored according to his merit." <margin>A King should thoroughly investigate and know his servants.</margin> "And yet the King should thoroughly know those he wishes to have stationed near him, and investigate them: of what lineage they are, what the character of their fathers was, and where they were raised. He must not build his trust on appearance alone, just as a good physician <note>original: 'arzet'</note> is not satisfied to recognize the patient's ailment by his face alone, but rather he examines his urine, feels his pulse, and pays heed to his heat and cold, and the movement of his limbs." <margin>Parable of the physician.</margin> "A righteous and merciful man should also show mercy to lowly creatures in his time. Thus it is also sometimes useful for the wise to restrain themselves and not to trust everyone. And concerning this, the wise men write a parable." The King spoke, "How was that?" Sendebar answered:

¶ It often happens that an unremarkable person is more grateful than one who is considered great; as with the animals and the goldsmith who were helped out of the well.

# The Book of Wisdom

->[Image: A woodcut showing four men outside a fortified city. One carries a bundle of sticks; two are dressed as nobles; a younger man stands by a stone wall. A sprawling city with high towers rises in the background.]<-

It is said that four companions met together upon the road. One was a king’s son. The second was a merchant. The third was a nobleman’s son. The fourth was a wanderer <note>original: "wanderer" - here implying a common laborer or migrant worker</note>. All four of them came upon the path and joined together because of the poverty they all suffered; for they possessed nothing more than the clothes on their backs. One day, these four walked toward a great royal city, and when they began to feel hunger, they asked one another how they might obtain food. And as they made their plans, the king’s son spoke: "We may say what we wish, but things go only the way that is ordained by God; and whoever relies upon Him with faithful hope shall not be forsaken." The merchant answered: "Prudence joined with reason is above all things." The nobleman spoke: "A capable and well-formed youth is above them all." The wanderer spoke: "I believe that diligence <note>original: "sorgsamkeyt" - implies careful, industrious effort or 'taking pains'</note> with practice is the best, according to my understanding." With this disputation, they approached the city and sat together, considering how they might win their food. 

And then the three spoke to the wanderer: "You speak of your diligence; go now and take such care that we may have our food this night." "I shall try this," he replied, "provided that when it comes to one of you, he shall do the same." And he went into the city and asked what a man might do in one day so that he might feed himself and four others for a day. They told him of nothing better than carrying wood, for wood was expensive there, as the forest where one had to gather wood was far from the city. He went quickly into the forest and bound a burden of wood that he was able to carry, and brought it into the city. He sold it immediately for two silver pennies <note>original: "silberin pfennig"</note>, and with them he bought food for himself and his companions. And he wrote upon the city gate with chalk: "A diligent, pious man has, through the exercise of his industriousness, earned two silver pennies in one day." And with that, he went to his companions and brought them food.

### ¶ Concerning well-formed youth, and what it may achieve.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicts four men in a rural landscape. On the left, three men are seated beneath a tree, engaged in conversation; one man gestures as he speaks. Early on the second day, they spoke to the Nobleman: "Now see to it that you provide us with food today, and take your handsome appearance and whatever skills you know to your aid." He went forth, and as he neared the city, he thought to himself: "You do not know how to labor or do anything else by which you might acquire anything. And yet it is shameful for you to return to your companions having accomplished nothing." And so, with such thoughts, he stood before the city by a tree, intending to part from his companions in sorrow. By chance, a wealthy citizen-woman, a widow, passed by. When she saw him with such a handsome and well-formed youth <note>The text emphasizes his physical beauty and noble bearing as a "resource" in itself</note>, she judged him to be capable and was inflamed with love for him. So that he would come into her house, she sent her maid after him and prepared a costly feast for him; she kept him with her the whole day and enjoyed his company according to her pleasure. In the evening, she gave him a hundred gold pennies <note>original: "guldin pfennig," likely referring to gold florins or ducats</note>. With these, he bought food for himself and his companions. And he wrote upon the gate: "With a capable youth, one has earned a hundred gold pennies in a single day." And he returned joyfully to his companions.

# CI.

was satisfied with even the smallest amount of nourishment. Then one among the people stood up and spoke: "Now we hear for the first time that you are truly worthy of this kingdom, since God has bestowed so much wisdom and reason upon you. And we are provided for with you as a wise King for our own good; God is more faithful to you than to others, for He has not brought you to this company without purpose. Otherwise, you would not have been capable of receiving this kingdom. He has fulfilled our benefit through you. Therefore, we rightly offer Him praise and thanks that He has so honored us with you." And being there together, they began to speak: "We are bound to praise God, who has set you as King over us, for this has happened solely through His divine ordinance."

Then the King spoke: "I tell you, in the time of my youth, I was with a nobleman, and when I became a man, it seemed right to me to leave the world with its pleasures. And when I departed, there remained of my wages two pennies. I thought to give one for God’s sake <note>original: "vmb Gotts willen"; a common phrase for charitable almsgiving</note> and to keep the other for my own necessity. I saw a fowler in the market carrying two turtle-doves that he had caught. I thought to myself: 'There is no greater merit before God than when one may ransom someone from death.' I bargained for the two doves, and when he would not give me both for one penny, I thought: 'If you leave one without the other, they will be separated from each other.' So I gave the two pennies for the two doves, carried them to a wide field, and let them fly. They flew away and sat upon the branch of a tree. As I departed from them, I heard one say to the other: 'Now this man has rescued us from death and has purchased our lives with all the wealth he possessed. We are rightly bound to repay him with good.'

And they called back to me and spoke: 'You have shown us great mercy, which we are bound to repay. Now, a great treasure is laid by the root of this tree; dig there, and you shall find it.' I went to the tree, dug, and found a treasure. Then I thanked God and prayed that He would protect them from all evil, and I spoke to them: 'Since your reason and wisdom are so great, and you fly between heaven and earth, how did you fall into the snare from which I purchased you?' They gave me an answer: 'You wise man, do you not know that the flight of birds, the running of the swift, nor the strength of warriors avails nothing? Only the time of divine ordinance closes the eyes of the living, against which no creature can protect itself, against that which is ordained for it by divine providence.'"

Sendebar <note>A legendary Indian philosopher, often associated with the "Seven Wise Masters" tradition.</note> answered: There was once a lake into which many delightful waters flowed. Many fish were therein, and this lake was far from people, so that the birds of the sea had no dealings there. At one time, a bird named Holgott <note>original: "Holgott"</note> came there. And when he saw the abundance of the lake and the delight of the water in secrecy and far from people, he spoke to himself: "Here you shall lead your wife and household for an eternal dwelling of their lives, for here we may find enough delightful food for every necessity. And this dwelling shall be an inheritance for me and my children, and no one here is adverse to me." Now he had a wife who sat at that time in her nest over her eggs, which were soon to hatch. She had another bird who was friendly and companionable to her, who was named Mosam. He was very dear to her, so much so that she tasted neither food nor drink, nor had any pastime, without that bird. And when she heard the intention of her husband, it was very adverse to her, and she did not wish to hide it from her friend, although her husband had strictly forbidden it. And she thought of a sin <note>original: "sünd"</note>, how she might have cause to go to Mosam the bird, so that she might make plans with him regarding these things behind her husband's back; and she spoke to Holgott her husband: "Now our young are almost ready to hatch. Now I have been told of a useful medicine <term>artzney: medicine or remedy</term> for our young when they are to hatch, so that their feathers will grow firm and strong; also it shall otherwise be fortunate for them all their lives against all evil accidents. If this were pleasing to you, I would wish to seek it." The husband spoke: "What is that?" She answered: "It is a fish in a lake on an island that has been shown to me, and no one else knows this but I and he who taught and showed it to me; and therefore my advice is that you sit meanwhile over the eggs in my place to hatch them until I may come back from the lake. Then I will bring you a fish or two, which we shall take with us to that same place where we wish to go." The husband spoke: "It does not befit a sensible person to try everything the doctor says. For they sometimes speak of things that one cannot have, and which are impossible or very dangerous to obtain. For I have often heard that their books speak of precious medicines <note>original: "Medicamenten"</note> that are to be made from the fat <note>original: "vnschlitt"</note> of a lion and from the venom of an adder. But no sensible person is advised to seek the lion in the wilderness and the adder in its hole for that reason, and thereby give himself into the worry of death. Therefore, leave off from your intention and take this nest with our young with you to carry to where we wish to go; there you may find all kinds of fish, and no one knows them but us. And I know that whoever turns to the doctor's teaching and lets himself be directed to seek medicine in dangerous places, it happens to him as it did to the Ape." The wife spoke: "How was it with him?" The husband answered:

This one answered, "I know no particular cause for it, other than that it is the will of God, which no one may flee." The other monkey answered, "I once knew one who had the same sickness. And he could not be helped, except with the head of a black adder <note>original: "natern"</note>. And when he ate that, he recovered. Therefore, if you could have that, it would be good." This monkey answered, "How could I obtain that, since I cannot even obtain my nourishment due to weakness?" The other gave him an answer and said, "Two days ago, I saw a man standing in a cave in a high rock, waiting for a black adder for its tongue, which he wanted to tear out as a medicinal treat <note>original: "kredentz" - here referring to a specialized medicinal preparation or a 'tasting' for health</note> for his heart. I will direct you there, and if he has killed it, then take the head for yourself." The sick monkey answered, "I am ill; if you can show me some kindness, I shall be in your debt to earn it back." He led him along and showed him a cave under a high rock, wherein he knew a dragon <note>original: "Drachen"</note> dwelled. And when the sick monkey crawled up there, he found large footprints before the hole. These he thought had been made by a human who had sought the adder, and with that, he went into the hole. There lay the dragon, and he snatched the monkey to be his food. When the other monkey heard this and realized that the dragon had eaten the old monkey, he settled himself in possession of the good fruit trees; and thus he had betrayed his companion and suggested a medicine for his death.

Every kind of wisdom is to be praised. But that is the greatest wisdom: he who can bury his secret <note>original: "heymligkeyt"</note> so that no one finds it." ¶ Through a lack of secrecy, a double murder often occurs, as happened to the two birds.

I have told you this parable <note>original: "gleichnůß"; a teaching story or fable</note> so that you may understand that it is not fitting for a sensible person to disdain the advice of his friend, when it is given with loyalty and faith. For it is said that the advice of a friend, though it may be heavy, is like a medicine <note>original: "artzney"</note>; although it is bitter, it nevertheless helps against sickness. Therefore, you should not disdain my advice. And as for letting your heart deceive you into not killing your husband—for you would suffer great harm from that—if you were to let him live, I will give you a better plan. When the woman heard of a better plan, she was startled to promise it, yet she desired to have a better husband, and spoke back to him: "I perceive your advice is given in faithfulness, and that it is a sign of your love and loyalty toward me. For I find my heart entirely devoted to you, and even if your advice were only for your own benefit, I would still carry out your will. But how may I bring this about? It is impossible for me." After this, these two birds remained together in good company. Thereafter, the wife remembered the word that Mosam the bird had promised her—to give her a young husband—and she begged him to look out for one for her. He promised her he would. At one time, he was walking along the shores of the sea, where he encountered a fox; the bird spoke to him: "I wish to reveal a matter to you from which you shall receive great joy. I have a bird living with me, quite fat, whom I hate, and I would gladly be rid of her; I am thinking how I might bring her to you for food. Now, I shall do this: she is desirous of a husband, and a young one at that. I will tell her that I have found a young bird of her kind here, and have told him about her, and that he wishes to take her. You hide yourself behind these rocks, and I will tell her to seek him out until you can seize her, so that the old woman's unchastity <note>original: "vnkeu̇scheyt" - referring here to her lustful desire for a younger mate after murdering her husband</note> may be punished." This pleased the fox for the sake of his own profit. So Mosam came to his companion and spoke: "I have found one of your kind by the water; and when I told him of your beauty and good conduct, and also of your intellect and the good company between us two, and the pleasantness of this place, it pleased him so that he shall take you as a lawful wife. And he has begged me to lead you to his dwelling to complete the marriage. For he wishes to delight in your beauty and wisdom. Therefore, come, and prepare yourself to go with me." This pleased the wife, and she went with joy because she was to have a young husband. The bird led her to a stone where he had appointed the fox to be, and said: "Wait here a little while; he will come by this way." And he went away from her as if he were going to meet the bird. And as the wife looked intently, setting her eyes toward the path to watch for her husband's arrival, the fox sprang upon her neck and seized her by her throat for his meal.
